### Revision History

#### 4.118.0 - (unreleased)
* **BUILD**: `java-util` 4.117.0 → **4.118.0**, its next development version: json-io depends on the java-util of its own version number. Until java-util 4.118.0 is released, build it locally first (`mvn install -DskipTests` in java-util).
* **BUG FIX**: Strict JSON mode now rejects numbers such as `1.e20`, where an exponent with multiple digits previously hid the missing fractional digit. Both the streaming tokenizer and object reader enforce this check; permissive JSON5 parsing is unchanged.
* **BUG FIX**: **Strict JSON mode refuses leading zeros and `-.5`.** RFC 8259 writes the integer part of a number as `0` alone or as a digit 1-9 followed by digits, with a digit right after the minus sign. `strictJson()` accepted `01`, `-01`, `00`, `007` and `00.5` (reading 1, -1, 0, 7 and 0.5), and accepted `-.5` while refusing `.5`. Now each is a parse error, "Leading zeros not allowed in strict JSON mode" or "Leading decimal point not allowed in strict JSON mode", through `JsonIo.createTokenizer()` and `JsonIo.toJava()` alike. Non-strict reading is unchanged: `01` is 1 and `-.5` is -0.5. Found while reviewing #439 (the entry above).
  * **Tests.** `JsonTokenizerTest`: the refused forms through the tokenizer and through `toJava()` in arrays and objects; what strict mode still accepts (`0`, `-0`, `0.5`, `0.05`, `0e5`, `10`, `-1000`, `100.001`, `[0,0 , 0]`); and non-strict reading unchanged. 15 of them fail on 4.117.0, and removing either check fails a test.
* **BUG FIX**: **Strict JSON mode refuses unescaped control characters and the `\'` escape in strings.** RFC 8259 allows U+0000 through U+001F inside a string only escaped, and its escapes are `\" \\ \/ \b \f \n \r \t \uXXXX`. `strictJson()` accepted a raw tab, line feed or any other control character in a string or a field name, and the JSON5 escape `\'`. Now each is a parse error naming the character ("Unescaped control character U+0009 not allowed in strict JSON mode", "Escaped single quote (\') not allowed in strict JSON mode"), wherever it falls: in the first 256 characters of a string or past them, before or after an escape, read from a String, an InputStream or the tokenizer. Jackson refuses both by default. Non-strict reading is unchanged (measured within 2%). Strict mode looks at each plain character of a string once more, which costs 10-17% on string-heavy JSON (2,000 objects of 12 strings, `toMaps()`, JDK 21 and 25).
  * **Tests.** `StrictJsonStringsTest` (18): all 32 control characters; a tab in each place a string is read in runs (a short string, the first run of a long one, a later run, after an escape, a later run after an escape, a field name), through `toJava(String)`, `toJava(InputStream)` and the tokenizer; escaped control characters, characters from U+0020 up (DEL, Latin-1, the euro sign, an emoji) and the other escapes kept; `\'` refused in values and keys; non-strict reading unchanged. 8 of them fail on 4.117.0, and removing any one of the five checks fails a test.
* **BUG FIX**: **`floatPointBoth()` and the TOON reader keep the number in the text** (with java-util 4.118.0). Both choose Double or BigDecimal through java-util's `MathUtilities.parseToMinimalNumericType()`, which 4.118.0 corrects: a decimal is a Double when the double reads back as the same number, and otherwise a BigDecimal holding that number. Before it, `99e307` read as Infinity, `8.000000000000001` read as the Double 8.000000000000002, and a double json-io had written with 17 digits (`0.30000000000000004`, about a third of all doubles) read back as a BigDecimal, as did doubles near the ends of the range (`1e308`, `Double.MAX_VALUE`). What the user guide says of `floatPointBoth()`, Double "except when precision would be lost", now holds. The default, `floatPointDouble()`, is untouched. The TOON reader's comment on this is corrected and moved to the method it describes.
  * **Tests.** `DecimalReadsBackTest` (4): `floatPointBoth()` and TOON on the values above, and doubles json-io writes, as JSON and as TOON, reading back as the same Double. All 4 fail with java-util 4.117.0. (TOON writes numbers without an exponent, so a double such as 1e23 is written, and read back, as an integer: unchanged.)
* **DOCS**: **The user guide and the `strictJson()` Javadoc list what strict mode refuses**, now including leading zeros, `-.5`, `1.e5`, unescaped control characters in a string and the `\'` escape (the three fixes above).
* **PERFORMANCE**: **Strict mode finds a control character in a string in the scan that finds the string's end.** The strict string rule above checked each run of a string's plain characters in a second pass, which made strict reading of string-heavy JSON 10-16% slower. It now asks java-util 4.118.0's `FastReader.readUntil(..., stopBelow)` for the closing quote, a backslash or a control character in one scan, and the cost is 0-5% (`toMaps()` of string-heavy JSON, reads/s: JDK 25 616/603 against 611/603 without the check, JDK 21 561/564 against 591/570). Non-strict reading runs the code it ran before.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** 100,000 random documents (control characters, escapes valid and not, `\'`, strings past the 256-char run buffer, in keys and values), read strict and non-strict, from a String and from an InputStream: all 400,000 results and error messages are those of the two-pass check. `StrictJsonStringsTest` adds a tab just past the reader's 65,536-char buffer, where a string's first run is copied rather than borrowed and no test reached; it passes on the two-pass code too. Removing any one of the six checks fails a test.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON with type info on keeps a whole-number Double a Double.** TOON writes a number with no exponent and no `.0` (spec section 2), so 1.0 is `1`, 1e23 and `Double.MAX_VALUE` are their digits, and -0.0 is `0`. With `showTypeInfoMinimal()`, `MinimalPlus()` or `Always()`, json-io adds `$type` where the reader cannot tell the class, but it left it off every Double, which JSON reads back as itself. So where nothing declares the type (the root, an `Object[]` or raw list element, a map value, a field declared `Object` or `Number`, a table row, a key declared `Number`, an Optional's content) a whole-number Double read back as a Long or BigInteger, and -0.0 as 0. Now such a value carries `$type`, and -0.0 is written as the text `"-0.0"` in it, so each reads back as the same Double, sign included. A whole-number Float under `MinimalPlus()`, which leaves `$type` off a Float, read back as a Long and now carries it too. A Double with a fraction is unchanged, as is anything where `double` or `Double` is declared, and TOON with type info off, which by its spec carries no types (1.0 reads back as 1).
  * **Tests.** `ToonTypedNumbersTest` (10): whole-number Doubles (1.0, 100.0, 1e23, ±`Double.MAX_VALUE`, 2^63, ±0.0) at the root, in arrays, lists, maps, fields declared `Object` and `Number`, map and object table rows, keys, and Optionals, under all three type-info modes; Floats under `MinimalPlus()`; no `$type` where a Double is declared; type info off unchanged. 8 of them fail on the code before this change; removing any one of the 13 parts of the change (the whole-number test, Float, the declared-type exemption, the -0.0 text, the Optional path, each of the seven places that now ask with the value) fails a test. TOON written from 20,000 random graphs is unchanged with type info off, and unchanged in every mode where no whole-number Double or Float appears.
* **DOCS**: **Seven doc comments sat above the wrong member**, each followed by another doc comment, where the Javadoc tool ignores the first. Five describe a method that has none, and now sit on it: `Resolver.isPseudoPrimitive()`, `ObjectResolver.seedIncrementalContainerMetadata()` and `parseWithFieldFormat()`, `CharStreamGenerator.resetForBridgeInsideObjectBody()`, and `ToonReader`'s constructor that takes a `ReferenceTracker`. Two were older descriptions above a newer one, and are gone (`ToonReader.findColonInBuf()` keeps the return contract the old one stated). Found by looking for a doc comment followed by another: none is left in `src/main`.
* **BUG FIX**: **A class with an `@IoValue` method reads back from the value it is written as.** `@IoValue` (4.96.0) writes a class as the single value its method returns, `"user@example.com"` rather than `{"address":"user@example.com"}`, and where type info is needed as `{"@type":..,"value":..}`. Only that form read back. The value alone, which json-io writes wherever the class is declared (so for every field of the class in the default mode), threw in a field ("Unsupported conversion") and in an array, threw at the root even with `asClass(EmailAddress.class)` ("Return type mismatch"), and was stored as a String in a `List<EmailAddress>` or among a map's values or keys, with no error. JSON and TOON alike. Now the value goes to the class's one-parameter `@IoCreator`, as the typed form's `"value"` always did, converted to its parameter (a Long to an `int`). A field declared `Object` whose `@IoTypeInfo` names such a class reads it too. An empty string is the value only for a creator that takes text; elsewhere it is null, as before. A class whose `@IoCreator` takes more than the one value, or that has none, and no constructor that takes it (see below), cannot be built from it: in a field or at the root the error now says so ("... is written as the single value its @IoValue method toValue() returns, and reading it back from that value takes an @IoCreator with one parameter or a constructor that takes a String: its @IoCreator has 2 and no constructor takes one"); in a list or a map the value is stored as before. A class with a one-parameter `@IoCreator` and no `@IoValue` method is written as its fields, and a bare value is still not read as it.
  * **Tests.** `IoValueReadBackTest` (8): a String-valued class (constructor) and an int-valued one (static factory) declared as a field, in a list, as map values and keys, in an array, and at the root, written as JSON in all three type-info modes and read back, and read from TOON; the empty string; the error for a creator with two parameters; `@IoTypeInfo`; a creator without `@IoValue` not used. 7 of them fail on 4.117.0 (the last one passes on it: nothing changed there). Of 13 mutants of the change, 11 fail a test. Two pass, for these reasons: without the check that the value is a string, number or boolean, a list or an object reaches the creator only to fail with a different message; and the list and map case is kept as it was on purpose (a String stored), where an error would be the alternative.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** 18,600 reads of documents aimed at 26 declared types and 13 root classes, none with an `@IoValue` method (objects, a one-parameter `@IoCreator`, enums, UUID, LocalDate, BigDecimal, lists, maps, arrays, Optional, a type variable, `@IoTypeInfo`), holding strings, numbers, booleans, objects and arrays, as JSON and TOON: every result and every error message (9,064 of them) is the one before. A mutant of the new code changes 4,481 of them, so the documents reach it. The new code runs only after the existing conversions have found nothing, so other reads run what they ran before.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `@IoValue` object held in two places is written as its value in each, and the JSON reads back.** Such an object is written as the value its method returns, in its place, where no `@id` can go. The reference pass still counted it as an object, so where one was held twice (two fields, a field and a list, a list twice) the second place held `{"@ref":1}` to an `@id` never written, and reading threw "Forward reference @ref: 1, but no object defined (@id) with that value", in every type-info mode. Now it is never referenced itself: each place holds its value, as each place holding a String or a LocalDate holds it, and reads back as an equal object (two places, two objects). A value that is itself an object (an `@IoValue` method returning an `Address`) keeps its identity as any object does: held twice, through one holder or two, it is written once with an `@id` and then as `{"@ref":n}`, cycles included, and every `@ref` names an `@id` written. Reading such an object value back into the holder's class is not supported yet, as before. A class that a custom writer writes is unchanged: the writer wins, and the object keeps its `@id`. A value method that makes a new object of its own class each time, which overflowed the stack, is refused at the depth limit ("Object graph too deep"; with cycle support on, the default).
  * **Tests.** `IoValueIdentityTest` (5): an `@IoValue` object held in two fields, a field declared `Object`, a raw list and a `List<Email>`, in three type-info modes, with no `@ref` or `@id` written and each place reading back; an object value held through one holder twice, and held by its holder and a field, written once; a cycle through a holder; a custom writer still winning, and the class written by value once its custom writer is turned off; the never-ending value method; `WriteOptions` other than json-io's own. All 6 fail on the code before this change. Of 10 mutants of the change, 9 fail a test; the one that passes gives a holder's value its holder's depth, not one deeper, when the holder is met again, which matters only at the depth limit.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** 20,000 random graphs of objects, lists, maps, arrays and dates with shared objects and cycles, written in five modes (type info minimal, always, never and minimal-plus, and `cycleSupport(false)`): all 100,000 results are byte for byte those before (15,560 of them hold an `@ref`, and 3,689 are cycles refused under `cycleSupport(false)`), and again with `@IoValue` objects in them that are never shared (22,620 of the writes hold one).
  * **Speed.** The reference pass learns that a class is written by its `@IoValue` method from the class's write plans, which it fetches anyway, and marks such an object once; nothing else is looked up for other objects. Writing 2,000 orders (8,000 lines, 200 shared customers) with cycle support on, writes/s, old and new alternately: JDK 25 342.0, 340.8, 337.2 against 343.4, 353.0, 342.2; JDK 17 351.9, 343.7, 351.4 against 349.8, 341.6, 351.2. A first version that looked at every object's class cost 3%.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON writes an `@IoValue` object as its value, as JSON does.** TOON used the method only for the root, which then did not read back, and everywhere else wrote the object's fields: `email:` with `address: a@x.com` under it, where JSON writes `"email":"a@x.com"`. Now TOON writes the value in the object's place (`email: a@x.com`, `emails[2]: c@x.com,d@x.com`, a map's values and keys, an array's elements, a row's cells, an `Optional`'s content), and with type info on, where nothing declares the class, `$type` and `value`, the form JSON writes as `{"@type":..,"value":..}`. Each reads back (the reader fix above). As in JSON, such an object gets no `$id` of its own, and a value that is itself an object keeps its identity (the entry above). A custom writer still decides: its `toData()` when it has one, else the object's fields, as before. **TOON written before reads as it did**: the fields form still reads through the `@IoCreator`. **json-io 4.117.0 and earlier cannot read the new form** of such a class: their reader has the bug fixed above. The reader also builds the class inside an `Optional` from the value alone (`Optional<EmailAddress>`), which TOON now writes where the Optional is declared and JSON written by hand may hold.
  * **Tests.** `ToonIoValueTest` (11): the value form in a field, a list, a map's values, an array, an Optional and table rows, in three type-info modes; each place, map keys and keyed tables reading back in each mode; `$type` and `value` in an `Object[]`, and the root; an object held twice written as its value, with no `$id` or `$ref`; an object value written once and referenced, through one holder twice and through a cycle; a value method that never ends, and two objects written as each other, refused at the depth limit (`IoValueIdentityTest` adds the second case for JSON); the fields form written before; a custom writer's data, a custom writer without data, and none; the reference pass following what is written (an object's fields when its writer gives no data, an Optional's content); `WriteOptions` other than json-io's own, untyped and typed; an Optional from the value alone, as a field and at the root, in JSON. 7 of them fail on the code before this change; the other four (each place reading back, an object value's identity, the fields form written before, what the reference pass follows) passed before and must still. `AnnotationTest.testIoValueToonWrite` now asks for exactly the value: it passed because the fields hold the address. Of 16 mutants of the change, 15 fail a test. The one that passes no longer stops following a chain of such objects at a class TOON writes as a list or map, which only an `@IoValue` method on a List or Map class would show. Removing the depth limit was not run: the two tests that need it would never end.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** TOON from 20,000 random graphs of objects, lists, maps, arrays and dates with shared objects and cycles, in five modes: identical where no `@IoValue` object appears (100,000 writes). With them, the 18,616 writes that changed are exactly writes holding one, none has a `$ref` to an undefined `$id`, and none is left in the fields form; the 4,004 that did not change hold one only at the root, which TOON already wrote as its value. The 20,000 graphs of the TOON entry above, in four modes, are identical (80,000 writes), as are the 18,600 reads of the reader's corpus.
  * **Speed.** The reference pass asks the class's cached write plans one flag (a custom data writer or an `@IoValue` method) and handles either out of line, so its two hot methods are smaller than before (`traceReferences` 191 to 159 bytes of bytecode, `pushReferenceCandidates` 368 to 284). A first version that asked inline grew them, and on JDK 25 the JIT inlined less into them. TOON writes of 2,000 orders with cycle support on, writes/s, 8 runs each, old and new alternately: JDK 25 mean 350.5 against 356.5, JDK 17 366.3 against 357.5; the medians are within 1.5% (JDK 25 353 and 354, JDK 17 359 and 355.5). Runs of this benchmark land near 350 or, now and then, near 380, for either version (old twice in 16 runs, new once).
* **BUG FIX**: **An object with a `"value"` key in a typed field reads as what it is.** Since 4.103.0, a JSON or TOON object read into a field whose type the Converter can build from a Map was taken for json-io's wrapped form `{"@type":"byte[]","value":"..."}` whenever it held a key named `value` or `_v`, and handed to the Converter. Three things went wrong:
  * **A Map field** (`Map<String,Object>`, `TreeMap`, ...) whose map had such a key came back a `LinkedHashMap` whatever its class, and a map under that key stayed json-io's internal `JsonObject` whose values were never read: a `Date` in it stayed a `JsonObject`, a `List` a `JsonObjectArray`. 4.102.0 read it correctly.
  * **An exception field** whose class has a field named `value` came back with only its message: that field and the others were 0 or null, as JSON and as TOON in every type-info mode. 4.102.0 read it correctly.
  * **An enum field in TOON written with `showTypeInfoAlways()`**, which TOON writes as `$type` and `value: B`, threw "No enum constant ...value" (4.117.0 too).
  * **Now** the step that did this, in `ObjectResolver`, is removed. The wrapped form needs nothing from it: it reads through `ArrayFactory` (4.113.0's fix for the root and `Object` fields), and every other class through its usual path.
  * **Tests.** `ValueKeyReadTest` (8): a map under `value` and under `_v` in a `Map` field, its `Date` and `List`; a `TreeMap` and a `HashMap` keeping their class; a field declared `TreeMap`; TOON; maps inside a `List` field and a `Map` field; one map held by a `Map` field and an `Object` field staying one object; enum fields (one with constant bodies), a list and an array of them, from TOON in all four type-info modes and from `{"value":"B"}`; an exception with a `value` field as JSON and TOON. 7 fail on the previous code; putting the step back, with or without Maps excluded, fails 2 or 7.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** For each of the 61 classes the Converter builds from a Map (`byte[]`, `ByteBuffer`, `UUID`, `BitSet`, the `java.time` and `java.sql` classes, `Currency`, `Locale`, ...), and for `GregorianCalendar`, two enums and two exceptions, a field of that class read twelve ways (json-io's own output as JSON in two type-info modes and as TOON in three, the form it writes in an `Object` field, `{"value":..}` and `{"_v":..}` with and without `@type`, and `@id`/`@ref` sharing in both orders): every result is the same with and without the step, and every shared value is one object, except the enums and the exception with a `value` field, which now read as above. The full suite passes without it. Over 6,407 random graphs written by 4.117.0 and by this version in 15 JSON and TOON modes (676,560 reads), the 1,936 reads that changed are 304 holding a map with a `value` key and 1,632 of an enum field in TOON with type info always. 1,574 of them now equal what was written; the rest differ from it only as the same values do outside such objects (an `@IoValue` object whose value is an object, which a later fix reads; `NaN` written as `null`; a `BigDecimal` JSON writes without its exponent; numbers TOON writes without their type).
* **BUG FIX**: **An `@IoValue` class without an `@IoCreator` reads back through the constructor that takes its value.** Such a class, `Tag` with `@IoValue String name()` and `public Tag(String name)`, did not read back from the value json-io writes for it: in a field, an array or at the root the read threw "... takes an @IoCreator with one parameter: it has no @IoCreator", and in a list or a map the value was stored as a String (only `{"@type":..,"value":..}`, in a field declared `Object`, read back). And since TOON now writes such a class as its value (above), a class whose TOON read back from its fields through 4.117.0 stopped reading back. Now, with no one-parameter `@IoCreator`, json-io uses the constructor whose one parameter has the type the `@IoValue` method returns, or its boxed or primitive form (an exact match first), as Jackson uses a single-argument constructor. It may be private. A one-parameter `@IoCreator` still comes first, and a two-parameter one still reads the fields form. The fields TOON wrote through 4.117.0 still read back. The error for a class nothing builds from its value now names both ways.
  * **Tests.** `IoValueImplicitConstructorTest` (8): a String, a private int, an `Integer` taking an `int` value, and a class with a two-parameter `@IoCreator`, in a field, an `Object` field, a list, a map's values and keys, an array, an `Optional` and at the root, as JSON in three type-info modes and TOON in four; the fields form; the empty string; a one-parameter `@IoCreator` winning over the constructor; `int` over `Integer`; the error for a class whose only other constructor takes two. 6 fail on the previous code, and each of 7 mutants of the change fails one.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Only a class with an `@IoValue` method and no one-parameter `@IoCreator` can read differently. Over 6,407 random graphs written by 4.117.0 and by this version in 15 JSON and TOON modes (676,560 reads), the 12,227 reads that changed all hold such a class, and none throws now (11,146 did). Each such object reads back as written wherever its class is known (declared, or named by type info); the only differences left in them are lone surrogates, which the test's UTF-8 files cannot hold.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `@IoCreator`'s arguments are read as the types its parameters declare.** A creator taking an object, `Person(String name, Address address)`, could not read back even json-io's own output: the argument arrived as the parsed map, and the read threw "@IoCreator invocation failed" (4.117.0 too). Only values the Converter makes (strings, numbers, dates, ...) reached a creator; a `Map<String, Address>` argument arrived as json-io's internal `JsonObject` with its values unread. Now each argument is read as a field of its parameter's type is: an object, a list, map or array of them, an object of the type written in an `Object` parameter, or another class built by its own creator, from JSON and from TOON. A reference among the arguments is the object it names, read first if it comes later in the document, so one object given to a creator twice, or also held by a field, stays one object. An argument that refers back to the object being created is an error that says so, since a creator cannot be given an object before it exists.
  * **Tests.** `IoCreatorObjectArgumentsTest` (6): an object, a `List<String>`, a `Map<String, Address>`, an `int[]` and an `Address[]` as JSON in three type-info modes, as TOON in three, and hand-written; a creator taking classes built by creators, one and in a list; an `Object` parameter and a type-variable one; one object given to a creator twice (a plain one and one built by a creator), and held by fields before and after it, and named by references before it is defined; the error for an argument that is the object being created. 4 fail on the previous code; 5 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** For a creator taking each of the 61 classes the Converter builds from a Map, `GregorianCalendar`, two enums and two exceptions, each read twelve ways as JSON and TOON (as for the `"value"` key fix above): the same results as before, but for `byte[]` (4 forms) and enums (5 forms), which threw and now read. Over 6,407 random graphs written by 4.117.0 and by this version in 15 JSON and TOON modes (676,560 reads), the 16,981 reads that changed all hold an `@IoValue` class whose creator takes an object (`Site(Address)`), and all of them threw before.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `@IoValue` class whose value is an object, a list or a map reads back.** `Site` with `@IoValue Address address()` and `@IoCreator Site(Address address)` is written as the Address (`"site":{"street":"1 Main","zip":1}`, or `{"@type":"Site","value":{...}}` where its type must be named), and it read back as `Site(null)` in a field, a list, a map or an array, and threw at the root (4.117.0 too, in JSON; in TOON since TOON writes the value, above). A value that was a list (`["a","b"]`) came back an empty list or threw. Now the creator takes the value whole. A JSON object holding the creator parameter's key is read as the class's fields, as TOON wrote such a class through 4.117.0, so that still reads; any other object, or an array, is the value. Jackson's `@JsonCreator(mode = DELEGATING)` or `mode = PROPERTIES` decides when given. A `"value"` key alone, with a type, is json-io's typed form; beside other keys it is the value's own field.
  * **Identity.** The value keeps its identity, as the writer gives it (an `@IoValue` object has none of its own). An `@id` written on the value names the value, so a `@ref` to it in a field declared `Address` reads back as the Address, not the Site; before this, it read back as the Site. A `@ref` in a place declared `Site` (a field, a list, an array, a map's values) names the value too, and reads back as a Site built around it, whether the value comes before or after it in the document, or was built first for a creator.
  * **Tests.** `IoValueObjectValueTest` (10): an object value in a field, an `Object` field, a list, a map and an array, a list value and a map value, as JSON in three type-info modes and TOON in four; the root; the fields form; a null value; a value with a field named `value`; one Address held by a field and by Sites in a field, a list, an array and a map, in both orders, and named by references before it is defined, as JSON and TOON; a value built first for a creator, defined later in a field, a list and an array; Jackson's DELEGATING with a key named as the parameter, and PROPERTIES without it. 8 fail on the previous code. 14 mutants of the change are each caught; one more, which builds again a referenced object already built, changes nothing and stays for the work it saves. Three wraps no reading reached, and a condition that changed nothing, were removed; a creator taking `Object` now takes a map value whole.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Only an `@IoValue` class whose creator takes an object can read differently. Over 6,407 random graphs written by 4.117.0 and by this version in 15 JSON and TOON modes (676,560 reads), the 14,332 reads that changed all hold `Site(Address)`; 74 of them threw and now read, and in each mode with type info every Site and Address reads back as written, but a Site holding null, which is written as null. The probes of the `"value"`-key and creator fixes above give the same results byte for byte.
  * **Speed.** The new cases sit out of the reading loops, in helpers they call only for a `@ref` or a finished value, so the hot methods are the size they were (`traverseCollection` 919 to 921 bytes of bytecode, `traverseArray` 664 to 666, `assignField` 1044 to 1059). A first version that checked inline, and looked up each collection's element class, grew them by 70 to 84 bytes, and reading slowed by up to 3% (JDK 17 TOON, JDK 25 JSON). Reading 2,000 orders (8,000 lines, 200 shared customers, as JSON and as TOON), reads/s, old and new alternately, median of four rounds: JDK 25 JSON 202.2 against 203.4, TOON 187.9 against 187.9; JDK 17 JSON 180.7 against 184.7, TOON 199.4 against 196.4 (both 193 to 200 from round to round).
* **BUG FIX**: **An enum with an `@IoValue` method, or Jackson's `@JsonValue`, is written and read as that value.** json-io ignored the method on an enum: `GREEN("g")` with `@JsonValue String code()` was written as `"GREEN"`, and Jackson's `"g"` did not read ("No enum constant ...g"); under `writeEnumAsJsonObject()` json-io wrote the value and then could not read it back. Now the value is written wherever the enum appears -- a field, an `Object` field (`{"@type":..,"value":"g"}`), a list, an array, an `EnumSet`'s items, an `EnumMap`'s and any map's keys, a map's values, an `Optional`, the root -- as JSON and as TOON, as Jackson 2.22 writes it, and a number value (`@IoValue int code()`) as a number. A constant with its own body is written by its enum's method. It reads back from the value and then from the name, so JSON and TOON written through 4.117.0 read as before; a value that is also another constant's name is the value, and of two constants with one value, the first. A custom writer registered for the enum still comes first; `writeEnumAsJsonObject()` does not, as for any `@IoValue` class.
  * **The output changes for such an enum**, which is the point: what reads json-io's output sees `"g"` where it saw `"GREEN"`, and json-io 4.117.0 and earlier cannot read it ("No enum constant"): upgrade readers first. A root enum is written with its type (`{"@type":..,"value":"g"}`), as a root `@IoValue` class is, so it reads back as the enum even when no class is asked for (the bare name came back a String); in Maps mode (`toMaps()`) such a root reads as a JsonObject typed as the enum and holding the value (`{"value":"g"}`), as a root `@IoValue` class does, where the bare name read as a String.
  * **Tests.** `IoValueEnumTest` (9): a String-valued `@JsonValue` enum, an `@IoValue` enum whose constants have bodies, an `int`-valued one and an enum without the method, in every place above; JSON written in four modes and TOON in four, written as values and read back; the names json-io wrote through 4.117.0, as JSON and TOON; Jackson's own output for the class; the root; a value that is another constant's name, and two constants with one value; a custom writer, as JSON and TOON, and through WriteOptions other than json-io's own. 7 fail on the previous code; 17 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Over 6,407 random graphs, the JSON and TOON that 4.117.0 and the previous commit wrote (names) read exactly as before (676,560 reads), but for the 6,288 reads of what `writeEnumAsJsonObject()` wrote as values, which threw and now read. Written by this version and read back, every read that changed holds a `@JsonValue` or `@IoValue` enum; where the enum's class is known it reads back as written; in an untyped place it is now the text `"g"` rather than `"GREEN"`.
  * **Speed.** The lookup by value sits in one helper the read paths call where they called the Converter, so their methods are no larger (`assignField` 1059 to 1056 bytes of bytecode, `readWithFactoryIfExists` 268 to 265, `convertToType` 619 to 616); a first version, with the lookup at each site, grew them by 15 to 23. Looking up the enum's class on every value cost an enum-heavy TOON read 2-4% on JDK 25, so a read remembers the last four enum classes it met without the method, and for those the look is four comparisons. Reading 2,000 orders each with an enum field, a list of three enums and four lines with an enum, reads/s, old and new alternately, median of four rounds: JDK 25 TOON 322.2 against 321.4, JSON 251.4 against 250.5; JDK 17 TOON 340.6 against 340.5; and the benchmark of the fix above (no enums) on par.
* **BUG FIX**: **A BigDecimal in TOON keeps its scale with type info on, and one read from a TOON number is the number its text is.** TOON writes a number with no exponent and no trailing zeros, so `1.50` was written `1.5` and read back `1.5`, even under `showTypeInfoMinimal()` and the others, which promise what JSON gives back; and a BigDecimal read from a number went through the Double the reader makes of it, so `0.0000000000000000000000000001` read back as `1.0E-28`, its text's number with another scale (since this version's reading of decimals keeps more of them Doubles, for about 0.9% of BigDecimals that 4.117.0 read exactly). Now, with type info on, a BigDecimal is written as its text, as JSON writes it (`dec: "1.50"`; plain digits, so an exponent's scale is not kept, also as JSON); with type info off it stays a TOON number. And a TOON number read into a BigDecimal -- a field, a list, a map's values, an array, an `Optional`, the root, `$type: BigDecimal` -- is the number its text is. JSON is unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `ToonBigDecimalTest` (6): eight BigDecimals (`1.50`, `0.000`, `-2.50`, `123.4560`, 28 decimal places, `-0.0000000000000003`, `100`, `7`) in every place above, in three type-info modes, written as text and read back equal, scale and all; `1E+3` written as JSON writes it; without type info, numbers, and read as their text; what 4.117.0 wrote with type info on (numbers) read as their text, at the root too; an array holding a typed element; a stream; JSON's `1.0` and `1.0e-5` read as before. 5 fail on the previous code; 10 mutants, each caught. `ToonMinimalTypeTest` expects the text form. `Resolver.convertValue(value, type)` is public, for a `ClassFactory` that converts a value as json-io does (an enum by its `@IoValue`, a TOON number into a BigDecimal); `ArrayFactory` uses it.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** 200,000 random BigDecimals written by 4.117.0 as TOON with type info (numbers), read into a field and an `Object` field: of the 154,140 that 4.117.0 read exactly, 154,017 still are, and the other 123 lost a trailing zero when 4.117.0 wrote them (`0.00030` as `0.0003`), which its reading put back by chance; 1,264 that 4.117.0 read with another scale now read exactly, and the 74 it read as a different number are right. Over the 6,407 random graphs, the reads that changed are TOON reads of a BigDecimal, each now the number its text is or the value written. Reading is on par (the benchmark of the `@IoValue` object fix above, JDK 25 and 17, JSON and TOON).
* **BUG FIX**: **In Maps mode, asking for `Object` at the root reads whatever the JSON holds, as asking for no class does.** `JsonIo.toMaps(json, options).asClass(Object.class)` -- and `asType(new TypeHolder<Object>(){})`, and the same on `fromToonToMaps()` -- threw "the rootType 'java.lang.Object' is not supported" for every object, list and array at the root, typed or not; only a bare value read. Jackson's `readValue(json, Object.class)` reads them all. Now it returns what `asClass(null)` returns. The same request made inside a read failed the same way: a TOON list at the root whose elements name their type (`- $type: ...`) threw in Maps mode even with `asClass(null)`; it reads, each element as its JsonObject (a UUID as the UUID).
  * **Tests.** `MapsModeObjectRootTest` (4): `asClass(Object.class)` against `asClass(null)` for eleven roots (an object, a map, a list, an array, a UUID, an `@IoValue` class, an `@IoValue` enum and a plain one, text, a number, an empty map) written as JSON in three modes and as TOON in two; the TOON list of typed elements asked for as nothing, as `Object` and as `List`; a plain and a typed object asked for as `Object`, and through a `TypeHolder<Object>`. All 4 fail on the previous code; 3 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Over the 6,407 graphs of the cross-version check, in the 92,799 Maps-mode reads of what 4.117.0 wrote and the 92,799 of what this version writes, `asClass(Object.class)` now reads exactly what `asClass(null)` reads, every one; before, it threw for 74,035 and 73,103 of them that `asClass(null)` read. `asClass(null)` reads as before but for one, the TOON list above.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `@IoValue` class that nothing builds from its value is an error in a list, a map and an `Optional` too, not the bare value stored in its place.** A class written as the value its `@IoValue` (or `@JsonValue`) method returns reads back through a one-parameter `@IoCreator` or a constructor taking that value; with neither, a field and the root said so ("... takes an @IoCreator with one parameter or a constructor that takes a String: it has neither"), but a `List<Mail>`, a `Set`, a map's values and keys, an `Optional<Mail>` and a list of lists held the String itself, so the read succeeded and the first use of an element threw `ClassCastException`; an array threw "java.lang.String". Now each says what a field says, in JSON and TOON, as Jackson 2.22 throws in each place. A null element reads as before, and Maps mode, which builds no classes, is unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `IoValueNoCreatorElementsTest` (6): a String-valued class and an `int`-valued one whose only constructor takes text, in a list, a set, a map's values and keys, an array, an `Optional`, a list of lists, as JSON and TOON, at the root (`List`, array, `Optional`), and as json-io writes them; nulls, the same places for a class with a creator, and Maps mode, unchanged. 4 fail on the previous code (every one of the 16 places read without error or threw "java.lang.String"); 4 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** The 862,158 reads of the cross-version check (what 4.117.0 wrote and what this version writes, every read mode) are byte-identical before and after. The method the element paths share changes by one constant, so its bytecode size and speed are unchanged.
* **BUG FIX**: **A BigDecimal with a negative scale keeps it: `1E+3` is written `"1E+3"`, not `"1000"`.** json-io writes a BigDecimal as text that reads back as the same number with the same scale -- plain digits (`"1.50"`, `"0.0000001"`), and past the plain-digit limit the exponent form -- but for a negative scale the plain digits are another scale: `1E+3`, which is what `new BigDecimal("1000").stripTrailingZeros()` returns, was written `"1000"` and read back with scale 0, so not `equals()` to what was written, and in a field declared `Number` it came back a `Long`. Now a negative scale is written in its exponent form (`"1E+3"`, `"1.2E+4"`), as Jackson 2.22 writes it, in JSON wherever a BigDecimal is written (a field, an `Object` field, a list, a map's values, `@keys` and stringified keys, an array, an `Optional`, the root) and in TOON with type info; every other BigDecimal is written as before. TOON without type info writes numbers, which have no scale (`1000`), as before.
  * **The output changes for such a value:** what reads json-io's JSON sees `"1E+3"` where it saw `"1000"`. json-io 4.117.0 reads it back as the BigDecimal `1E+3` everywhere, as does `new BigDecimal(text)`, and JavaScript's `Number("1E+3")` is 1000; a reader that expects only digits does not. An amount that went through `stripTrailingZeros()` has a negative scale when it is a whole number ending in zeros (`1000.00` becomes `1E+3`), so it is written `"1E+3"`.
  * **Tests.** `BigDecimalScaleTest` (5): eight negative scales (`1E+3`, `1.2E+5`, `-5E+2`, `1E+30`, `12E+3`, `1.5E+2`, `1E+1`, `1E+999999999`) in every place above, as JSON in five modes and TOON in three, each read back `equals()` to what was written; six other scales written as before; stringified keys; TOON numbers; and the `"1000"` json-io wrote before reading as before. `ToonBigDecimalTest` and `HugeExponentWriteTest` now expect the exponent form (`"1E+3"`, `"9.99E+14"`). 4 fail on the previous code; 5 mutants, each caught (and `scale <= 0` makes no difference: a zero scale's exponent form is its plain digits).
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Of the 96,105 texts the cross-version check writes, the 3,476 that changed each hold a negative-scale BigDecimal, and each change is exactly its plain digits becoming its exponent form; the negative-scale texts that did not change are TOON without type info. Reading them, by this version (431,079 reads) and by 4.117.0 (431,079): no read that worked now throws, or the reverse; 9,506 and 5,494 reads now keep the scale, and 206 and 123 read a `Number` or `Object` value as the BigDecimal written where they read a `Long` or `BigInteger`. What 4.117.0 wrote reads as before. Not benchmarked: one comparison per BigDecimal written, one `instanceof` per stringified key.
* **BUG FIX**: **`showTypeInfoAlways()` writes `@type` on the elements of a declared collection or map too.** Always mode writes the type on every object, as its documentation says, and it did on a field and an array element, but an element of a collection or a map declared with its type (`List<Point>`, `Set<Email>`, `Map<String, Point>`'s values and `@keys`, `List<UUID>`, `List<LocalDate>`, `List<BigDecimal>`, `List<List<Point>>`) was written without it when its class was the declared one, as in minimal mode: `"points":{"@type":"ArrayList","@items":[{"x":1}]}` where a `Point[]` wrote `{"@type":"Point","x":1}`; a list of an `@IoValue` class wrote `"a@x.com"` where its field wrote `{"@type":"Email","value":"a@x.com"}`. Now each such element is written as a field or an array element of that declared type is: with its `@type`, but for a String, Long, Double or Boolean, and an element declared as a primitive wrapper (`List<Integer>`, as `Integer[]`), which are written bare. Jackson's default typing types each element. Only always mode changes; JSON written this way reads back as before, in json-io 4.117.0 too.
  * **The output changes in always mode:** the elements above gain their `@type`. Every json-io reads it.
  * **Tests.** `AlwaysTypesElementsTest` (4): every place above under always mode; native and wrapper elements bare; the graph read back `deepEquals` to what was written; minimal mode unchanged (an enum list keeps its type, as before). 1 fails on the previous code (the other three pass before and after); 3 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Of the 96,105 texts the cross-version check writes, the 986 that changed are all always-mode JSON. Reading them, by this version and by 4.117.0: no read that worked now throws, none that read back as the original no longer does, and 60 and 300 more read back as the original by value; where a read still differs from the original, the change itself is toward it (an `@IoValue` object whose value is null, written `null` in a list and read back as null, is now written with its type and read back as the object). What 4.117.0 wrote reads exactly as before (431,079 reads).
  * **Speed.** One test of a final field per element in the other modes. Writing 2,000 orders (minimal mode; each a list of four lines, each with a list of tags), writes/s, old and new alternately, median of three rounds: JDK 25 324.3 against 330.0, JDK 17 338.4 against 341.2.
* **BUG FIX**: **An enum's one-parameter `@IoCreator` (or Jackson's `@JsonCreator`) factory reads it.** json-io read an enum from its name, and since this release from the value its `@IoValue` method writes, but ignored a static factory method: `@JsonCreator static Shade from(String text)`, which accepts a code or a name in any case, read `"Dark"` in Jackson 2.22 and threw "No enum constant ...Dark" in json-io. Now the factory reads the value first, wherever an enum is read from one -- a field, an `Object` field (`{"@type":..,"value":..}`), a list, an array, a map's values and keys, an `EnumSet`'s items, an `Optional`, the root -- in JSON and TOON. A factory that throws or returns null leaves the value to the `@IoValue` lookup, then to the name, so the names json-io wrote through 4.117.0 read past a factory that takes codes only; the value is converted to the factory's parameter type (`of(int n)` reads `2`), and one that cannot be is left to the others. `@IoCreator` on an enum's constructor is ignored, as before. Writing does not change.
  * **Tests.** `EnumCreatorTest` (6): an `@IoCreator` factory reading codes and names in any case, a Jackson `@JsonCreator` one reading abbreviations (null for others), one taking an `int`, one that throws for names and one that returns null for its codes, and `@IoCreator` on a constructor, in every place above, at the root and in TOON; values and names read as before; what none reads is an error; writing unchanged. 2 fail on the previous code (the other four pass before and after); 6 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** The cross-version corpus has no enum with a factory: its 96,105 written texts are byte-identical, and its 862,158 reads too, but for 12 that throw the same `NullPointerException` either way and differ only in whether the JIT kept its message.
  * **Speed.** The factory sits in the per-enum lookup the value already used, and an enum with neither still takes the four-class plain-enum check. Reading 2,000 orders, each with an enum field, a list of three enums and four lines with an enum, reads/s, old and new alternately, median of three rounds: JDK 25 TOON 317.1 against 316.8, JSON 247.8 against 248.9; JDK 17 TOON 334.5 against 341.7.
* **BUG FIX**: **A String holding an unpaired surrogate is written with it escaped (`"\ud800"`), and reads back as it was.** A surrogate is half of a character outside the Basic Multilingual Plane (an emoji); one without its other half, as a string cut in the middle of an emoji holds, has no UTF-8 form. json-io wrote it as the raw char, which an encoder turns into `?`: written to a stream, or to a String later sent as UTF-8, `"a\ud800b"` came back `"a?b"`. Now an unpaired surrogate is written as its `\uXXXX` escape, as Jackson 2.22 writes it, in a value and a key, double- and single-quoted (JSON5), and through the `JsonGenerator`; it reads back as the same char, in strict mode too. A surrogate pair, and every other char, is written as before. TOON is unchanged: its strings have no `\u` escape, so a TOON stream still writes `?` for one.
  * **Tests.** `LoneSurrogateWriteTest` (5): a lone high (the last one, U+DBFF, too), a lone low, a low before a high, two highs, a pair cut off at the end, a pair followed by a lone low, as a value and a key, to a String and to a UTF-8 stream, read back (strict too); JSON5's single-quoted form; the generator; pairs (the highest, U+DBFF U+DFFF, too), the chars either side of the surrogates, other non-ASCII text and U+2028 unchanged. 4 fail on the previous code (the fifth passes before and after); 8 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Of the 96,105 texts the cross-version check writes, the 15,514 that changed are JSON holding an unpaired surrogate, and each change is a `?` (in the UTF-8 the check stores) become its escape (25,059 escapes); TOON is unchanged. Reading them, by this version and by 4.117.0: no read that worked now throws, none that read back as the original no longer does, and 23,197 and 13,927 more read back as the original.
  * **Speed: a cost on text with emoji.** An ASCII char is checked as before; a non-ASCII one now also against the surrogate range, and a surrogate against its neighbor. Writing 2,000 maps of ten strings and a 300-char note, writes/s, old and new alternately, median of three rounds (JDK 25; JDK 17): ASCII 517.3 against 518.2 (700.6 against 706.8); non-ASCII without emoji 285.2 against 278.1 (273.4 against 273.1); a mix of ASCII, accented, CJK, Hangul and emoji 272.3 against 258.6, 5% (275.6 against 251.3, 9%); dense emoji 384.3 against 351.6, 9% (358.2 against 344.3, 4%). The cost is the branch on a surrogate, which emoji placed at random mispredict: the forms tried to avoid it cost more (moving the loop's own index past a pair, 13%: the loop is no longer a counted loop; testing below U+2028 first, 20%; deciding without branches, 7-18%; one compare for the range, the same).
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON honors `maxObjectGraphDepth` and `maxObjectCount`, as JSON does; and a graph too deep for the stack is a `JsonIoException` that says so, in both.** The two options (and `addPermanentMaxObjectGraphDepth` / `addPermanentMaxObjectCount`, defaults 100,000 levels and 1,000,000,000 objects) bounded only JSON: TOON checked neither, so a TOON write of a hostile or runaway graph ran until the stack or the heap gave out. Now TOON checks them where JSON does -- in the reference trace, with `cycleSupport` on -- with the same messages. Without `cycleSupport`, which is TOON's default (`toToon(obj, null)`), there is no trace; the depth is now checked as each object is written, in JSON as well, which checked nothing there (the count guards what a trace keeps per object, so it is not). And JSON and TOON write a nested object by recursion, so a graph a few thousand levels deep -- a long linked list -- overflowed the thread's stack long before 100,000 levels: that `StackOverflowError` is now a `JsonIoException` ("Object graph too deep to write: the thread's stack overflowed ...", the error as its cause). The writer is usable afterward.
  * **Javadoc.** `WriteOptions.getMaxObjectGraphDepth()` / `getMaxObjectCount()` and their builder methods said the defaults were 10,000 and 100,000; they are 100,000 and 1,000,000,000.
  * **Tests.** `ToonWriteLimitsTest` (5): the depth limit falls at the same level for JSON and TOON, with the same message; the count, as JSON; the permanent defaults reach `toToon(obj, null)` and options of its own; without `cycleSupport`, both check the depth and neither the count; a 50,000-deep chain on a 256 KB stack is the new error in JSON and TOON, with and without `cycleSupport`. All 5 fail on the previous code; 10 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** The cross-version check's 96,105 written texts are byte-identical (none is near either limit).
  * **Speed.** The checks sit at a push or a path entry, with their errors built out of line: JSON's `writeOptionalReference`, which every object passes, is 87 bytes of bytecode against 115 before (its no-trace branch is now a helper), TOON's trace push 80 against 35. Writing 2,000 orders, writes/s, old and new alternately, median of three rounds: JDK 25 JSON 328.7 against 329.2 (cycleSupport on) and 464.3 against 463.7 (off), TOON 347.8 against 345.9 and 459.4 against 454.9; JDK 17 JSON 342.1 against 334.6 and, over five rounds, 489.4 against 486.4, TOON 349.6 against 344.2 and 461.9 against 452.4. JDK 17's JSON without cycleSupport is bimodal from one JVM to the next (about 485 or 515 writes/s, old and new alike); the new one took the lower mode in all of its eight runs, the old in five of eight.
* **BUG FIX**: **A creator given a map finds it filled.** json-io fills a map at the end of a read, once every reference it may hold is known; a creator is called during the read. So an `@IoCreator` (or `@JsonCreator`) given a map -- its `@IoValue` value, or one inside a list or an object among its arguments -- received it empty: one that kept the map saw it filled later, but one that read it (`new LinkedHashMap<>(m)`, `Map.copyOf(m)`, a sum over it) kept nothing (a total of 0). Now the maps read for a creator's argument are filled before the creator is called; read through to the end of that argument, their keys are complete, and the end of the read fills them again with what references resolved later give.
  * **Tests.** `IoCreatorMapArgumentsTest` (3): a creator that copies and sums its map value, a list class built from a map of counts, a creator given a list of maps and an object holding a map, as JSON and TOON, and as json-io writes them. All 3 fail on the previous code; 3 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Of the 862,158 reads of the cross-version check, one changed: a TOON document 4.117.0 wrote without type info, read into its class, whose untyped map with object keys inside a creator-built object lost an entry; it now reads as written (4.117.0 itself threw on it). Not benchmarked: per creator argument, one size read and a loop over the maps read for it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A list or map class with an `@IoValue` (or `@JsonValue`) method is written as that value and reads back, in JSON and TOON.** `Tags extends ArrayList<String>` with `@IoValue String joined()` and `@IoCreator static Tags of(String)`: JSON wrote `"x,y"` but traced the object as a list, so a second field holding it was written `{"@ref":1}` to an `@id` never written, and the read threw; TOON wrote the list itself (`a[2]: x,y`); a `Dict extends LinkedHashMap` read from its value went to the Converter ("Unsupported conversion ... 'Map'"), typed (`{"@type":..,"value":..}`) gained an entry `"value"`; and a list or map given as its items or entries went to the creator that takes text ("@IoCreator invocation failed"). Now such a class is written as its value wherever it appears (a field, an `Object` field with its type, a list, an array, a map's values, an object inside a list, the root), never referenced itself (each place holds the value, as for any `@IoValue` class), and read back through its creator, as Jackson 2.22 writes and reads it. Given its items or entries -- as TOON wrote such a class through 4.117.0, and as other JSON may -- it is built as any list or map is, so that TOON now reads (it threw); in a list of such a class, a TOON item written as a nested list still reads as an `ArrayList`, as for any list subclass (found, not touched).
  * **The output changes for such a class in TOON:** `a: "x,y"` where it wrote `a[2]: x,y`. JSON's output changes only where the object was held twice (the value, where `{"@ref":1}` was).
  * **Tests.** `IoValueContainerTest` (4): a list class and a map class written as text, a map class written as a list (`Pairs`), a list class written as a map (`Counts`) and a map class written as a list of maps (`Entries`), held twice, in every place above (and inside an object in a list, where TOON writes fields inline), as JSON in three type-info modes and TOON in three, read back; a list subclass without the method unchanged; the root; Jackson's output; 4.117.0's TOON forms (items, entries, `$id`/`$ref`); JSON items and entries. All 4 fail on the previous code; 20 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** The cross-version corpus has no such class: its 96,105 written texts are byte-identical, and its 862,158 reads too, but for 9 that throw the same `NullPointerException` either way and differ only in whether the JIT kept its message.
  * **Speed.** The traces and the TOON field writers ask whether a list or map class is written as a value; the last four classes found not to be are remembered, so the look on a list or map is four comparisons (the class's metadata lookup on every list and map cost writes 1-3%), and the TOON check sits in the list and map branches, after the scalar fast paths. Writing 2,000 orders, writes/s, old and new alternately: JDK 25 JSON 329.0 against 329.1, TOON (cycleSupport off) over five rounds 454.9 against 459.5; JDK 17 JSON 338.8 against 356.0 (bimodal run to run), TOON 465.0 against 465.8. Reading: JDK 25 JSON 201.7 against 204.5, TOON 188.0 against 189.9; enums: TOON 320.0 against 319.7, JSON 249.9 against 251.1; JDK 17 TOON enums 341.1 against 341.1.
* **BUG FIX**: **JSON5 writes a String holding DEL (U+007F) and a double quote.** JSON5 writes such a String (a `"`, no `'`) in single quotes, escaping control chars as the double-quoted form does; DEL looked up a table of the 32 control chars and threw `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException` ("Index 127 out of bounds for length 32"), so a graph holding one did not write as JSON5. DEL is now `\u007f` there too, as in double quotes.
  * **Tests.** `Json5DelEscapeTest` (2): DEL in single-quoted values, list elements and the root, read back; double quotes as before. 1 fails on the previous code; 2 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Of the cross-version check's 96,105 written texts, the 59 that changed are JSON5 writes that threw this error (in 4.117.0 too): 48 now write, and 11 now meet the cycle error they would have met next (JSON5 writes without cycleSupport). Nothing else changed.
* **IMPROVEMENT**: **TOON's `showTypeInfoAlways()` leaves a value bare where its primitive's wrapper is declared, as JSON does.** A `List<Integer>`'s elements, an `Integer[]`'s, a `Map<String, Short>`'s values and an `Integer`, `Float`, `Byte` or `Character` field were written `{$type: Integer, value: 7}`; JSON's always mode writes them bare, the declaration giving the type, and now TOON does too. A value in a place that does not declare it (an `Object` field, a raw list) keeps its type, and a -0.0 keeps its typed form (`value: "-0.0"`), since a TOON number has no -0 -- in a `Double` slot too, which always mode wrote bare, losing the sign.
  * **The output changes for TOON in always mode only** (smaller); json-io 4.117.0 reads it as before.
  * **Tests.** `ToonAlwaysWrapperTest` (5): each wrapper bare where declared; undeclared places typed; -0.0 keeping its sign in a `Float` field, a `Double` field and a `List<Double>`; the graph read back `deepEquals`; JSON the same. 2 fail on the previous code; 4 mutants, each caught.
  * **Same behavior, shown.** Of the cross-version check's 96,105 written texts, the 1,227 that changed are TOON in always mode. Read back by this version and by 4.117.0: no read became an error, Maps mode reads a scalar where it read `{"value": ...}`, and 7 reads into the class now keep a -0.0 they lost; nothing else changed.

#### 4.117.0 - 2026-10-16
* **BUG FIX**: **Options copied from options that write enums as objects wrote enums as bare strings.** `new WriteOptionsBuilder(options)` is "a copy as a starting point", and it copied the settings one by one by hand. `enumWriter` was not among them, since the copy was added in 2024 (`enumPublicFieldsOnly` was, from two weeks later). So with `writeEnumAsJsonObject(true)` on the original, the copy wrote `"loc":"CELL"` where the original wrote `"loc":{"name":"CELL"}`, with no error. **`customOptions` (what `addCustomOption()` stores) was left out too**: a `WriteOptions` copy lost every custom option of the original (a `ReadOptions` copy has always copied them). Found in n-cube, whose servlet copied its options only to keep the stream open; the editor reads `delta.loc.name`, and from n-cube 6.74.0 every difference opened in a compare dialog read `null` on both sides. It is in every json-io from the day the copy was written: 4.108.0 and 4.110.0 through 4.116.0 all write the bare name from the copy (checked), and a copy of options that never set the enum form is unaffected.
  * **Now** the copy carries both. **Does it touch an execution path?** Only through the copy constructor: `new WriteOptionsBuilder(options)` with options that wrote enums as objects, or that held a custom option, now writes as the original does. Everything else is as before, including `new WriteOptionsBuilder()` and a copy of default options. **A caller who copied such options and has come to depend on bare enum names** gets objects now; add `.writeEnumsAsString()` to the copy to keep them (a copy can change the form without touching the original).
  * **Tests.** `OptionsCopyCoversEveryFieldTest` (3) is the one that was missing. It reads the fields of `DefaultWriteOptions` and of `DefaultReadOptions` by reflection, sets each to a value that cannot be its default, copies the options, and asks whether the copy holds the value; a field a copy must not carry over (the per-instance caches, `aliasesShared`) is named in an exemption list with the reason. **A field added to the options and not to the copy fails it the day it is added.** On 4.116.0 it names exactly `enumWriter` and `customOptions` for `WriteOptions` and finds nothing for `ReadOptions`. `WriteOptionsCopyEnumTest` (3) pins the behaviour: a copy writes objects, or strings, as its original does, and a copy can change the form without changing the original. Full suite (json-io module): 5,466 tests, 0 failures, 7 skipped; the two Spring modules add 166 and 28, all passing.
* **BUILD**: `java-util` 4.116.0 → **4.117.0**, released the same day: json-io depends on the java-util of its own version number. It adds `GraphComparator.Delta.getPath()`, which json-io does not use.

#### 4.116.0 - 2026-10-15
* **BUG FIX**: **An `XMLGregorianCalendar` or a `javax.xml.datatype.Duration` is written as its XML text, and reads back, on every JDK.** They are what JAXB generates for `xs:dateTime`, `xs:date` and `xs:duration`, and their runtime classes are the JDK's internal `com.sun.org.apache.xerces.internal.jaxp.datatype.XMLGregorianCalendarImpl` and `DurationImpl`. json-io wrote them field by field under those names.
  * **4.113.0 to 4.115.0:** the write threw on every JDK -- "For security reasons, cannot load: com.sun.org.apache.xerces.internal.jaxp.datatype.XMLGregorianCalendarImpl" -- because java-util 4.113.0 blocks the JDK's internal `com.sun.` classes. An object generated by JAXB could not be written at all.
  * **Through 4.112.0, and since with `SecurityChecker.allowClass(...)`:** correct on JDK 8 and 11. On JDK 17 and later, which do not open those classes, every field was written as 0 or null without an error (`"year":0,"month":0,"day":0,...`), and reading that back threw.
  * **Now** each is written as its lexical form under its public type: `{"@type":"XMLGregorianCalendar","value":"2026-10-15T10:20:30.123-04:00"}` and `{"@type":"javax.xml.datatype.Duration","value":"P1DT2H"}`, or the text alone with no type information (`"lossDate":"2020-06-28"`). It reads back through java-util 4.116.0's Converter, which makes it with the runtime's `DatatypeFactory`. A value keeps exactly the fields it has: `"2020-06-28"` has no time and no zone, and a zone stays as written. No internal class is named, so neither `allowClass` nor `--add-opens` is needed, and the security block is unchanged. As with a `Calendar`, one instance in two places is written twice, never as `@id` and `@ref`. `javax.xml.datatype.Duration` keeps its full name, since `Duration` is `java.time.Duration`'s alias.
  * **JSON written before reads:** the internal class names are read as the public types, and the Converter reads the fields as 4.112.0 wrote them. The zeros written on JDK 17 and later are refused (month 0 is no month): they never held the value.
  * As for a `TimeZone`, an `Object` field holding one comes back as its text when nothing in the document gives its type (`showTypeInfoNever()`, `json5()`, TOON). A field, array, list or map value declared `XMLGregorianCalendar` or `Duration` comes back as itself.
  * **Unchanged:** every other document. 10,800 random-map documents under 9 sets of write options, and 98,030 writes of what `toMaps()` reads from them and from the test resources, are byte for byte what 4.115.0 wrote. Classes in the JDK's internal packages other than these two are still refused, in writing as in reading (4.113.0).
  * **Tests.** `XmlDatatypeRoundTripTest` (8); all 8 fail on 4.115.0 (7 by throwing), and 7 with java-util 4.116.0 alone (TOON then works through the Converter). A class shaped as JAXB generates one -- a date-time, a date, a duration, an `Object` field, an array, a list, a map -- under the default options, pretty printing and `json5()`, with no `com.sun` in the JSON; the exact JSON with no type information; as the root and in untyped arrays and lists; one instance in two places; JSON 4.112.0 wrote, verbatim; Maps mode writing back what it read; TOON; 500 calendars from a fixed seed under two sets of options. 12 mutants, all caught: each writer, the non-referenceable rule, the names read from before, the two `customWriters.txt` lines and the alias.
* **BUILD**: `java-util` 4.115.0 → **4.116.0**, released the same day: json-io depends on the java-util of its own version number. From it the Converter reads and writes `XMLGregorianCalendar` and `javax.xml.datatype.Duration` (the first entry).

#### 4.115.0 - 2026-10-14
* **BUG FIX**: **A `java.sql.Time` comes back with the date it had.** A `Time` is a `Date` and holds any instant (`new Time(millis)`); JDBC's time of day is the case where the date is 1970-01-01. In 4.113.0 and 4.114.0 the date was lost wherever a `Time` travelled as text. Nothing failed and nothing looked different, because `Time.toString()` prints the time of day only: the `Time` read back prints the same and differs in `getTime()`, `equals()` and `compareTo()`.
  * **Reading.** Text that names a date -- in a `Time` field, or as `{"@type":"java.sql.Time","value":"2020-06-28T01:00:00"}` -- was read as 01:00:00 on **1970-01-01**. Up to 4.112.0 it was read as the instant it names. A wire format that writes a `Time` as a date-time, with a custom writer for instance, came back fifty years off.
  * **Writing.** Under `isoDateFormat()`, and in TOON under any options, a `Time` on another day than 1970-01-01 was written as its time of day (`"01:00:00"`), without its date.
  * **Now** text with a date is read as the instant it names, the one a `Date` field gets from the same text; and a `Time` on another day is written as that instant, the way a `Date` is (`"2020-06-28T05:00:00.000Z"`). Unchanged: a `Time` on 1970-01-01 is written `"13:14:15"` (`"13:14:15.123"` with milliseconds) and read from it, and JSON's default form, the epoch milliseconds (`{"@type":"java.sql.Time","date":1593320400000}`), which never lost anything.
  * **Where the fix is.** In java-util 4.115.0's `Converter`, which json-io uses for both directions; json-io's own code is unchanged. Its changelog has the rule and the comparison with 4.112.0.
  * **Tests.** `SqlTimeKeepsItsDateTest` (5); 4 fail with java-util 4.114.0, and the fifth holds what is unchanged. A `Time` on 2020-06-28 comes back from JSON under the default, `isoDateFormat()` and `longDateFormat()` as the root, a field, an `Object` field, an array element, a list element and a map value; from TOON as the root, a field, an array element and a list element. Text with a date is read as its instant under each key a `Time` is read from, in Maps mode too, and as a bare string. 600 `Time`s from a fixed seed, a third of them times of day, come back exactly from JSON under each date format and from TOON.
* **BUG FIX**: **A getter that throws for one object is still called for the next.** With `useGetters(true)`, or for a getter named for a field (`@IoGetter`, `@JsonGetter`, `addNonStandardGetter`), one throw switched that getter off for good: every object written afterwards with the same `WriteOptions` got the field's value where the objects before it had got the getter's. Nothing said so. With a getter that adds tax to a price and refuses an item not priced yet, an item written as `"price":15` came out as `"price":5` once an unpriced item had been written in between. It was so through 4.113.0 too, when getters were used by default.
  * **Now** a getter that throws stands for the object it was called on: the field is written in its place for that object, as before, and the getter is called again for the next one. A field's own read is still switched off for good if it ever throws; it does not, for an object of its class.
  * **Cost:** none on the ordinary paths -- 2,000 objects of 10 fields, through fields and through getters, before and after in interleaved fresh JVMs: within 3% either way on JDK 8, 17 and 21. A getter that throws for EVERY object now throws once per object instead of once: 2,000 such objects took 1.3 ms to write where they took 0.22 ms.
  * **Tests.** `GetterThatThrowsTest` (4), all four fail on the previous code: the same object before and after another's throw, in JSON and TOON; a getter named with `@IoGetter`; each of the nine reads an `Accessor` makes (the eight primitives and `retrieve()`), each in both orders around the throw, with one call of the getter per read; a getter that always throws. `AccessorDifferentialTest`'s test of a throwing getter now expects one call per read. 21 mutants: 20 caught. The one left never switches a field's read off, which no test can tell apart, for the reason above.
* **BUG FIX**: **A map's `@keys` and `@items` come from one pass over its entries, so each key stays beside its own value.** A map whose keys are not strings is written as two lists, and the n-th key belongs to the n-th item. The JSON writer took the keys from `keySet()` and then the items from `values()`. A map whose two views do not run in the same order -- the `Map` contract allows it -- was written with each key beside another key's value; a map that changed between the passes had more keys than items, or the reverse, or (emptied in between) threw. Nothing said so; the document read back as a map with the wrong pairs. TOON has written its `$keys` and `$items` from one pass since 4.114.0.
  * **Now** one pass over `entrySet()` writes each key as it comes and keeps its value for the `@items` list, whatever the map does meanwhile.
  * **Unchanged for every ordinary map:** 10,800 documents (400 random maps of 9 kinds -- `HashMap`, `TreeMap`, `ConcurrentHashMap`, `JsonObject`, `CompactMap` and others -- with 8 kinds of key, alone, in a holder and nested, under 9 sets of write options) are byte for byte what the previous writer wrote, 19.7 million characters. Speed: on par (writing a 1,000-entry `HashMap<Long, String>` and 200 maps with `UUID` keys, within 3% either way on JDK 8, 17 and 21).
  * **Tests.** `MapKeysBesideTheirItemsTest` (7). Three fail on the previous code: a map whose `values()` runs in reverse, a `ConcurrentHashMap` that loses an entry while it is written, a map that says it is not empty and has nothing by then. The others hold what must not change (ordinary maps against their two views as the oracle, pretty printing, a map that grows while written, a `size()` that is out of date). 11 mutants, all caught.
* **BUG FIX**: **With `stringifyMapKeys(true)`, a map with a key that is itself a map is written as `@keys`/`@items` instead of throwing.** `stringifyMapKeys` -- on in `standardJson()` and `json5()` -- writes a map whose keys all have a text form as `{"7": ...}` and any other map as `@keys`/`@items`. A key that was a map passed the check and failed the write: "To convert from Map to 'String' the map must include: [value] or [_v] as key with associated value." `Converter` lists Map → String for every map, and reads a value only from a map that wraps one.
  * **Now** a map key that `Converter` turns into text is stringified as before -- a map that wraps one value, as a key read in Maps mode from `{"@type":"BigDecimal","value":"1.5"}` does, is written `"1.5"` -- and any other map key sends its map to `@keys`/`@items`, as with the option off. So also under a declared key type (`Map<Map, V>`), and for a map key that got into a `Map<Long, V>` through a raw reference.
  * **Unchanged for everything that did not throw:** of the 10,800 random-map documents of the previous entry, the 44 that threw (all under `stringifyMapKeys`) are now written, and read back as Java objects and as maps; the other 10,756 are byte for byte as before. A first version of this fix stringified no map key at all, and this comparison caught it: 34 documents read in Maps mode, whose `BigDecimal` keys had been written `"81598.5"`, came out as `@keys`/`@items`.
  * **Faster:** the keys are now checked a class at a time -- `Converter` is asked about a key's class once for a run of keys of that class, instead of for every key; a map key is still asked about one by one, since its text depends on what it holds. Writing a 1,000-entry `HashMap<Long, String>` under `stringifyMapKeys` takes 13% less time on JDK 17 and 7% less on JDK 21; 200 maps of 5 `UUID` keys, 1-5% less (medians of 6 interleaved fresh JVMs each).
  * **Tests.** `MapKeyThatIsAMapTest` (7); 5 fail on the previous code, all by throwing: a map key in a plain map, a `JsonObject` key and a `TreeMap` key; the map key comes back; a declared map key type and a raw reference; `standardJson()` and `json5()`; two map keys of one class of which only the first wraps a value, under no declared key type, under `Map<Map, V>` and through a raw reference into a `Map<Long, V>`. The other 2 hold what must not change: a key that wraps one value, keys with a text form, and a key of another class after a run of one class. 9 mutants, all caught.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `JsonObject` with a key that is not a String is written as a map, and json-io writes back what `toMaps()` read.** A `JsonObject` is a `Map<Object, Object>`. One that its type did not mark as a map was written as an object, its keys as field names, and the write threw at the first key that was not a String: `ClassCastException`, or "Field name must not be null" for a null key. It took no object built by hand. A map whose keys are not Strings, written with no type information (`showTypeInfoNever()`), is `{"@keys":[1,2],"@items":["a","b"]}`; read with `toMaps()`, its root is such a `JsonObject`, and writing it back threw. So did a `JsonObject` read from `{"a":1}` and given the key `5L`.
  * **Now** a `JsonObject`'s keys are field names only when every one is a String. Otherwise it is written as the map it is, the way a `LinkedHashMap` with the same entries is: `@keys`/`@items`, or `{"1": ...}` under `stringifyMapKeys`, with its `@id` when it is referenced twice. TOON already wrote such a `JsonObject` as it writes a `LinkedHashMap`.
  * **Measured:** 10,845 documents -- the test resources' JSON files that read, and the 10,756 random-map documents of the entries above -- read with `toMaps()` and written back under 9 sets of options. Of the 97,605 writes, 16,092 threw and now write; each of those, read with `toMaps()` and written again, comes out the same, and reads as Java objects too. Every other write is byte for byte as before (four still throw, rightly: a cyclic document under `cycleSupport(false)`). The 16,092 are every write of 596 of the 800 random maps written with `showTypeInfoNever()`, under its three option sets that include it; none of the test resources' files was affected.
  * **Speed: on par.** Before a `JsonObject` is written as an object, its keys are looked at once. Writing what `toMaps()` read from 2,000 objects of 10 fields, and of 2, with and without cycle support: medians within 1.5% either way on JDK 8, 17 and 21 (6 interleaved fresh JVMs each).
  * **Tests.** `JsonObjectWithOtherKeysTest` (6); 5 fail on the previous code, all by throwing: a `JsonObject` with `Long` keys under the default, no type information, `stringifyMapKeys`, `forceMapOutputAsTwoArrays` and pretty printing, read back; the typeless root of an `@keys`/`@items` document read with `toMaps()` and written back, with `Long` keys and with a list key and a map key; a key added after reading; a null key, last and first; such a `JsonObject` inside a list twice (`@id` and `@ref`) and as a field. The sixth holds what must not change: a `JsonObject` with String keys, empty, and with an `@type`. 6 mutants, all caught.
* **BUILD**: `java-util` 4.114.0 → **4.115.0**, released the same day: json-io depends on the java-util of its own version number. From it `Converter` reads and writes a `java.sql.Time` with its date (the first entry).

#### 4.114.0 - 2026-10-13
* **DOCS**: **README ratio table re-measured for 4.114.0, all three columns.** `JsonPerformanceTest --with-gson`, median of three runs, JDK 21.0.12, `jackson-databind 2.22.3`, `gson 2.14.0`. 4.113.0 was measured again on the same day, its runs alternating with 4.114.0's, so the two can be compared: between days the machine alone moves a ratio by about 3% (Gson's typed read against Jackson went from 1.42x to 1.48x with neither library changed).

  | Mode (ratio to Jackson) | JsonIo | TOON | Gson |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Read `toJava` (typed) | 1.59x | 1.54x | 1.48x |
  | Read `toMaps` | 1.28x | 1.35x | 1.47x |
  | Write `cycleSupport=true` | 1.54x | 1.48x | 2.15x |
  | Write `cycleSupport=false` | 1.39x | 1.37x | 2.15x |
  | Write `toMaps` `cycleSupport=true` | 1.52x | 1.50x | 2.10x |
  | Write `toMaps` `cycleSupport=false` | 1.36x | 1.37x | 2.10x |

  * **Against 4.113.0, measured the same day:** one cell changed. A TOON `toMaps` read takes about 6% longer (1.27x → 1.35x; 4,466 → 4,720 ms for 100,000 reads). It is the cost of the list fix in this release ("The elements of a TOON list are read wherever the list stands"): the benchmark's TOON names `$type`, so the elements of its lists are now resolved, as JSON's always were. Before, they were passed on as parsed -- which is how a `$ref` in such a list came back an empty map. Measured across 18 commits of this release, the step is at that one. A document that names no `$type`, `$id` or `$ref` does not pay it. Every other figure is within about 3% of 4.113.0's: typed reads and TOON writes the same, JSON writes about 2% faster.
  * **The benchmark's TOON text is the same in both versions**, byte for byte (15,628 characters): the faster reading of quoted cells and quoted inline arrays in this release does not show here, because this data has almost none.
  * **Derived text in the README:** Gson 1.5-2.2x Jackson (was 1.4-2.2x); typed reads about 7% longer than Gson (was 12%, of which the Gson figure's drift is most); `cycleSupport(false)` 9-10% faster.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **json-io reads and writes fields. Getters and setters are used only when you ask for them.** A getter or setter may do more than hand over a field, and a serializer cannot tell; so by default no code of your class runs, and what comes back is the state that was written. Two new options call the methods instead: **`WriteOptionsBuilder.useGetters(true)`** and **`ReadOptionsBuilder.useSetters(true)`** (and `addPermanentUseGetters(true)` / `addPermanentUseSetters(true)` for every new options object). They are independent: set both, one or neither.
  * **Reading.** json-io 4.55.0 through 4.113.0 called a class's public `setXxx(value)` in place of assigning the field, whenever the class had one. It was never announced: 4.55.0 corrected a lookup that had asked for a method returning `Void` (the wrapper class), which no setter does, so until then every field had been assigned.
