A setter that does more than assign then ran on an object that was still being read, and a value could be lost with no error: a `setActive(boolean)` that consults a registry dropped the JSON's `false`, a `setParent(p)` that also adds the child to `p.children` read a parent of two children back with four, and a setter that copies the list it is given copied it before json-io had filled it. **From 4.114.0 the field is assigned**, private and final fields included, as in every release before 4.55.0.
  * **Writing.** Through 4.113.0 the writer called a class's public `getXxx()` / `isXxx()` in place of reading the field, whenever the class had one. **From 4.114.0 the field is read.** The JSON differs only where a getter returns something other than its field: a computed value, a copy, a read-only view.
  * **Unchanged, with or without the options:** a getter or setter *named* for a field is used (`@IoGetter`, `@IoSetter`, `@JsonGetter`, `@JsonSetter`, the non-standard getter and setter settings); an `AccessorFactory` or `InjectorFactory` you added is asked for every field; a method filter still takes a getter out of use; and the JDK's own classes (`java.*`, `javax.*` and the like) are read and set through their public getters and setters on every JDK, as before: their fields are an implementation detail (a `Throwable` without a cause holds itself in `cause`), and from JDK 17 on they cannot be reached anyway. An enum written as an object still counts a private field with a public getter as public.
  * **If you upgrade** and relied on the methods being called, add `useGetters(true)` / `useSetters(true)`. Under `useSetters(true)` remember that a setter runs on an object that is still being read.
  * **Speed.** Reading is unchanged. Writing through fields costs what writing through getters costs, to within 2% on JDK 8, 17, 21 and 25. Against 4.113.0, a class whose every field has a public getter writes within 3% on JDK 17+ (1% faster to 3% slower), and a class without getters 2-12% faster: see the next entry.
  * **Tests.** `UseSettersTest` (15) and `UseGettersTest` (15): the defaults, each option, which methods count, named methods, caller-added factories, JDK classes, enums, TOON, the copy of the options and the permanent settings. All 19 mutants are caught.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **The writer reads fields 2-12% faster, through one mechanism where there were four.** `Accessor` called a getter through a `LambdaMetafactory` lambda where one could be made, read a field of primitive type through a lambda bound to a `VarHandle`, read any other field through `MethodHandle.invoke`, and fell back to `Field.get`. `LambdaMetafactory` cannot make a lambda for a field at all, so the path built to be the fastest served getters only, and the fields -- now the default -- took the slower ones. Every field and every getter is now read through one `MethodHandle`, adapted once to `(Object)Object` (and to `(Object)int` and its like for a primitive) and called with `invokeExact`; `Field.get` remains for a field no handle reaches. `Accessor.java` goes from 936 lines to 576, with no `LambdaMetafactory` or `VarHandle` code left.
  * **Measured** writing 2,000 objects of 10 fields to JSON, before and after in interleaved fresh JVMs (medians of 3 runs, of 9 on JDK 25). Through fields, the default: ten primitive fields 10-12% faster on JDK 17, 21 and 25; four primitives and six objects 4-8%; ten `String`s 2-3%; on JDK 8 12%, 12% and 4%. Through getters (`useGetters(true)`): on JDK 17, 21 and 25 from 1% faster to 3% slower than the lambdas, and 4% slower for one of the three classes on JDK 25; 4-12% faster on JDK 8, which never had the lambdas. Bytes allocated per write: the same to the byte on JDK 17, 21 and 25, and 5-13% fewer on JDK 8, where a primitive field was boxed on its way out.
  * **One difference.** A getter that throws was called twice before json-io read the field in its place (once through the lambda, once through the method handle), on the JDKs where the lambda could be made. It is called once.
  * **API.** The eight nested interfaces `Accessor.BooleanGetter` ... `Accessor.DoubleGetter` are deprecated: json-io no longer uses them. They remain so that code naming them compiles.
  * **Tests.** `AccessorDifferentialTest` (12). The oracle is plain reflection on its own `Field` and `Method` objects: for 68 fields and 64 getters of four classes (every primitive, every wrapper, `String`, array, list, enum, `final`, `transient`, `volatile`, public to private, a subclass that hides a field and overrides a getter, a class that is not public), over 300 objects each filled from a fixed seed with edge values, NaN, -0.0 and nulls, `retrieve()` and every typed getter return what `Field.get` / `Method.invoke` return. Also: getters that do not return their field, the cases with no method accessor, a JDK class's closed and public fields, a static field, a getter that throws, a null or foreign object. Ten of the twelve pass unchanged on the previous `Accessor` on JDK 8 and 21 (the other two are the difference above and a check of the new handles), which is the evidence that values did not change. One test looks inside: every ordinary field and getter has its adapted handle and no read falls back to `Field.get` -- the loss of speed that comparing values cannot see. All 21 mutants are caught.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **The reader sets fields through one adapted `MethodHandle` as well.** `Injector` set a field through `MethodHandle.invoke`, which looks up the handle's adapted form on every call; called a setter through a `LambdaMetafactory` lambda where one could be made; and kept a `VarHandle` path besides. Every field and every setter is now set through one handle, adapted once to `(Object, Object)void` (and to `(Object, long)void` and its like for a primitive number) and called with `invokeExact`; `Field.set` remains for a final field on JDK 8 through 16 and for a field that cannot be opened. `Injector.java` goes from 738 lines to 547, with no `LambdaMetafactory` or `VarHandle` code left.
  * **Measured:** the injector calls a read makes for 2,000 objects of 10 fields, without the parser, before and after in interleaved fresh JVMs (medians of 5). Fields, the default: 1-8% less time per field on JDK 17, 11-17% less on JDK 21, 8-9% less on JDK 25, 18-25% less on JDK 8. Through setters (`useSetters(true)`): the same for primitive numbers; for the other fields, where the lambda was used, between 4% less and 9% more on JDK 17, 21 and 25 (9% more for one class on JDK 17, 3-4% more on JDK 25), and 16-18% less on JDK 8.
  * **In a whole read** this is small: setting a field takes 3-4 ns of the 55-65 ns that reading one costs. Reading the same JSON end to end, before and after differ by less than one JVM start differs from the next (about 5% either way), so no end-to-end gain is claimed.
  * **BUG FIX**: a final field given to `Injector.create` without having been made accessible got, on JDK 9 and later, an injector that could set nothing. It held a `VarHandle`, and a `VarHandle` of a final field has no `set`: every injection failed with "VarHandle injection failed for field". json-io always passes fields it has already opened, so a read never met this; code that calls `Injector.create` itself did. The field is now opened and set.
  * **Tests.** `InjectorDifferentialTest` (12). What a field holds after an injection is read back with plain reflection: for 66 fields and 54 setters of four classes (every primitive, every wrapper, `String`, array, list, enum, `final`, `transient`, `volatile`, public to private, a subclass that hides a field and overrides a setter, a class that is not public), 200 rounds each from a fixed seed, a value of the field's own type is stored as it is, a value of another type as the Converter makes it (or refused with the Converter's own exception, the field untouched), and a `long` and a `double` as a Java cast narrows them. Also: setters that do not store their argument, the cases with no method injector, a setter that throws, null into a primitive, a null or foreign object, a static field. Ten of the twelve pass unchanged on the previous `Injector` on JDK 21 and eleven on JDK 8; the others are the fix above and a look inside -- every ordinary field and setter has its adapted handle -- that guards the speed. 21 of 23 mutants are caught; the two that survive open a field the other of two equivalent ways.
* **TOON spec v4.4: all 564 official cases pass** (160 for writing, 404 for reading, strict and non-strict). json-io 4.113.0 conformed to spec v3.3; against the v4.4 fixtures 148 of its cases failed. The entries below, from this one down to "TOON reads the keyed tabular form", are the way there. `known-gaps.txt`, the list of official cases json-io did not pass, is empty; `ToonSpecFixtureTest` still fails the build if a case stops passing. Key folding and path expansion, which v3.3 had and v4 dropped, remain as options that are off by default, with their own fixtures.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON: strict mode refuses a depth jump.** The first line of a nested scope stands exactly one level below the key or header that opens it (TOON spec §8, §9, §12). Strict mode read `a:` followed by `    b: 1` -- two levels down -- as if it stood one level down, and likewise a list's first item and a table's first row; it refused only the first row of a keyed table. All four are refused now, with the line that jumps. Non-strict mode reads such a scope from where it stands, as before (§14.4, recovery 5), and holds the rest of the scope to that depth.
  * **Tests.** `ToonDepthJumpTest` (5). For 300 documents from a fixed seed, written by json-io, each scope in turn -- the lines below a key or a header -- is pushed one, two or three levels further in: non-strict mode reads the document that was written, and strict mode names the first pushed line (over 500 scopes). Also each kind of scope by hand, at the root, nested and under a list item. Four fail on the previous code. 7 mutants, all caught. Official cases `decode/indentation-errors.json#12`, `#13`, `#32` and `#33` now pass, the last four.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: a header that is not one by the grammar is an error, in both modes.** TOON spec §5.2: a line with an unquoted bracket before its first unquoted colon is a header, and it must match the header grammar of §6. A header syntax error is none of non-strict mode's five recoveries.
  * **Non-strict mode read a header it could not parse as a key with a bracket in it**, and what followed the colon as its value, with no error: `items[abc]: 1,2,3` gave the key `items[abc]` with the string `1,2,3`; `rows[1]{a{b}c}:` gave a key of that text holding an empty object, and its rows and every field after them were dropped. Each is an error now.
  * **Both modes read as headers what the grammar does not allow:** a space between the bracket segment or the field list and the colon (`items[2] : 1,2`, `a[1]{x} :`), a length with a plus sign (`x[+3]:`) or with digits of another script (`k[٢]:`), a space or tab between a key and its bracket (`foo [2]:`, which gave the key `foo ` with its trailing space), an empty field list (`{}`) and an empty field entry (`{x,}`). All are refused, in both modes. A length is ASCII digits without a leading zero; nothing stands between a key, its bracket segment, its field list and the colon; a field list has at least one field, no empty entry, and a nested group stands directly after its name.
  * **Unchanged:** a field name that stands twice in one field list is still the one thing the mode decides (an error in strict mode, the last one wins in non-strict mode); a line without any colon is no header (`a[2]` alone is the string `a[2]`); a quoted key may hold a bracket (`"a[0]": x`); the key of a keyed row is whatever stands before its colon.
  * **Tests.** `ToonHeaderGrammarTest` (8). All 3,456 combinations of sixteen lengths, a keyed colon or none, six delimiter symbols, a space, a tab or nothing before the bracket, a space or nothing after it, and no field list, one, or one with a space before the colon: the line is read as a header exactly when the grammar allows it (45 of them), and is otherwise refused in both modes, and not for its count. Also ten field lists that are field lists and eighteen that are not, each as a field, as a list item's first field and in a keyed header; what makes a line a header line and what does not; and the wording of each refusal. Five fail on the previous code. 16 mutants, all caught. Three older tests that expected non-strict mode's reading follow. Official cases `decode/validation-errors.json#18`, `#25`, `#27`, `#28`, `#30`, `#35`, `#65`, `#66`, `#67`, `#83`, `#84`, `#90` and `#93` now pass: 4 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: every line belongs to the value, and a declared count never cuts a list short.** An array's scope is the lines one level below its header, an object's the lines one level below its key (TOON spec §8-§10). json-io read a list or a table until the declared count was met and no further, and in non-strict mode -- its default -- left whatever no reader had taken unread. **So non-strict mode dropped data with no error**, in all of these:
  * **Items beyond the count.** `items[2]:` with three items read two, and the fields after the list were lost with the third item; `pts[1]{x}:` with two rows read one. The count is a claim, checked in strict mode; it never ends a scope or cuts it short (§14.1, §14.4). Non-strict mode now reads every item, row and value the scope holds; strict mode refuses the mismatch as before. `a[0]: 1,2` read as an empty list in **both** modes: it is `[1, 2]` in non-strict mode and a count mismatch in strict mode.
  * **Lines that belong to nothing.** `a: 1`, then `  b: 2` indented under it, read as `{"a": 1}` and dropped `b` and every line after it. `hello`, `world` read as `hello`; `[2]: 1,2`, `junk: 3` as the array; a document indented as a whole as `{}`. A line among list items that is no item ended the list and the read. Each is an error now, in both modes: non-strict mode has five recoveries (§14.4), and none of them skips a line. Strict mode already refused them.
  * **A table row of the wrong width** (`items[1]{a,b,c}:` with the row `1,2`) gave a short object in non-strict mode. A row's width is checked in both modes (§14.1).
  * **Also refused now, in both modes:** a field on a line of its own under a bare `-` (the bare hyphen is the empty object; an object's first field stands on the hyphen line); a header without a key anywhere but at the root and as a list item (`a:` then `[2]: 1,2`), and one with a field list anywhere but at the root (`- [2]{x}:`); anything after the colon of a header that has a field list (`b[1]{c}: x`); a hyphen without its space (`-b`) or with a tab (`-<TAB>x`) where a list item must stand; a first line that is indented; list items or rows at their header's own depth. A line at row depth with a colon before its first delimiter is no row (§9.3) and ends the rows.
  * **BUG FIX: a declared count was used as an allocation size.** `a[2000000000]:` -- twenty characters -- threw `OutOfMemoryError` from `new ArrayList<>(count)`. A count beyond `int` (`a[99999999999999999999]: 1`) was "Invalid array count". A list is now given room for at most 1,024 items at first and grows with what is read, and a count of any length is a count (§6), which strict mode finds unmet.
  * **If you upgrade:** text json-io 4.113.0 wrote reads as before, with two exceptions -- the two shapes of the entry below, which 4.113.0 wrote and TOON does not have (a typed collection or a complex-key map as a list item; a complex-key map without type information), and a list of like objects directly inside a list (`- [2]{x}:`). Write such data again with 4.114.0; `toonLegacyForms(true)` now writes that last one item by item too, as the v4 forms do (148 of the 12,000 random legacy writes hold one and differ from the previous writer for it; the rest are identical). Text from elsewhere that leaned on non-strict mode's silence -- wrong counts with missing or extra items, stray lines -- now reads in full or fails, where it was cut short.
  * **Speed.** Reading costs what it did: ten kinds of document of 2,000 rows or items, before and after in interleaved fresh JVMs, are within 4% either way on JDK 21 and 25 (medians of 4, of 8 for the two that looked slower); on JDK 17 a table with a nested field group and inline arrays that hold a quoted token read 3-4% slower, the other eight the same or faster.
  * **Tests.** `ToonScopeTest` (16). For 300 documents from a fixed seed, written by json-io, each of their 711 declared counts in turn is made wrong (one more, one less, zero, two thousand million): non-strict mode still reads the document that was written, and strict mode refuses every one. Also each case above by hand, in both modes; 2,000 headers that each declare two thousand million items, read without running out of memory; where a header may stand; rows under a list item's first field; which lines are rows; and the one recovery about depth (non-strict mode takes a scope's depth from its first line, and a later line shallower than that is still an error). All sixteen fail on the previous code. 21 mutants: 20 caught; the one that survived showed a redundant condition, which is gone. A dozen older tests whose input was one of the shapes above, or that expected a list cut at its count, follow the spec now. 33 official cases pass with this: 17 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: json-io no longer writes two shapes that TOON does not have.** Both were read back by json-io's own reader and by no other; the spec has a reader refuse them (§6, §10), and json-io's reader is about to.
  * **A field on a line of its own under a bare hyphen.** In a list an object's first field stands on the hyphen line, and a bare `-` is the empty object. json-io wrote a list, set or array that carries its `$type` or `$id`, and a map whose keys are not strings, as a bare `-` with `$type:` and `$items[N]:` on the lines below. They are now `- $type: ArrayList` with `$items[2]: 1,2` one level below the hyphen, like any other object in a list.
  * **A header without a key under `key:`.** Without type information a map whose keys are not strings was written as `key:`, then `[N]:`, then one `$key` / `$value` pair per entry under hyphens at that header's own depth. A keyless header stands only at the root and as a list item. Such a map is now its keys beside its values, `$keys[N]` and `$items[N]` -- the form json-io has always used with type information on, and the one it uses in JSON (`@keys` / `@items`). Keys that are alike make a table: a `Map<Point, String>` of two entries is `$keys[2]{x,y}:`, two rows, and `$items[2]: a,b`.
  * **Reading.** Into a declared map field both the old text and the new give the same map. Elsewhere the old form did not come back a map at all: an element of a declared `List<Map<Point, String>>` read back as a list of its `$key` / `$value` entries, and so did such a map read without a declared type. Both are the map now. json-io 4.113.0 reads the new text for both shapes exactly as it read its own: checked with its reader over an object graph holding each of them, in three type-information modes, in strict and non-strict mode.
  * **`toonLegacyForms(true)` writes the new shapes too.** Legacy text is for a reader of 4.113.0, which reads them; and 4.114.0, which will refuse the old ones, reads what a peer in legacy mode writes. The text is unchanged for everything else: the 12,000 random writes compared with the previous writer hold none of these shapes and are identical.
  * **BUG FIX**: the keys and the values of such a map are taken from one pass over its entries. They were taken from `keySet()` and then from `values()`, which paired a key with the wrong value for a map whose two views go in different orders. java-util's `MultiKeyMap` was one (its `keySet()` is fixed in java-util 4.114.0 as well): written to TOON without type information it read back with values swapped.
  * **Tests.** `ToonWrittenShapeTest` (7): the exact text for typed collections, sets and arrays as list items, for shared ones (`- $id: 2`, `- $ref: 2`), for maps with object, list, map and number keys in a field, a list, a declared list of maps, an `Object` field and at the root, and for a map whose `keySet()` runs backwards; each read back in both modes. And 400 graphs from a fixed seed -- lists, sets, arrays and maps with keys of every kind, some held twice -- written six ways (no type information, minimal, minimal-plus and always, with references, with and without legacy forms): a check of the text itself finds no line under a bare hyphen, no keyless header under a key, no list item at its header's depth, no blank line and no trailing space; both modes read it; and with full type information what is read is what was written. All seven fail on the previous code. 10 mutants: 9 caught; the tenth removes a reset that nothing reaches. Five tests that described the entry form (`ToonReaderTest` 3, and two written this release) follow.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON: a line ends at its line feed, and only one carriage return goes with it.** TOON spec §12: a document is split at LF, and a single CR at the end of a line belongs to the line ending, so CRLF input reads like LF input; a CR anywhere else is content. json-io ended a line at every carriage return. `a: x`, CR, CR, LF read as `x` followed by a blank line where the spec has the value `x\r`, and a CR in the middle of a value cut it in two lines: `a: 1`, CR, `b: 2` was two fields.
  * **Now** a line is read to its line feed and one carriage return is taken off its end. CRLF documents read as before, from a `String` and from a stream; so does a last line that ends in a CR without a line feed. A document whose only line endings are bare CRs (classic Mac OS) is one line, as the spec has it. json-io writes LF only and writes a CR inside a string escaped, as `\r`, so its own output is unaffected.
  * **Needs java-util 4.114.0**, which adds `FastReader.readLineLf` and `readLineBorrowedLf` for it: `readLine` ends a line at a lone CR and does not say how a line ended.
  * **Speed.** Reading is as fast as before: two tables of 2,000 rows read within 2.3% either way on JDK 17, 21 and 25 (interleaved fresh JVMs, medians of 4).
  * **Tests.** `ToonLineEndingsTest` (7): a document with every construct read with LF, with CRLF, with both mixed and with a trailing CR, from a `String` and from UTF-8 bytes, in both modes; blank lines and comments with CRLF; a CR in a value, a key, an array token and a table cell; a second and a third CR before the LF; 4,000 lines of every length from 1 to 60 and one of 50,000 characters, so that line endings fall at every place of the reader's buffer; a last line without a line feed of every length around the powers of two up to 131,072, which fills the reader's room for a line exactly as the input ends; and what the writer writes for strings with a CR. Three fail on the previous code. 7 mutants, all caught. Official case `decode/whitespace.json#15` now passes: 50 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt` -- 20 that strict mode must reject and 30 that non-strict mode must.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: a key that holds a dot is written without quotes.** TOON spec §7.3: a key is unquoted when it matches `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.]*`, the dot included, and quoted otherwise. json-io quoted a dotted key (`"server.port": 8080`) unless `toonKeyFolding` was on, to keep it from a reader that expands paths. It is now `server.port: 8080`: as a `Map`'s key, an object's field name (`@IoProperty("server.port")`), a table's field name, a keyed row's key and in a list item.
  * **Reading is unchanged**: by default a dotted key is the one key it is written as, quoted or not. Only a reader told to expand paths (`toonExpandPaths(true)`, json-io's option from spec v3) splits an unquoted dotted key into nested objects; text written by 4.114.0 and read that way has its dotted keys expanded, where 4.113.0's quotes kept them whole.
  * **For an older reader** nothing needs doing (4.113.0 reads the unquoted key as one key too), and `toonLegacyForms(true)` writes the quotes as before: the legacy text is the previous writer's, character for character, over the 12,000 random writes of the entry on the v4 forms, 837 lines of which hold a dotted key.
  * **Tests.** `ToonDottedKeyTest` (8). The oracle is the spec's pattern: 5,000 keys from a fixed seed over letters, digits, underscore, dot, hyphen, space and colon are quoted exactly when the pattern does not match, in a field and in a list item, and read back whole in both modes. Also the exact text in each position, an object's renamed field, the keys that stay quoted (a leading dot or digit, a hyphen, a non-ASCII letter), the legacy forms with and without key folding, key folding itself, and a reader that expands paths. Five fail on the previous code. 6 mutants, all caught. Official case `encode/objects.json#19` now passes: all 160 official cases for writing pass, and 51 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`, all for reading.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON: in a `key: value` line the key may be empty, and the value is one value.** TOON spec §5.2: the first unquoted colon ends the key, and what follows it is the value.
  * **The empty key.** `: 1` is `{"": 1}` (§7.4). json-io failed on such a line in a nested object or after another field with an internal error ("Error parsing TOON value", from a `StringIndexOutOfBoundsException`), read a document that starts with it as one string, and took `- : 3` in a list for the string `: 3`. The empty key is now read at the root, in a nested object, as a list item's first or later field and as the key of a keyed row; twice in one object it is a duplicate like any other key. json-io writes it in quotes (`"": 1`), as before, and reads both forms.
  * **A value that starts with a bracket is a string.** In a field only the token `[]` means something after the colon: the empty array (§9.1). json-io read a whole array header there: `a: [2]: x,y` gave the array `["x","y"]`, and `a: [2]: x` failed in strict mode with a count mismatch. Both are the strings they are, as are `[2]{x}:`, `[0]:` and `[x`. An array's header stands before the colon (`a[2]: x,y`), and a header without a key at the root and as a list item, where the spec has them; json-io has never written one after a colon. In a table cell, an inline array and a keyed row, `[]` is a string too, as it was.
  * **A header without a key is no field.** `a: 1` followed by `[2]: x,y` is an error in both modes (§6: a keyless header stands only at the root and as a list item), with a message that says so. It was an error before only by accident, through the internal error above. An array under the empty key is `""[2]: x,y`.
  * **Tests.** `ToonKeyValueLineTest` (14): the empty key in each position, twice, written and read back in a `Map`, a table, a list and a declared `Map<String, Integer>`, and at row depth; bracket-led values in a field, a nested object and a list item's fields; `[]` with and without spaces; headers where they belong and where they do not; `[]` as a cell; and what the writer writes for arrays in fields. Nine fail on the previous code. 13 mutants, all caught. Official cases `decode/objects.json#10` and `#47` now pass: 52 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: a line is split into tokens as the spec has it.** Four rules of TOON spec §5.2, §7.4 and §11.2, for inline arrays, table rows and keyed rows alike. json-io broke each of them somewhere, differently in each of its three splitters.
  * **An empty token is the empty string, and every delimiter has a token after it.** An empty table cell (`1,,x`) was read as `null`; in an inline array it was `""` unless the line also held a quoted token, and then `null`. A delimiter at the end of a line was not followed by a token when the declared count was already met, so `a[2]: x,y,` passed in strict mode. N delimiters now make N + 1 tokens everywhere: that line is a count mismatch in strict mode and `["x","y",""]` in non-strict mode.
  * **A quote opens a quoted span wherever it stands**, also in the middle of a token, and the next quote that is not part of an escape closes it; a delimiter inside is part of the token. `k[2]: a"b,c",d` is the two strings `a"b,c"` and `d`. Strict mode failed on it with "Unclosed quoted string"; it also refused the plain string `abc"`.
  * **Outside a span a backslash is a character like another.** `paths[2]: C:\,D:\` was one token (the backslash "escaped" the comma): a count mismatch in strict mode and, in non-strict mode, the single string `C:\,D:\` with no error. It is two.
  * **A token that opens with a quote ends at its closing quote, in both modes.** `k: "abc" def`, `k: "abc` and `k: "abc\"` (its last quote is escaped) are errors. Non-strict mode returned such a token as a string with its quotes in it, and took the last of these for `abc\`. The spec's non-strict mode has five recoveries (§14.4), and this is none of them.
  * **If you upgrade:** an empty cell read into a declared class is now what the empty string is anywhere in json-io (`""` for a `String`, `0` for an `Integer`, `false` for a `Boolean`), as a quoted `""` in a field always was; it was `null`. json-io writes `null` for a null and `""` for an empty string, so its own output reads as before. Text with an unterminated quoted string, or with text after a closing quote, is now refused in non-strict mode too.
  * **How.** One loop shape in all three splitters: a quoted span is entered at a quote and left at the next unescaped one, and tokens are read straight from the line. The two slower paths that copied a line holding a quote or a backslash into a buffer, with their own rules, are gone, and a line with a backslash but no quote stays on the fast path. **Measured** (reading 2,000 rows or items, the reader before the TOON fixes of this release against the reader after this one -- so with the number grammar, the trimming and the blank-line handling of the entries below -- in interleaved fresh JVMs, medians of 4 on JDK 17, 21 and 25): a table with two quoted cells in each row reads 42%, 31% and 48% faster, inline arrays that hold a quoted token 24%, 26% and 31% faster; lines without a quote -- typed and untyped tables, a table with a nested field group, lists of unlike objects, lists of lists, Maps mode -- read as fast as before, between 6% faster and 4% slower with no direction to it.
  * **Tests.** `ToonDelimitedTokensTest` (11). The oracle is a line built from cells whose values are known in advance: 3,000 lines of four to seven cells from a fixed seed, drawn from nineteen kinds (plain, number, literal, empty, padded, quoted with the delimiter inside, with an escaped quote, a backslash, a span in mid-token, a span left open to the end of the line), are joined by each of the three delimiters and read as an inline array, a table row, a row under a nested field group and a keyed row, in both modes -- five readings of each line, all the same list of values. Also each rule by hand, the errors in both modes, the colon and bracket after a key, a typed table, and 2,000 documents of strings made of quotes, backslashes, delimiters and colons that json-io writes and reads back. Nine fail on the previous code. 20 mutants: 19 caught; the one that survives sends a row with a backslash down the path for quotes, which gives the same answer. Three tests in `ToonReaderTest` that expected the lenient reading of an unterminated string now expect the error. Official cases `decode/delimiters.json#22`, `decode/objects.json#32` and `decode/validation-errors.json#69`, `#102` now pass: 54 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: an object with no fields is written as the empty object, and `{}` is read as a string.** TOON's empty object is `key:` with nothing after it in a field, a bare `-` in a list and an empty document at the root (spec §8, §9.4); it has no `{}`. json-io wrote an empty `Map` that way, but an *object* with no field to write -- a class without fields, or one whose fields are all null under `skipNullFields(true)` -- as `{}`.
  * **In a field the text was broken**: `key:`, then `{}` on a line of its own at the left margin. json-io could not read it back ("Unexpected content after root value" in strict mode; in non-strict mode the rest of the document was dropped). Such an object is now `key:`, in a field, as a `Map`'s value, as a list item's first or later field, at the end of a folded key, and as the key or value of a map whose keys are not strings. In a list it is a bare `-` (was `- {}`), at the root an empty document (was `{}`).
  * **Reading: `{}` is a string**, as the spec has it (§7.4: a value an encoder would have quoted is read as it stands) -- in a field, a list item, an array, a table cell and at the root. json-io read it as an empty object in a field, a list and at the root. An empty object read into a declared class is still an instance of that class with its initial values.
  * **If you upgrade:** TOON text that json-io 4.113.0 wrote with `- {}` or a root `{}` for an object without fields now reads as the string `{}` in those places; write it again with 4.114.0. (The field form could not be read by any version.) json-io writes the string `{}` in quotes, as before.
  * **Tests.** `ToonEmptyObjectTest` (8): the exact text for an object with nothing to write in each position, among them a class with no fields, one with all fields null and one whose fields come from an `@IoAnyGetter`; the read back into the declared classes and the same text written again; the root; with type information or an id, where the object is not empty; `{}` read in every position; the string `{}` written and read; and 400 graphs from a fixed seed, with nulls written and skipped, whose text has no blank line, no `{}` and no trailing space, is read in strict mode and writes the same text again. Six fail on the previous code. 15 mutants, all caught.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON: a blank line never ends an object.** TOON spec §12: a blank line never creates or closes structure and is never counted; it is an error only inside a header span -- from an array's or keyed table's first item, row or entry line through the last line of its content -- and only in strict mode.
  * **A blank line after `key:` ended that object.** `a:`, a blank line, `  b: 1`, `  c: 2` read in non-strict mode, json-io's default, as `{"a": {}}`: **the nested fields were dropped with no error.** Strict mode failed with "Unexpected content after root value". Both now read `{"a": {"b": 1, "c": 2}}`.
  * **A blank line between a header and its first item was refused** in strict mode (`items[2]:`, a blank line, `  - a`): it lies before the span, and is now ignored, for a list, a table and a keyed table alike.
  * **Inside a list item, non-strict mode dropped what followed the blank line**, again with no error: `- a: 1`, a blank line, `    b: 2` read as `{"a": 1}`, and `- k:`, a blank line, the nested fields read as `{"k": {}}`. A blank line inside a span is ignored there (§14.4, recovery 4), and the item is read whole.
  * **Strict mode refuses every blank line inside a span**, with the number of that line. It let one through between two list items when the first held a nested object, and reported others as "Unexpected content after root value" at a later line.
  * **How.** Blank lines are skipped in one place, where lines are fetched, and no reader sees one; the first blank line's number is kept until the next line is taken. If that line is taken while an array's or keyed table's span is open, the blank line lay inside the span. Each of the nine places that skipped blank lines for themselves, each with its own idea of the rule, is gone.
  * **Tests.** `ToonBlankLinesTest` (7). The oracle restates the spec's rule on the text of a document: 300 documents from a fixed seed (objects, inline arrays, tables with and without nested groups, lists, keyed tables, nested up to five deep) are written by json-io, and a blank line -- empty, of spaces, or two of them -- is put at each of their 3,601 positions in turn (1,346 inside a span). Non-strict mode always reads the document that was written; strict mode does too, except where the position lies inside a header span, and there it names that line. Also the cases above by hand, the edges of the document, a typed read, and the kind of span named in the message. Five fail on the previous code. 20 mutants, all caught. Official cases `decode/blank-lines.json#10`, `#11`, `#12`, `#16`, `#17` and `#20` now pass: 58 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: only spaces are trimmed; in non-strict mode a tab indents one level.** In TOON the space, U+0020, is the only whitespace (spec §12): around a token and at the end of a line spaces are removed and nothing else. json-io removed every character up to the space -- tabs and the other control characters with it.
  * **A tab-indented document was flattened** in non-strict mode, json-io's default, with no error: `a:`, `<TAB>b:`, `<TAB><TAB>c: 1`, `<TAB>d: 2` read as `{"a":{},"b":{},"c":1,"d":2}`. The tab was taken off the front of each key, and every line stood at the left margin. Each tab now counts as one level and each `indentSize` spaces as another, wherever they stand in the indentation (spec §14.4, recovery 3), so that document reads `{"a":{"b":{"c":1},"d":2}}`. Strict mode refuses a tab in the indentation, as before.
  * **With the tab as delimiter, an empty last token was lost**: `a[2<TAB>]: x<TAB>` failed in strict mode with a count mismatch and read `["x"]` in non-strict mode; a table row lost its last cell the same way. The tab that ends the line is a delimiter, and both now read their two values.
  * **A tab or control character at the edge of a token is kept**: `a: v<TAB>` is `"v\t"`, `a:<TAB>1` is the string `"\t1"` and no number, `k<TAB>: 1` has the key `"k\t"`; likewise in an inline array, a table cell, a field list, a keyed row and a list item. A no-break space was always kept. A token with a tab beside it is not the empty array `[]` or a number either.
  * **A tab after a closing quote is an error in strict mode** (`a: "x"<TAB>`, `"k"<TAB>: 1`): a token that opens with a quote ends with its closing quote. It was trimmed away.
  * **If you upgrade:** what json-io writes is unaffected -- it quotes any string that holds a tab or a control character or has a space at either end. TOON written by hand or by another tool with a tab after a colon (`key:<TAB>value`) now has that tab in the value.
  * **Tests.** `ToonSpacesOnlyTest` (14): values, keys, field names, array tokens, table cells, keyed rows and list items with a tab, a no-break space or a control character at either edge, on the fast path and on the one a quoted token takes; the empty last token of a tab-delimited line; a tab after a closing quote; a tab inside a header where none may stand; strict mode's refusal of a tab in the indentation; non-strict mode's count of tabs and spaces, in a nested object, a list and a table; blank lines; and what the writer writes for such strings, with each delimiter, read back. Eleven fail on the previous code; three guard what did not change. 46 mutants -- one for each of the 32 places that trim, the rest in the indentation: 40 caught, the six that survive change nothing that can be observed (three are fast paths with the same answer, one trims a position that is never a space, two set the depth of a blank line, which nothing reads). Official cases `decode/whitespace.json#4`, `#5`, `#6`, `decode/validation-errors.json#77`, `#78`, `decode/comments.json#11` and `decode/indentation-errors.json#11` now pass: 64 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON: a byte-order mark at the start of a document is removed.** TOON spec §12: a reader removes a single U+FEFF at the very start of the document before it looks at the first line in any other way; a U+FEFF anywhere else is content. json-io kept it. A file saved with a mark, as Windows editors do, read back with the mark as the first character of its first key: read into a class, **the first field was lost with no error** (`name: Ada`, `age: 36` gave a `Person` with `name` null); a comment on the first line was taken for the document (strict mode failed at the second line; non-strict mode returned the comment's text and dropped the rest); a document that is one number read as a string.
  * **Reading.** The mark is removed from a `String` and from a stream alike, in strict and non-strict mode, before comments and blank lines are found. Only one is removed, and only there: a second U+FEFF, or one that starts a later line, a value or a key, stays where it is.
  * **Writing.** json-io writes no mark, as before. A document that is a single string starting with U+FEFF is now written in quotes (§7.2), since without them a reader removes its first character. This holds under `toonLegacyForms(true)` too -- the one place where legacy text differs from what 4.113.0 wrote: quoted, every reader reads the string; unquoted, 4.114.0 would not.
  * **Tests.** `ToonByteOrderMarkTest` (8): an object, a nested object, a root array, a root table, a keyed table and a quoted first key after the mark; a root primitive; the mark before a comment, before a blank line and alone; a second mark and marks elsewhere kept; a typed read; the root string quoted in both writing modes and read back; no quotes for the mark in a field, an array cell or a root array. Each read is made from a `String`, from UTF-8 bytes and into Maps, in both modes. Six fail on the previous code; two guard what did not change. 9 mutants: 8 caught; the ninth removes a bounds guard for an empty first line. Official cases `decode/whitespace.json#11`, `#16`, `#17` and `encode/primitives.json#37` now pass: 71 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON: a token is a number only when it is one in JSON's grammar.** TOON spec §4: an unquoted token is a number if and only if it matches `-?(0|[1-9][0-9]*)(\.[0-9]+)?([eE][+-]?[0-9]+)?`, and a reader must not leave that decision to its language's number parser. json-io did, after a check of the characters: `.5`, `1.` and `+1` were read as `0.5`, `1.0` and `1`. The spec has an encoder write the strings `.5` and `1.` without quotes, so such a string written by another TOON library came back from json-io a number, changed and with no error.
  * **Reading.** `.5`, `1.`, `+1`, `+1.5e3` and `1.e5` are strings now, in a field, an inline array, a table cell and a list item, in strict and non-strict mode. `05`, `1e`, `1_000`, `0x10`, `NaN` and `Infinity` were strings already. What is a number reads as before: `1.5000`, `-1E+03`, `0e1`, `-0`, integers beyond a `long`. A field declared as a number is still given its value, since a string is converted as a quoted one always was: `price: .5` into a `double` is `0.5`.
  * **Writing.** A string is quoted for resembling a number when it is *numeric-like* in the spec's words (§7.2: `[+-]?[0-9]+(\.[0-9]+)?([eE][+-]?[0-9]+)?`), and only then. json-io quoted more: `.5`, `5.`, `1e` and `1.e5` are now written without quotes, as the spec requires, and read back as the strings they are.
  * **For an older reader.** json-io 4.113.0 and earlier make a number of an unquoted `.5`. Under `toonLegacyForms(true)` these strings keep their quotes, and the text is still what 4.113.0 wrote, character for character: compared again with the previous writer over 23,000 generated tokens in fields and arrays with each delimiter, and over the 12,000 random writes of the entry below.
  * **Tests.** `ToonNumberGrammarTest` (6). The oracle is the spec's own two patterns. Every token of up to four characters from `0 1 9 . e + -` (2,800) and 20,000 longer ones from a wider alphabet and a fixed seed (`x`, `_`, `d`, `f`, `L`, `p` included: `1d`, `1f` and `0x1p3` are numbers to Java) are read in a field, an inline array, a table and a list item, in both modes: a number exactly when the grammar says so, with the token's value, otherwise the token itself. The same tokens written as strings are quoted exactly when numeric-like (or led by a hyphen) and read back unchanged. Also the spec's examples, a declared number, a table and an object's fields, and the legacy forms asked in turn with the spec's, since the two kinds of writer share a cache of quoting decisions. All six fail on the previous code. 26 mutants, all caught. Official cases `decode/numbers.json#10`, `#11` and `encode/objects.json#30` now pass: 75 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **A permanent setting reaches a call that passes no options.** `WriteOptionsBuilder.addPermanent...` and `ReadOptionsBuilder.addPermanent...` are documented to hold for the life of the JVM, and they did for every options object built afterwards. They never held for `JsonIo.toJson(obj, null)`, `JsonIo.toJava(json, null)`, `JsonIo.toToon(obj, null)`, `JsonIo.fromToon(toon, null)`, `JsonIo.deepCopy(obj, null, null)`, `JsonIo.createGenerator(out, null)` or `JsonIo.createTokenizer(in, null)`: the options those calls use were built once -- the two general ones while the builder class loaded, before the body of the first `addPermanent...` call could run, the four special ones in `JsonIo` at their first use -- and never again. So `addPermanentAlias`, `addPermanentWriteLongsAsStrings`, `addPermanentExcludedField`, a permanent writer or class factory, a permanent limit and every other one of the 79 were skipped by exactly the callers that configure nothing, whenever they were made; and a setting made between two first uses reached `toToon(obj, null)` but not `toJson(obj, null)`. Long-standing, not new in 4.114.0.
  * **Now** those options are built at first use and built again after a permanent setting is made or removed, so a call without options sees what a `new WriteOptionsBuilder().build()` / `new ReadOptionsBuilder().build()` made at that moment would see. `WriteOptionsBuilder.getDefaultWriteOptions()` and `ReadOptionsBuilder.getDefaultReadOptions()` return the same instance until the next permanent setting, and a new one after it.
  * **If you upgrade:** code that made a permanent setting and also passed `null` options now gets that setting. That is what the setting was made for, but it is a change: for instance `addPermanentAlias(Foo.class, "foo")` now writes `"@type":"foo"` from `toJson(obj, null)` too. It also makes the upgrade notes above true as written: `addPermanentUseGetters(true)` / `addPermanentUseSetters(true)` at startup cover every call.
  * **How.** A counter per builder class, moved on by every permanent setting; `DefaultOptionsCache` keeps the options with the count they were built at and builds again when the count has moved. The count is read before building, so a setting made by another thread while the options are being built is not lost: the next call builds again. A call without options costs one volatile read and one counter read more than it did, which does not show: writing and reading an object of one field with `null` options, the smallest work there is, takes the same time before and after to within 2% either way (interleaved fresh JVMs, four rounds each on JDK 17, 21 and 25).
  * **Tests.** `PermanentSettingsReachNullOptionsTest` (11): a write setting, an alias added and removed on each side, TOON's delimiter and legacy forms, the generator, `deepCopy`, a read setting (JSON and TOON), a read limit and the tokenizer, each made *after* the defaults had been used and each seen by a call that passes `null`; the default instance stays the same between settings; and two sweeps over the permanent settings themselves -- the 49 that take one primitive are called by reflection with the value they already have, the other 34 one by one with a class nothing else uses -- checking that each moves the count, and that no public permanent setting of either builder is outside both sweeps, so one added later without the count fails the build. `DefaultOptionsCacheTest` (3): built once, built again after a setting, a setting made during the build not lost. All eleven fail on the previous code. 45 mutants, all caught: 30 of them each take the count out of one of the settings that the second sweep covers.
* **BUG FIX**: **The elements of a TOON list are read wherever the list stands.** TOON's parser hands a list to the resolver as a `java.util.List` (JSON's hands an `Object[]`), and only a list that was the value of a field declared as a collection or an array, or the root, had its elements read. Anywhere else -- an `Object` field, a `Map`'s value, a list inside a list -- the list was passed on as parsed. 4.113.0 does the same; JSON reads all of these correctly.
  * **A `$ref` in such a list came back an empty map**, with no error: written with `cycleSupport(true)`, a map held by a field and again in a list inside a `Map<String, Object>` read back once as itself and once as `{}`. The same in Maps mode (`fromToonToMaps`).
  * **An element that names its `$type` was not built**: it stayed a map.
  * **The inner lists of a `List<List<Integer>>` held `Long`s** (a `ClassCastException` at first use), those of a `List<List<Point>>` held maps.
  * All are now read as JSON reads them: a reference is the object it names, also when it comes before the `$id`; a typed element is built; an inner list holds its declared elements. An untyped slot still gets the `ArrayList` it got before.
  * **Speed.** A TOON document that names no `$type`, `$id` or `$ref` anywhere is plain data: the reader notes that, and its lists in untyped slots are handed over as before, at the same cost (within 3% either way on JDK 17, 21 and 25, typed tables and Maps mode included). Only in a document that names one are such lists read element by element, as JSON's arrays always are; done for every document, that took untyped lists 2 to 2.8 times as long to read. A `List<List<Integer>>` costs 12-27% more to read than it did when it was handed `Long`s.
  * **Tests.** `ToonListElementsResolvedTest` (12): a `$ref` in a list in a `Map`, in an `Object` field, in a list in a list, in a `Map<String, List<Object>>`, in an array, to a map and to an object, forward, inside the root list, and in Maps mode -- each the very object it names; typed elements and typed table rows in untyped lists; `List<List<Integer>>`, `List<List<Point>>`, `List<List<Map<String, Point>>>`; plain lists unchanged in class and content; and 300 graphs from a fixed seed with maps and objects shared between untyped lists, where TOON reads what JSON reads and as many distinct objects. Eleven of the twelve fail on the previous code. 18 mutants, all caught.
* **FEATURE / BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **TOON is written with the forms of TOON spec v4: keyed tables and nested field groups.** The spec requires them of an encoder (§9.3, §9.5), and they are what makes TOON small for nested data. json-io has read both since the entries below; it now writes them.
  * **An object of like objects is a keyed table.** A `Map`, or any object, with at least two entries whose values are objects with the same fields is written as one header and one row per entry, each row led by its key: `servers[2:]{host,port}:`, then `alpha: a.example.com,8080`. A `Map<String, Point>` field is one; so is an object whose fields all hold like objects. At the root the header has no key: `[2:]{age,city}:`. Such an object was written field by field.
  * **A table column of like objects is a nested field group.** A list of objects with the same fields was a table only when every field held a primitive. A field that holds objects with the same fields is now a group in the header, `orders[2]{id,customer{name,country},total}:`, and its values are cells of the row, `1,Ada,DK,99`. Groups nest to any depth. A `List<Order>` whose orders hold a `Customer` is one. Such a list was written item by item.
  * **A list directly inside a list is written item by item**, never as a table: a header with a field list and no key stands only at the root (§9.4), and a spec v4 reader refuses the `- [2]{x}:` that 4.113.0 wrote there.
  * **Rules.** Every row must have the same fields, each holding a primitive or a like object: one `null` where the others hold an object, an empty object, a list, or a missing field, and the list is written item by item (the object field by field). The first row gives the order of the fields at every level, and read back every row has them in that order. Written as before: an object shared when references are written (`cycleSupport(true)`; it needs its `$id`); an object that carries `$type` (type info on and its class not given by the declaration, or `@IoShowType`), while a table's rows still carry theirs in the table's `$type` column; an object with an `@IoAnyGetter`, or one written as its custom writer's data; under `prettyPrint(true)`, any object that is not a `Map`; and the value of a folded key (`toonKeyFolding`).
  * **Who reads it.** json-io 4.114.0. **json-io 4.113.0 and earlier, and any other reader of TOON spec v3, do not**: 4.113.0 in its default mode reads a keyed header as an odd key and loses the rows, with no error. **To write for such a reader set `WriteOptionsBuilder.toonLegacyForms(true)`**, or `addPermanentToonLegacyForms(true)` for every `WriteOptions` built afterwards. The text is then what 4.113.0 wrote, character for character (compared over 12,000 random writes, 12.8 MB, across delimiters, type-info modes, `cycleSupport`, `skipNullFields`, `prettyPrint` and key folding).
  * **Size and speed** (2,000 objects, `cycleSupport(false)`, before and after in interleaved fresh JVMs, medians of 4 on JDK 17, 21 and 25). A list of orders that each hold a customer and an address: 67% less text, written 34-37% faster, read back 28-31% faster. A `Map` of points: 52% less text, written 33-34% faster, read back 29-33% faster. On JDK 8 both are written 42-44% faster. Data the new forms do not apply to -- a table of primitive columns, objects with a list field, maps of unlike maps -- is written and read as fast as before, to within 3% either way, and with `toonLegacyForms(true)` so is everything. Looking for a keyed table costs each object and `Map` in a field one look at its first value; done less carefully (the full check, with cycle tracking, for every one) it cost 13-17% on such data.
  * **Reading.** A table's `$type` column, which json-io writes in quotes, was not recognised in a header that also has a nested field group (rows read as maps, not as their class). It is now, as in any other header.
  * **Tests.** `ToonWriteNestedFieldGroupsTest` (26), `ToonWriteKeyedTabularTest` (24), `ToonLegacyFormsTest` (5): the exact text for `Map`s and for objects, both sides of every rule above, the three delimiters, key and cell quoting, the root, a list item's first and later fields, type info in each mode, shared objects and cycles, the option, its copy and its permanent form; everything written is read back in strict and non-strict mode and compared with what was written. `ToonV4FormsDifferentialTest` (3): 400 documents and 240 object graphs from a fixed seed, shaped to meet the rules from both sides, written in the v4 and the legacy forms and read back in both modes -- all four readings equal what was written (232 of the 400 documents use a new form). 48 mutants: 47 caught; the one that survives removes a shortcut whose answer the full check gives again. The 15 official cases for writing these forms now pass: 78 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`, 3 of them for writing.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON reads a list item whose first field is an empty object.** A list item that is an object carries its first field on the hyphen line, and that field stands one level below the hyphen (TOON spec §10). json-io writes an empty map there as `- labels:`, with the item's other fields on the lines below, as the spec has it. The reader took those lines for the content of the empty field: `{"labels": {}, "name": "write", "rank": 7}` in a list was read back as `{"labels": {"name": "write", "rank": 7}}`, with no error, in strict and non-strict mode (4.113.0 too). They are now read as the item's other fields. What a first field holds lies two levels below the hyphen, where json-io has written it since 4.92.0, and is read as before; content only *one* level below the hyphen now reads as further fields of the item, as the spec says, not as the first field's own.
  * **Tests.** `ToonListItemEmptyFirstFieldTest` (5): the spec's case; what the writer writes for such items (an empty first field followed by fields, alone, and several in a row) read back; content two levels down still the first field's own; a quoted key and a nested list; a typed list of objects whose first field is an empty `Map`. Four fail on the previous code, in both modes; the fifth guards what did not change. Mutants (the old comparison, one level too deep, the first field at the hyphen's depth) each fail it. Official case `decode/arrays-nested.json#21` now passes: 93 of the 564 remain in `known-gaps.txt`.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON reads a list item whose first key is quoted.** A list item that is an object carries its first field on the hyphen line, and json-io writes that field's key in quotes when it holds a space, a hyphen, a leading digit or any character outside `[A-Za-z0-9_]`: `- "content-type": text/plain`. The reader took every hyphen line that starts with a quote for a string, so it could not read its own output for a list of maps that are not uniform: strict mode failed with `Unclosed quoted string`, and non-strict mode returned the line as a string and lost the rest of the list. It now reads the object, including a quoted first key that opens an array, a table or a nested object. A quoted string that holds a colon (`- "a: b"`) is still a string. The defect dates from the first TOON release. Tests: `ToonListItemQuotedKeyTest` (5; 4 fail on the old reader).
* **FEATURE**: **TOON reads the keyed tabular form.** Since TOON spec v4.0 an object whose values are uniform objects is a table whose rows carry their own keys (spec §9.5): the keyed header `servers[2:]{host,port}:` names the fields once, and each entry is one row, `alpha: a.example.com,8080`. A v4 encoder writes every such object this way, the root object too (`[2:]{age,city}:`). json-io now reads it: the object has one entry per row, a row's cells are read as a table row's are (nested field groups, the header's delimiter, quoted cells), and a typed target is filled as from any object, be it a `Map<String, Server>` or a Java object whose fields are the entry keys. Before, strict mode failed on the header, and **non-strict mode, json-io's default, returned `{"servers[2": "]{host,port}:"}` and dropped the rows without an error.** A row is split at its first unquoted colon, so an entry key may hold a delimiter or look like a header (`k[2]: 6`), and the object ends where the depth drops back to the header's. The syntax is new, so there is no lenient reading to keep: a row with too few or too many cells, a row without a colon, a row at another depth, text after the header's colon and a keyed header without a field list are errors in both modes. Non-strict mode differs by four recoveries of spec §14.4: the declared count is not checked, a repeated key lets the last one win, a blank line between rows is ignored, and a first row indented too far sets the depth of the rows. json-io does not write the keyed form yet. Reading is as fast as before. Tests: `ToonKeyedTabularTest` (18; all fail on the old reader), and 25 spec fixture cases leave `known-gaps.txt` (94 left), among them all 18 of the spec's keyed reading cases.
* **FEATURE**: **TOON reads nested field groups in a tabular header.** Since TOON spec v4.0 a field of a tabular header may carry its own field list, `orders[2]{id,customer{name,country},total}:`, and a v4 encoder writes a column of uniform objects that way (spec §9.3). A row then holds one cell per leaf field in the header's depth-first order, and the reader rebuilds the nesting: `1,Ada,DK,99` is `{id: 1, customer: {name: Ada, country: DK}, total: 99}`. Groups nest to any depth (bounded by `maxDepth`), follow the header's delimiter, and fill nested Java objects when the target is typed. Before, strict mode failed on such a header, and non-strict mode returned the whole header as one key with an empty value. A row under nested groups must hold exactly one cell per leaf field, in non-strict mode too: without every cell the nesting cannot be rebuilt. json-io does not write nested field groups yet. **The header is now read from the line itself**, by one parser for a header with a key and a header without one, which changes four edge cases: a field name may hold a colon (`{2key,x:y}`) and a quoted name may hold braces; in strict mode a name repeated within one field list (`{a,a}`) is an error, as spec §14.2 requires (non-strict mode still lets the last one win); a tab-delimited array with no key keeps an empty first value (`[2<TAB>]: <TAB>b` read as one value); and a malformed header with no key is an error in both modes, where `[0]{unclosed` read as an empty array and `[2]{a,b}x:` ignored the `x`. Reading is as fast as before (measured on tabular rows, array headers and nested objects). Tests: `ToonNestedFieldGroupsTest` (16; 14 fail on the old reader), two more in `ToonReaderCoverageTest`, and 12 spec fixture cases leave `known-gaps.txt` (119 left).
* **FEATURE**: **TOON reads comment lines.** A line whose first character, after its leading spaces, is `#` is a comment (TOON spec §5.1, new in spec v4.0). The reader drops it before it looks at anything else: a comment is never a row, a list item or a blank line, it never opens or closes a scope, and it may be indented by any number of spaces. A document of only comments is an empty object. A `#` anywhere else on a line is content, and a line indented with a tab is not a comment. Errors still name the line of the file, comment lines included. json-io never writes a comment. **What can change for text you stored:** json-io through 4.113.0 wrote a string that starts with `#` without quotes. Where that string began its line (the first cell of a table row, or a document that is one such string), the line is now a comment: strict mode fails with a row count mismatch, non-strict mode reads the table without that row, and the one-string document reads as an empty object. Such text has a line matching `^ *#`; read it with json-io 4.113.0 and write it again. Text written by 4.114.0 quotes such a string (next entry). Tests: `ToonCommentLinesTest` (10; 7 fail on the old reader), and 11 spec fixture cases leave `known-gaps.txt` (131 left).
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON writes a string that starts with `#` in quotes.** Since TOON spec v4.0 a line whose first character is `#` is a comment, and a reader drops it. json-io wrote such a string bare, so `#ff0000` in the first column of a table, or as the whole document, started a line: a v4 reader lost that row and then reported the row count wrong. A table now reads `palette[2]{color,name}:` then `"#ff0000",red`. The quotes are written wherever the string stands (field value, inline array, list item, table cell, whole document), as spec §7.2 requires; a `#` that is not first (`a#b`, `issue #12`) stays bare. Every json-io version reads the quoted form. Tests: `ToonHashLeadingStringTest` (10; 7 fail on the old writer), and six spec fixture cases leave `known-gaps.txt` (142 left).
* **TESTING**: **The official TOON spec fixtures move from v3.3 to v4.4.** `toon-spec-fixtures/fixtures` now holds the spec's 564 cases at `toon-format/spec@d09c3d33` (v4.4.0, 2026-10-08), run by `ToonSpecFixtureTest`. json-io does not pass all of them yet: `toon-spec-fixtures/known-gaps.txt` lists the 148 that fail, grouped by spec feature (comment lines, nested field groups, the keyed tabular form, the number grammar, strict and non-strict mode, quoting). A listed case is reported as skipped while it fails and **fails the build once it passes**, so each fix removes its lines; a case that is not listed must pass. A case is identified by its file and position (`decode/comments.json#3`), because the spec rewrites case names. Spec v4.0 removed key folding and path expansion; json-io keeps both as opt-in options (`toonKeyFolding`, `toonExpandPaths`), so v3.3's 24 cases for them stay, under `toon-spec-fixtures/v3-options`.
* **BUILD**: `java-util` 4.113.0 → **4.114.0**, released the same day: json-io depends on the java-util of its own version number. From it the TOON reader uses `FastReader.readLineBorrowedLf` (a line ends at its line feed), and a `MultiKeyMap` written as TOON reads back with its keys and values paired, now that `keySet()` goes through the map in the order `values()` does.

#### 4.113.0 - 2026-10-10
**Upgrading from 4.112.0 -- what can change a result.** Each row is pinned by `UpgradeTo4113NotesTest`; every
example gave the 4.112.0 answer shown. json-io reads and converts through java-util, whose 4.113.0 has its own table
(conversions, dates, zones, the class block list): see java-util's
[changelog](https://github.com/jdereg/java-util/blob/master/changelog.md). The entries below have the details.

| Area | 4.112.0 | 4.113.0 |
|---|---|---|
| Default JSON type info | an `ArrayList` in a `List` field was `{"@type":"ArrayList","@items":[...]}` | `[...]`: the class the reader builds for the declared type is left out (about half the JSON of a typical graph of DTOs). Readers older than 4.88.0 read such elements as maps; `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()` writes 4.112.0's text |
| A JSON string in a `byte[]` field | guessed: `"CAFEBABE"` was hex (4 bytes), `"AQID"` text | Base64 when padded correctly, as Jackson reads it (`"CAFEBABE"` is 6 bytes); otherwise the text's bytes (`"AF"`) |
| A `ByteBuffer` in TOON; a JSON string in a `ByteBuffer` field | TOON wrote its bytes as text and read back every byte from 128 up as two (256 bytes as 512); a JSON string was guessed, as for a `byte[]` (`"AQID"` gave its text, `"CAFEBABE"` hex) | TOON writes Base64, as JSON does; a string is read as Base64 when padded correctly (`"AQID"` is `{1, 2, 3}`), as for a `byte[]` |
| The type asked for at the root | `asType(new TypeHolder<List<Item>>(){})` on plain JSON gave maps | the elements are `Item`s |
| TOON whole doubles | `9.223372036854776E18` was written as `9223372036854775807` (a long's limit) | `9223372036854776000`, the decimal the double prints |
| `Pattern` | its flags were dropped | written inline (`"(?i)abc"`); reads back matching what it matched |
| `BigDecimal` with a huge exponent | `1E+100000` was written as 100,001 digits | written with its exponent |
| A JSON number too large for its field | wrapped silently (`99999999999999999999` into a `long`) | `JsonIoException` |
| A blank string into a `UUID` or an enum | threw | `null` |
| Number text into a `Number` field (or a `T extends Number`) | refused | the number (`"42"` is `42L`) |
| `EnumMap`, `ArrayBlockingQueue`, `AtomicReferenceArray`, `LongAdder`, `InetAddress` | the first three failed to read back, `LongAdder` read back 0, `InetAddress` could not be built | read back (an `ArrayBlockingQueue`'s capacity is its size, as Jackson reads it) |
| `LRUCache`, `TTLCache` | could not be read back | an `LRUCache` reads back with its entries; a `TTLCache` writes its settings and reads back as an empty cache |
| An empty `Optional` at the root of TOON | read back `null` | `Optional.empty()` |
| A shared `Optional` in plain TOON (cycle support on) | read back as unresolved maps | one content, held twice; a TOON root list resolves `$id` and `$ref` as a JSON root array does |
| A map shared between a `Map<String, String>` field and another field, in TOON | the second was written `$ref: 0` and read back empty | written again, as JSON writes it |
| A `StackTraceElement` in a slot that does not declare it | written `"@type":"StackTraceElementElement"` (a slip that doubled its last word, since 2024) | `"StackTraceElement"`; the old name still reads, and `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()` still writes it. A reader of 4.112.0 or earlier knows only the old name. An exception's own stack trace carries no alias either way |
| An `EnumSet` of an enum with more than two fields, in JSON | each item an object of the enum's fields (for a JDK enum such as `TimeUnit` on JDK 17+, whose fields cannot be read, every value `0`) | each item its name, as an enum constant is written anywhere else and as TOON writes it; every reader from 4.50.0 on reads it. `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()` still writes the objects |
| `com.cedarsoftware.io.factory.BitSetFactory` | a public class (nothing reached it) | **removed**: code that names it no longer compiles or loads. A `BitSet` reads through the Converter, and the older index form (`[1,3,5]`) reads again |

Also: json-io reads the aliases of every `config/aliases.txt` on the class path, and writes with the first copy's
(an application's own copy decides), pinned by `AliasesOfEveryCopyAreReadTest`.

* **BUG FIX**: **JSON writes an `EnumSet`'s items as their names.** An enum with more than two fields had each item written as an object of its fields: for a JDK enum such as `TimeUnit`, whose fields cannot be read on JDK 17+, every value was `0` (`"scale":0` for `SECONDS`), followed by its name; for an application's enum, values that go back to the constant's own on reading anyway. Each item is now its name, as an enum constant is written anywhere else and as TOON already wrote an `EnumSet`. Readers of json-io 4.50.0, 4.70.0, 4.80.0, 4.87.0 and 4.112.0 were each run on the new form and read it. `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()`, which keeps 4.112.0's text byte for byte, still writes the objects, and those still read.
  * **Tests.** `EnumSetItemsAreNamesTest` (2): `TimeUnit`, an application enum with fields, and an `EnumSet` in an `Object` field, written as names in three modes and read back (fails on the previous code); the legacy text keeps the objects and they read. Mutants (legacy text always, never) each fail it; the 9 existing `EnumSet` test classes pass unchanged.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `StackTraceElement`'s alias is `StackTraceElement`, not `StackTraceElementElement`.** The doubled name came in with the 2024 move of every alias to `config/aliases.txt` and was written wherever a `StackTraceElement` sat in a slot that does not declare it (an `Object` field, a raw list); an exception's own stack trace never carries it. The old name is still read -- JSON and TOON written by 4.112.0 and earlier -- as a read-only alias, and `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()`, which keeps 4.112.0's text byte for byte, still writes it unless the application chose its own alias. A json-io reader of 4.112.0 or earlier knows only the old name.
  * **Tests.** `StackTraceElementAliasTest` (4): written as `StackTraceElement` (and `StackTraceElement[]`) in JSON and TOON in three modes, read back; the old name reads in JSON and TOON; legacy mode writes the old name; an application's own alias stands. The first fails on the previous code; mutants (old name not read, legacy writing the new name, legacy overriding the application's alias) each fail it.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON writes a `ByteBuffer`'s bytes as Base64, as JSON does, and reads them back.** It wrote them as text -- each byte a character -- and read that text back as UTF-8, so every byte from 128 up came back as two (256 bytes as 512), in every type-info mode (4.112.0 too). A string read into a `ByteBuffer` -- a field, a collection, the root, JSON or TOON -- is now Base64 when padded correctly, by the rule a `byte[]` follows (`ByteUtilities.decodeBase64`); any other string gives its own bytes. Jackson writes a `ByteBuffer` as Base64, so its JSON now reads too.
  * **Tests.** `ToonByteBufferTest` (4): all 256 byte values in a field and a list, in four modes; at the root; only the remaining bytes of a heap or direct buffer, the buffer not moved; a JSON string in a `ByteBuffer` field. All four fail on the previous code; mutants (TOON writing text, no Base64 parse for `ByteBuffer`, the root not decoded) each fail it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A shared `Optional` in plain TOON is written once and read back once, and a TOON root list resolves its `$id`s and `$ref`s as a JSON root array does.**
  * **Writing.** With no type info (TOON's default), an `Optional` is written as its content, in the content's place, so it can carry no `$id`. A shared one threw "Reference tracking failure" when its content came round again (new in this release; 4.112.0 read it back wrong instead). Its content now carries the identity: `$id` where it is first written, `$ref` after.
  * **Reading an `Optional` field.** A `$ref` in an `Optional` field was taken for the content and failed ("Return type mismatch"); it now goes to the reference step, which wraps the content it names, or keeps the `Optional` it names.
  * **Reading a root list.** A TOON root list read as a raw `List` (with no element type, or in Maps mode) resolved its elements only when they named a `$type`: a `$ref` came back an empty map and `$items` unread, so a shared list or map at the top level of plain TOON was lost (4.112.0 too). A root list whose elements carry `$id`, `$ref` or `$items` is now read as JSON reads a root array; one without them reads as before.
  * **Tests.** `ToonSharedReferenceTest` (4 more): a shared `Optional` of a list in a root list (one list, held twice); shared `Optional` fields of a list and of an object, in all five modes (the same content); a root list with a shared list and a shared map read as a raw `List`, with no type, and in Maps mode, element for element as JSON reads the same array; a traced shared list still written once. All four fail on the previous code; mutants (no identity lent, a `$ref` in an `Optional` field taken as content, root lists not resolved) each fail it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A container shared between a field declared with leaf elements and another field is written again in TOON, as JSON writes it -- not refused, and not referenced as `$ref: 0`.** The reference pass skips a container declared with leaf elements (`List<String>`, `Map<String, String>`), as JSON's does, so it never gave such a container an id. When another field held the same container, TOON threw "Reference tracking failure" (a list, under `showTypeInfoAlways()`; 4.112.0 also under MinimalPlus) or wrote `$ref: 0`, a reference to nothing, which read back as an empty map (a map, in every mode, 4.112.0 too). A value written already that has no id is now written again, in all five places TOON checks for one.
  * **Tests.** `ToonSharedReferenceTest` (2): a `List<String>` and a `Map<String, String>` held by another field, and the map also inside a `List<Object>`, in all five type-info modes: no `$ref`, read back equal. Both fail on the previous writer; mutants (the reference check reverted in `writeReferenceIfSeen`, `writeMap`, `writeMapInline`) each fail it. A corpus of 60 TOON outputs (10 graphs x 6 option sets) is byte-identical to before except four that threw.
* **DOCS**: **README ratio table re-measured, all three columns**, after the exact TOON type rule: `JsonPerformanceTest --with-gson`, median of three runs, JDK 21.0.12, `jackson-databind 2.22.3`, `gson 2.14.0`. These replace the figures in the "README performance figures re-measured" entry below.

  | Mode (ratio to Jackson) | JsonIo | TOON | Gson |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Typed read | 1.61 -> 1.59x | 1.63 -> 1.55x | 1.44 -> 1.42x |
  | `toMaps` read | 1.30 -> 1.29x | 1.55 -> 1.27x | 1.42 -> 1.45x |
  | Write, `cycleSupport=true` | 1.59 -> 1.52x | 1.36 -> 1.46x | 2.18 -> 2.16x |
  | Write, `cycleSupport=false` | 1.45 -> 1.39x | 1.25 -> 1.36x | 2.18 -> 2.16x |
  | `toMaps` write, `cycleSupport=true` | 1.55 -> 1.53x | 1.37 -> 1.49x | 2.16 -> 2.18x |
  | `toMaps` write, `cycleSupport=false` | 1.42 -> 1.38x | 1.27 -> 1.38x | 2.16 -> 2.18x |

  TOON reads got faster; typed TOON writes cost more (they write 19% less text, and check every map's keys). Derived text updated: json-io 1.3-1.6x Jackson (was 1.3-1.8x), Gson 1.4-2.2x (was 1.4-2.1x); typed reads about 12% longer than Gson (was 10%); `cycleSupport(false)` 9% faster for typed writes and 10% in `toMaps` mode.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **TOON with type info writes about 3% faster** (`showTypeInfoMinimalPlus()` on `JsonPerformanceTest`'s data: 34.7 -> 33.8 us on JDK 21, 37.1 -> 36.1 us on JDK 17, fresh JVMs, medians of 5). Two checks the new type rule added per value are cut short where their answer is already known: a value declared `Object` (a map's value, a raw list's element) is not looked up for a natural default or an enum, which `Object` never has; and a list's scalar element is not asked whether it holds typed content, which only an object written in its place (an `Optional`) can. Same output: a corpus of ten graphs written under all six option sets (Never, Minimal, MinimalPlus, Always, MinimalLegacy, MinimalPlus without cycle support) is identical before and after, 60 outputs, and three mutants of the changed checks each change it. The rest of the cost against 4.112.0 (32.0 us on JDK 21, with 19% more text) is mostly the scan of each map's keys, which finds a non-String key in a `Map<String, ...>` -- generics are not enforced at runtime -- and which JSON's writer also does.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **The default type info is smaller, and still reads back exactly; `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()` writes what it wrote before.** `showTypeInfoMinimal()`, the default, no longer writes `@type` on a List, Set or Map -- or an element of one -- whose class is the one the reader builds for its declared type (its natural default: `ArrayList` for `List`, `LinkedHashSet` for `Set`, `LinkedHashMap` for `Map`, `TreeSet` for `SortedSet`, ...): an `ArrayList` in a `List` field is `[...]`, not `{"@type":"ArrayList","@items":[...]}`.
  * **Size.** 50 DTOs with List/Set/Map fields: 6,481 bytes instead of 13,086 (-50.5%), 2,233 tokens instead of 3,999; 50 orders with a `Map<String, Object>` each -23.3%; a `Map<String, Object>` of ints, strings and booleans 0%.
  * **Exact.** Every value reads back as the class it was: this is what `showTypeInfoMinimalPlus()` already left off, without its lossy part.
  * **Older readers.** A json-io reader from 4.88.0 on reads it exactly. One older than 4.88.0 reads a POJO element of a generic List or Map field back as a map, without an error (measured with released 4.50.0 - 4.87.0). For one, or for JSON text that must stay byte for byte what it was because it is hashed or compared, use `showTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()` (`WriteOptions.ShowType.MINIMAL_LEGACY`, `isMinimalLegacyShowingType()`, `addPermanentShowTypeInfoMinimalLegacy()`, Spring `show-type-info: MINIMAL_LEGACY`); readers 4.50.0 - 4.88.0 read its output exactly. TOON writes it as minimal.
  * **`showTypeInfoMinimalPlus()`** is unchanged: minimal, and also lossy where values sit in `Object` slots (an `int` in a `Map<String, Object>` reads back as a `Long`; a `Timestamp` in a `Date` field as a `Date`) -- smaller there (-41.8% on that map of ints). It stays the Spring Boot starter's default, for clients that are not json-io.
  * **Tests.** `MinimalLegacyTest` (4): legacy output is the text json-io 4.112.0 wrote, run on that release; the default's text, and the classes read back; minimal plus reads back a Long; legacy is a minimal mode (root type, TOON, the permanent setting).
