With every `showTypeInfoMinimal()` and the default routed to legacy, the whole suite passes unchanged (4,858 + 165 + 28 tests); with the new default 3 tests that pinned the old text change: `PrettyPrintTest`'s fixture, and two `CompactFormatTest` comparisons, which now compare against legacy. Mutants: legacy with natural defaults fails 2, minimal without them 1. README: an upgrade note.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `Charset` could be written but not read back.** A `Charset`'s runtime class is a JDK-internal subclass (`sun.nio.cs.UTF_8`, `sun.nio.cs.US_ASCII`, ...), and json-io wrote it as an ordinary object of that class — `{"@type":"sun.nio.cs.UTF_8","name":null,"aliases":null,...}` — which no JDK 16+ can instantiate (its package is not exported), so reading failed wherever a `Charset` appeared: an `Object`-typed field, a collection, the top level. It is now written like `Path`, as its canonical name under the public type: `{"@type":"Charset","value":"UTF-8"}`, whatever the implementation class (new `Writers.CharsetWriter`, plus the `Charset` alias and non-reference entries), and read back through java-util 4.113.0's new `String` → `Charset` conversion. JSON also stops embedding JDK-internal class names, which differ between JDK versions and vendors. `CharsetRoundTripTest` (5): `Object`- and `Charset`-typed fields, a list of charsets, the top level, the exact JSON written, and a TOON round trip.
* **BUG FIX**: **A strict-TOON object with more fields than the lookup threshold hid its later fields from `get()`.** Past the linear-search threshold (8 by default through `ReadOptions`), `JsonObject` finds keys through a hash index built on the first lookup. The parser's fast append, `appendFieldForParser()`, never added to that index, which was harmless while no lookup had built it yet. But strict TOON checks every key for a duplicate before appending it, so the index was built as soon as an object passed the threshold, and every later field was invisible to `get()` and `containsKey()` while `keySet()` still listed it. A `ClassFactory` reading a 12-field strict-TOON object by name got `null` for fields 10 to 12, and any other reader of the `JsonObject` saw the same. The fast append now adds each key to the index when one exists. It also clears the cached `hashCode()`, which it had left stale. `JsonObjectAppendIndexTest` (4), all failing against the previous code: a field appended after a lookup is found, every key `keySet()` lists can be looked up, the cached hash follows an append, and a `ClassFactory` sees every field of a strict-TOON object.
* **BUG FIX**: **`JsonObject.entrySet()` hands out a separate entry for each field.** Its iterator returned one `Map.Entry`, re-pointed at the next field on every `next()`. Code that reads each entry before advancing never noticed. Anything that keeps entries did: a `List` of them, `toArray()`, `Map.copyOf`, a sorted or collected stream all ended up holding one field N times (a sorted stream of `{a=1, b=2, c=3}` came back as `[a=1, a=1, a=1]`), and `setValue()` on an entry kept from earlier wrote to whichever field the iterator had reached. That breaks the `Map.Entry` and `Set` contracts. The JDK fixed the same pattern in `EnumMap` and `IdentityHashMap` as a bug (JDK-6312706). It reached code handed a `JsonObject`: a `ClassFactory`, a `JsonClassReader`, and java-util's `ClassUtilities`, which built an object from a `JsonObject` keyed `arg0`, `arg2` as `("c", "c")` where a `LinkedHashMap` gives `("a", "c")`. `JsonObjectMap` already allocated an entry per `next()`. Each entry of a plain `JsonObject` now reads and writes through to it and prints as `key=value`. json-io's own hot loops use the allocation-free `fastKeyAt`/`fastValueAt` and are unaffected. `JsonObjectEntrySetTest` (8): 7 fail against the previous code (entries kept in a list, a sorted stream, `toArray()`, `setValue()` on an earlier entry, the `Map.Entry` contract, a copy through the entry set, `ClassUtilities` with gapped keys); the eighth, `setValue()` writing through and clearing the cached hash, passes on both.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **Maps-mode reads no longer allocate a `Map.Entry` per field.** `MapResolver.traverseFields`, the loop that runs for every object a Maps-mode read builds, walked `jsonObj.entrySet()` and wrote back through `entry.setValue()`. Once `entrySet()` began handing out a real entry per field (above), the JIT could not eliminate those entries in a loop this size. The JDK's allocation profiler put them at 2.8% of all bytes allocated in `JsonPerformanceTest`'s Maps mode, and Maps-mode read was 3-4% slower (every such run slower than every baseline run). The loop now goes by index through the allocation-free accessors json-io's writer already uses (`fastEntryCount`/`fastKeyAt`/`fastValueAt`), plus a new package-private `fastSetValueAt`, which `JsonObjectMap` overrides alongside the others. Maps-mode read is back to its baseline (json-io/Jackson 1.26x before and after, interleaved median of 3), and a Maps-mode read of 100 objects allocates 132.7 KB instead of 154.3 KB before these `JsonObject` changes. The existing tests cover the change: a `fastSetValueAt` that drops its write fails 34 of them across 13 classes.
* **BUG FIX**: **A key a document repeats now reads as its last value everywhere.** For `{"k":"first","k":"second"}`, Maps mode and field injection already ended with `"second"`, but `JsonObject.get("k")` returned `"first"` when the object had no more fields than the lookup threshold (a linear scan from the front) and `"second"` above it (the hash index keeps the last). So a `ClassFactory`, a `JsonClassReader` or a constructor matched by parameter name could see either value depending on the object's size. The linear scan now runs from the end, so every lookup (`get`, `containsKey`, `put`) finds the last value at every size, as JavaScript's `JSON.parse` does. `remove()` takes every copy of the key, so removing it cannot bring back an earlier value. The parser still keeps each copy, so `keySet()` and `size()` list a repeated key as often as the document repeats it. De-duplicating while parsing was built and measured: it cost 5-9% of read time for keys that almost never repeat. RFC 8259 leaves repeated names to each implementation. The `ToonReader` comment that said the append "already overwrites the prior value" now says what happens. `JsonObjectRepeatedKeyTest` (6): 4 fail against the previous code (`get` at every size, `put`, `remove` taking every copy, a `ClassFactory` reading the last value); 2 pin what already held (a constructor, field injection and Maps mode reading the last value, and non-strict TOON).
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`JsonObject`'s lookup index is a compact `int[]` table instead of a `HashMap<Object, Integer>`.** Past the linear-search threshold (8 fields through `ReadOptions`), the first `get()`/`containsKey()` builds a hash index, which costs a node per field and often a boxed `Integer`. An object that was looked up therefore paid for its arrays and a whole `HashMap`, and at 10-22 fields a looked-up `JsonObject` was slower than a `LinkedHashMap` (97 vs 74 ns at 10 fields to append, walk and look up 3 keys). The index is now open addressing over positions: an `int` per slot, found by Fibonacci hashing and linear probing, in a power-of-two table kept at most half full. Plain low-bit hashing clustered on names like `field0`..`field999` and was slower than the `HashMap` at 1,000 fields; Fibonacci hashing spreads them. A repeated key still resolves to its last position. Append, walk and look up 3 keys, JDK 21, previous index → compact → `LinkedHashMap`: 10 fields **100 → 70 ns** (74), 22 fields **223 → 179 ns** (204), 100 fields **938 → 763 ns** (1,207), 1,000 fields **9.7 → 7.3 µs** (13.1), using about half the old memory at every size. Without lookups the parallel arrays beat `LinkedHashMap` by 1.9x to 4.5x, which is why `JsonObject` is not simply a `LinkedHashMap`. Reading 100 objects of a 12-field class built through its constructor (every parameter looked up by name) is **~9% faster and allocates 36% less** (120 KB → 76 KB per read). `JsonPerformanceTest` modes, which rarely look fields up, are unchanged (interleaved median of 3). `JsonObjectIndexTest` (6), passing on both indexes: keys with equal hash codes, growth to 2,000 keys, the threshold crossed by `put`, a repeated key, a null key, and 20,000 random appends, puts, removes and lookups checked against a `LinkedHashMap` (fixed seed). A probing bug planted in the new index fails 3 of them.
* **BUG FIX**: **`JsonObject`'s views follow the `Map` contract.** `values()` extended `AbstractSet`, so it claimed to be a `Set` although values repeat (`{a=1, b=2, c=1}` has three), and it compared equal to any `Set` holding the same values, which `HashMap.values()` does not. It is now a plain `Collection`. On a complex-keyed map (`@keys`/`@items`), `entrySet()` handed out detached `AbstractMap.SimpleEntry` copies, so `setValue()` changed the copy and left the map as it was. Both shapes now share one entry type that reads and writes through the fast accessors each shape overrides, clearing the cached hash on a write. `JsonObjectViewsTest` (4): 3 fail against the previous code (`values()` a `Set`, `values()` equal to a `Set`, a complex-keyed `setValue()` lost); the fourth, the `Map.Entry` contract for complex-keyed entries, passes on both.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **`ReadOptions` and `WriteOptions` no longer use `ClassValueMap` or `ClassValueSet`: building options is cheaper, and reads and writes are a little faster.** Every build copied the `BASE_*` class settings (about 150 entries for writing, 220 for reading) into new `ClassValueMap`s and `ClassValueSet`s. java-util 4.113.0's `ClassValueMap` no longer keeps its keys' classes alive, so it now attaches a slot to each class as the class is added. That made each copied entry cost ~250 ns: `WriteOptions` took ~37 µs to build and `ReadOptions` ~65 µs, up from 12 µs and 17 µs.
  * **The settings** (custom readers and writers, class factories, coerced types, non-referenceable and not-custom classes, included, excluded and not-imported fields, non-standard getters and setters) are plain concurrent maps and sets. json-io only reads them the first time it meets a class.
  * **The per-object caches** are `ClassValue`s: writers, readers, custom-writer gates, write-field plans, injectors, injector plans and instantiation plans.
  * **Building options:** `WriteOptions` ~11 µs, `ReadOptions` ~14 µs, and creating a `WriteOptions` then writing a small map ~16 µs (it was 20 µs).
  * **`JsonPerformanceTest`'s workloads, each alone in a fresh JVM (3 forks):** writing with cycle support 37.2 → 35.5 µs, without 34.0 → 33.1 µs, and reading 69.8 → 69.0 µs.
* **BUG FIX**: **A field one `ReadOptions` does not import is no longer missing for every other `ReadOptions`.** A class's fields and injectors are worked out from one `ReadOptions`' settings: fields not imported, excluded fields, field filters and injector factories. They were nonetheless cached in static caches shared by every `ReadOptions`, so whichever `ReadOptions` read a class first decided its fields for all of them. After `new ReadOptionsBuilder().addNotImportedField(Foo.class, "b")` read a `Foo`, field `b` was dropped by every `ReadOptions` in the JVM, including ones built later: `build()` cleared the shared injectors but never the fields. The reverse also held, with a class read first by other options ignoring the `addNotImportedField`. Both caches now belong to each `ReadOptions`. `ReadOptionsFieldCachesArePerOptionsTest` (2) checks both orders, and both fail against the previous code.
* **PERFORMANCE / BUG FIX (memory)**: **json-io uses no `ClassValueMap` or `ClassValueSet`, and what it works out about a class no longer keeps the class, or its `ClassLoader`, alive.** Writing or reading a class through the default options, which live for the life of the JVM, kept it in memory: a Groovy script n-cube recompiled, a plugin's class, a redeployed webapp's class, along with its loader and every class that loader loaded.
  * **Fixed tables of JDK classes** are filled once and read through `ClassValue`s: scalar and numeric kinds, primitive-array writers, default instantiators, natural defaults, java.time parsers, and the primitive and native-JSON sets. With 40 other `ClassValue`s on the same classes a lookup stays at ~2.2 ns, against ~2.9 ns for a `HashMap`.
  * **Caches filled for any class** are `ClassValue`s:
    * Annotation metadata. It uses a slot per class, so its scan-cycle guard still returns `EMPTY` without caching it.
    * Custom-writer dispatch.
    * `ReadOptions`' class-factory cache. It was a `ConcurrentHashMap`, which held every class it saw.
  * **`WriteOptions`' field and accessor caches** were `LRUCache`s sized by `lruSize`: bounded, but holding their classes until evicted, and writing on every hit. They are `ClassValue`s now. `lruSize` is kept for compatibility and no longer bounds anything; it already bounded nothing on the read side.
  * **Speed, fresh JVMs, 3 forks each, together with java-util's leak-free `TypeUtilities`:** writing with cycle support 36.3 → 35.8 µs, without 33.3 → 32.5 µs, reading 71.0 → 68.0 µs.
  * **Tests.** `ClassLoaderLeakTest` (1) writes and reads a class of a throwaway loader through the default options and checks the loader is collected. It fails against the previous code. Full suite: 4,696 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **Building options no longer copies the 700 permanent aliases.** Every `ReadOptionsBuilder` and `WriteOptionsBuilder` copied them into its own map. Builders now share one unmodifiable snapshot and copy it only if they change their aliases. The snapshot is rebuilt when a permanent alias is added or removed, under the same lock as the change.
  * **What each options object sees is unchanged:**
    * Its own alias changes stay its own.
    * A permanent alias reaches the builders created after it, not one already started.
    * Built options refuse changes.
    * A copy starts from the original's aliases without changing them.
  * **Building options, with the rest of this release's changes:** `WriteOptions` 11.4 → 3.8 µs and `ReadOptions` 15.1 → 5.8 µs; they were 12 and 17 µs in 4.112.0. Creating a `WriteOptions` then writing a small map takes 18.4 → ~11.6 µs.
  * **Tests.** `AliasesSharedUntilChangedTest` (4) pins those rules. It passes against the previous code and fails if a permanent alias leaves a stale snapshot. Full suite: 4,700 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `ReadOptionsBuilder` copied from a `ReadOptions` no longer changes that `ReadOptions`.** `new ReadOptionsBuilder(existing)` shared the original's conversion settings, so `setZoneId()`, `setLocale()`, `setCharset()`, `classLoader()`, `setTrueCharacter()` and `setFalseCharacter()` on the copy changed the original too, even when the copy was never built. For a copy of `ReadOptionsBuilder.getDefaultReadOptions()`, that changed every read in the JVM that passes `null` options. `addConverterOverride()` on such a copy threw `UnsupportedOperationException`, because the original's overrides are unmodifiable once built. The copy now shares the settings only until it changes one, and copies them then.
  * **Tests.** `ReadOptionsCopyKeepsOriginalTest` (4); all 4 fail against the previous code, each on the bug it targets. Full suite: 4,704 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX (memory)**: **`toMaps()` and `fromToonToMaps()` no longer keep every `ReadOptions` they are given.** They made a Maps-mode copy of the caller's `ReadOptions` and cached it in a static `ConcurrentHashMap`, keyed by that `ReadOptions`, that never let go. An application that builds a `ReadOptions` per call kept both objects for the life of the JVM (about 25 KB per call: 20,000 calls held about 500 MB), along with anything they name, such as a class loader, custom readers and class factories. The Maps-mode copy is now made once and kept on the `ReadOptions` itself, so it goes when that `ReadOptions` goes. A `ReadOptions` that already returns JSON objects is used as is instead of copied.
  * **Tests.** `ClassLoaderLeakTest` also checks that a `ReadOptions` built for one `toMaps()` call is collected afterwards; that check fails against the previous code. The test's fixture must stay the first nested class the test file mentions: on JDK 8, asking a class for its enclosing class resolves the outer class of each nested class listed before it, and the throwaway loader cannot see json-io's classes. Full suite: 4,704 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **Every read with one `ReadOptions` shares one `Converter`.** A `Converter` was created for every read, which costs an allocation and a copy of the converter overrides, although `ReadOptions` cannot change once built and are meant to be built once and reused. It is now created on first use and kept on the `ReadOptions`.
  * **Behavior change:** a conversion added through `Resolver.getConverter().addConversion()` from a custom reader or class factory now stays for later reads with the same `ReadOptions`, on every thread. Use `ReadOptionsBuilder.addConverterOverride()` to customize conversions.
  * **Speed**, small reads with one `ReadOptions`, fresh JVMs, 3 forks each: a two-field `Map` 714 → 611 ns, a three-field object 666 → 626 ns, `toMaps()` 619 → 589 ns; about 430 fewer bytes per read. `JsonPerformanceTest`'s workloads are unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `ReadOptionsSharedConverterTest` (2) checks that reads with one `ReadOptions` get one `Converter`, including 1,600 reads on 8 threads, and that two `ReadOptions` get different ones. Both tests fail against the previous code. Full suite: 4,706 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **TEST**: **json-io's suite runs clean on a JDK 8 runtime: 138 failures to none.** 133 came from one test. `WriteOptionsTest.testClassLoader` passes class loaders as `@ParameterizedTest` arguments, and JUnit closes every `AutoCloseable` argument after the invocation. On JDK 8 the system class loader is a `URLClassLoader`, and once closed it loads no further classes. Every later test that needed a class not yet loaded then failed with `NoClassDefFoundError` or a missing resource, and run alone the test crashed the forked JVM. The test now sets `autoCloseArguments = false`. The two VarHandle tests now run on JDK 9+ only, since VarHandle arrived in JDK 9. The other three failures were json-io bugs, fixed below. Full suite: 4,706 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **A null in an EnumSet's `@items` is reported as one.** It surfaced as a `NullPointerException` from `EnumSetFactory`, wrapped in a `JsonIoException` whose message was JDK 14+'s helpful NPE text, or, on earlier JDKs, nothing at all. It is now `An EnumSet cannot hold null, but the @items of an EnumSet of <enum> contain a null`. `EnumSetErrorTest` checks the message, which the previous code fails on every JDK. Full suite: 4,706 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX (JDK 8)**: **A setter that takes a non-public type no longer fails on JDK 8.** Without `privateLookupIn` (JDK 9+), the setter lambda `Injector` builds has only json-io's own access. It was declined when the setter's class was not public, but not when the value type was not, so `setA(PackagePrivateType)` on a public class failed at the first read with `IllegalAccessError` ("Unable to set field: a using setA"). Now every class in the setter's signature must be public, array element classes included; otherwise the method handle is used. `TemplateFieldsTest.test3TypeGeneric` failed on JDK 8 and now passes. Full suite: 4,706 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **Cycles and shared objects through an `Optional` work, on every JDK and in TOON.** An `Optional` is written as its content, but the writer traced it through its fields, which `java.util` hides on JDK 9+:
  * **A cycle through an `Optional`** (a parent holding an `Optional<Child>` whose child points back) recursed until `StackOverflowError` on JDK 9+, even with `cycleSupport(true)`. The released 4.112.0 did the same.
  * **An object shared through two `Optional`s** was written twice on JDK 9+ and read back as two objects.
  * **On JDK 8**, where the fields are visible, the second occurrence was written as `@ref` and the read failed ("Unsupported conversion ... target type 'Optional'"), because the reader put the bare content into the `Optional` field. A forward `@ref` failed that way on every JDK.
  * **TOON** wrote an `Optional` holding an object or a list on the key's line, so the content's fields landed at the parent's level and the document read back wrong, with or without a cycle.
  * **Now:** both writers trace the content; the reader wraps a `@ref` in an `Optional` field, whether it resolves at once or later; and TOON writes the content as the field's own value. JDK 8 and JDK 21 write the same JSON.
  * **Tests.** `OptionalSharedAndCyclicTest` (4); all 4 fail against the previous code. `OptionalPrimitiveVariantsTest.sharedObjectInOptional_cycleSupportEnabled` failed on JDK 8 and now passes. `JsonPerformanceTest`'s workloads are unchanged. Full suite: 4,710 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `AtomicReference` keeps its content on JDK 9+.** The content lives in a private field that `java.util.concurrent.atomic` does not open, and json-io writes a JDK field it cannot reach as `null`. The content was therefore written as `"value":null`, and reading it back failed. It is now written through `get()` and read through `set()` (`nonStandardGetters.txt`, `nonStandardSetters.txt`), as it effectively was on JDK 8, including shared objects and cycles through it. `AtomicReferenceRoundTripTest` (3); all 3 fail against the previous code on JDK 21. Full suite: 4,713 + 165 + 28 tests on JDK 21, and 4,713 on JDK 8, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **`Map.of(...)`, `EnumMap` and `ArrayBlockingQueue` now read back, as Jackson reads them.**
  * **`Map.of(...)`:** its own classes (`ImmutableCollections$Map1`, `$MapN`) had no factory, unlike those of `List.of` and `Set.of`, so reading one failed with "Unable to instantiate". They now read back as an unmodifiable map, as `List.of` does, and are aliased `Map1` / `MapN`.
  * **`EnumMap`:** failed to read on every JDK, both at the root and in a field ("JsonObject cannot be cast to String"). The new `EnumMapFactory` takes the enum class from the declared type, or from the first key json-io wrote. Plain string keys (`{"RED":…}`, as Jackson writes them) read into a field declared `EnumMap<Color, V>`. An empty `EnumMap` read with no declared type names no enum class, so it fails with a message that says so.
  * **`ArrayBlockingQueue`:** it has no no-argument constructor, so reading one failed. It now reads back with its items, in a queue as large as its items (at least 1), as Jackson does. Its capacity is not written.
  * **Tests.** `JDK9ImmutableTest.testMapOfRoundTrips`, `EnumMapRoundTripTest` (4) and `ArrayBlockingQueueRoundTripTest` (2); each fails without its fix. Full suite: 4,720 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **`java.sql.Time`, `InetAddress`, `LongAdder` and `DoubleAdder` round-trip**, carried by java-util 4.113.0's new conversions for them:
  * **`java.sql.Time`** read back as a `java.util.Date`. It is still written as epoch milliseconds, and it reads back as a `Time`. Jackson's `"13:14:15"` reads too.
  * **`InetAddress`** was written as an empty object on JDK 9+, and could not be read back on any JDK. It is now written as its IP address, preceded by the host name it already carries (`"build-server/10.0.0.7"`). It is read back without any DNS lookup: a bare host name is rejected, not resolved. Jackson resolves a host name when reading.
  * **`LongAdder` and `DoubleAdder`** were written as empty objects on every JDK, losing the count. They are now written as their sum, as `AtomicLong` is, and are treated as values (no `@id`), also as `AtomicLong` is.
  * **Tests.** `JdkValueTypesRoundTripTest` (3); against the previous code and java-util, the `InetAddress` and adder tests fail. Full suite: 4,723 + 165 + 28 tests on JDK 21 and 4,723 on JDK 8, 0 failures.
* **DOCS**: **User guide: "JDK Types Compared with Jackson".** It reports a survey of 104 JDK values written and read back as their declared types. json-io reads back 97 intact on JDK 21 (90 before this release) and 100 on JDK 8 (94 before). Jackson 2.22 with its `jdk8` and `JavaTimeModule` modules reads back 84, and so does Jackson 3.1. No type in the set reads back intact with Jackson but not with json-io. The section lists what json-io keeps that Jackson loses, and what neither keeps, with workarounds. The README's supported-types table adds `LongAdder`, `DoubleAdder`, `java.sql.Time`, `InetAddress` and `EnumMap`, and links the section.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `WriteOptionsBuilder` no longer changes other `WriteOptions`.** A builder's per-class settings start as shallow copies, of the permanent ones or of the options it was copied from, so the field-name sets and getter maps inside them were shared. `addExcludedFields()` (plural), `addIncludedField(s)()` and `addNonStandardGetter()` added to those shared sets:
  * **Exceptions lost their message:** excluding `Throwable`'s `detailMessage` on one builder made every `WriteOptions` built afterwards, including the default one, write exceptions without their message.
  * **Copies changed their original:** adding an included field to a copy added it to the original.
  * **The fix:** these methods now copy the set or map before adding, as `addExcludedField()` already did.
  * **`getIncludedFields()` and `getExcludedFields()`** now return an empty set for a class with none configured. They threw `NullPointerException`.
  * **Tests.** `WriteOptionsBuilderOwnSettingsTest` (3); all 3 fail against the previous code. Full suite: 4,726 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures.
* **PERFORMANCE / MEMORY**: **Builders share the permanent class settings instead of copying them. Building options is about 10x faster, and each `ReadOptions` or `WriteOptions` is 85–88% smaller.**
  * **What was copied:** every builder copied the permanent custom readers and writers, class factories, coerced types, non-referenceable and not-custom classes, excluded and not-imported fields, non-standard getters, and field and method filters.
  * **What happens now:** builders share read-only snapshots (`PermanentSetting`), and a builder copies a setting only when it changes it, as with the aliases.
  * **Size per instance:** a `ReadOptions` 11.9 → 1.7 KB, a `WriteOptions` 9.2 → 1.1 KB.
  * **Speed** (fresh JVMs, 3 forks):

    | | Before | After |
    |---|---|---|
    | `ReadOptionsBuilder().build()` | 6.6 µs | 0.6 µs |
    | `WriteOptionsBuilder().build()` | 3.9 µs | 0.4 µs |
    | Create a `WriteOptions` and write a small map | 11.6 µs | 6.7 µs |

    `JsonPerformanceTest`'s workloads are unchanged (within 2%).
  * **Unchanged behavior:**
    * A builder's changes stay its own.
    * A permanent change reaches the builders created after it, never a builder already started or options already built. Snapshots are deep, so the per-class field sets are copied too.
    * Built options refuse changes, including changes through their builder.
    * A copy starts from the original's settings without changing them.
  * **Tests.** `OptionsShareClassSettingsTest` (5). The 2 that check sharing fail against the previous code; the 3 that check behavior pass on both. Full suite: 4,731 + 165 + 28 tests on JDK 21 and 4,731 on JDK 8, 0 failures.
* **PERFORMANCE**: **A small read is 2.2–2.5x faster and allocates about a quarter as much.** The tokenizer's string cache is now lent per thread instead of made for every parse.
  * **The cause:** each parse allocated and zeroed the cache's 2,048-slot array (8 KB), which was most of the cost of reading a small document.
  * **What lends it:** the per-thread buffer recycler that already lends the reader buffers, for String and InputStream reads, and for the streaming tokenizer until it is closed. A read within a read gets its own.
  * **Earlier documents' strings:** a lent cache keeps strings of 64 characters or fewer from earlier documents, as ToonReader's cache does, and as the recycled reader buffer already keeps their text. A hit is always checked against the text, so a cached string never stands in for a different one. Repeated field names now hit across documents.
  * **Speed**, small reads with one `ReadOptions`, fresh JVMs, 3 forks:

    | | Before | After |
    |---|---|---|
    | Two-field `Map` | 612 ns | 281 ns |
    | Three-field object | 622 ns | 251 ns |
    | `toMaps()` | 589 ns | 231 ns |
    | Allocated per read | 10.4–10.8 KB | 2.0–2.5 KB |

    `JsonPerformanceTest`'s read is unchanged.
  * **Tests.** `StringCachePooledTest` (3). The allocation check fails against the previous code; the two that check stale entries and a read within a read pass on both. Full suite: 4,734 + 165 + 28 tests on JDK 21 and 4,734 on JDK 8, 0 failures.
* **BUG FIX**: **An object of a class declared inside an enum is written and read back.** For example, `enum Status { …; public static class Detail { … } }`. java-util's `getClassIfEnum()` reported such a class as its enclosing enum. json-io therefore chose the enum writer for it and failed ("Status$Detail cannot be cast to java.lang.Enum") on every JDK. Fixed in java-util 4.113.0. Enum constants with bodies, and enums nested in enums, still read and write as enums.
  * **Tests.** `EnumNestedClassTest` (1). Full suite: 4,735 + 165 + 28 tests on JDK 21 and 4,735 on JDK 8, 0 failures.
* **DOCS**: **README performance figures re-measured** with `JsonPerformanceTest --with-gson`, median of three runs, on JDK 21.0.12 with `jackson-databind 2.22.3` and `gson 2.14.0`. Ratios to Jackson:

  | Mode | Before (4.106.0) | Now |
  |---|---|---|
  | Typed read | 1.64x | 1.61x |
  | `toMaps` read | 1.32x | 1.30x |
  | Write, `cycleSupport=true` | 1.57x | 1.59x |
  | Write, `cycleSupport=false` | 1.40x | 1.45x |
  | `toMaps` write, `cycleSupport=true` | 1.65x | 1.55x |
  | `toMaps` write, `cycleSupport=false` | 1.38x | 1.42x |

  * **Measured twice.** The first measurement (before this release's faster write) put `cycleSupport=true` writes at 1.66x and `toMaps` writes at 1.59x and 1.45x; it was taken again after that change, and the figures above and in the README are the second.
  * **Against the released 4.112.0**, same machine and libraries, each workload alone in a fresh JVM, eight rounds: writes are 5.1% faster with `cycleSupport=true` and 5.6% without, and typed reads 4.2% faster, in every round (JDK 8: writes 3.0% and 1.5% faster). Jackson moved by 1% or less.
  * **Gson claim corrected:** the README said json-io was "roughly tied" with Gson on reads. It is faster on `toMaps` reads, and takes about 10% longer on typed reads.
  * **`cycleSupport(false)` tip updated:** the gain is now about 10%, for typed writes and in `toMaps` mode, since writes with cycle support got faster.
  * **Options-building tip corrected:** building options takes well under a microsecond.
* **BUG FIX**: **Lenient type matching never accepts an array for another array type.** When a value can't be converted to the requested type, json-io accepts a complex object that shares a meaningful common ancestor with that type: a `Car` where a `Motorcycle` is asked for, since both are `Vehicle`s.
  * **The defect:** java-util 4.113.0's class hierarchy follows the Java Language Specification. So two arrays of objects now share `Object[]` and `Serializable[]`, which the check did not skip, and an array of `Car`s shares `Vehicle[]` with an array of `Motorcycle`s. Either was then accepted as the other, and the caller got an array of the wrong type.
  * **Now:** an array is never accepted here, as before the hierarchy changed.
  * **Tests.** `ResolverValueToTargetTest.toJava_lenientModeRefusesAnArrayOfAnotherType` fails without this change (against java-util 4.113.0). Full suite: 4,736 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21. The core module passes on JDK 8 as well (4,736 tests, 0 failures).
* **PERFORMANCE**: **Writing is 2–3% faster: `Primitives` tests its two small fixed sets by identity instead of through `ClassValue`s.** `isNativeJsonType()`, asked for each element and field value the writer emits, and `isPrimitive()` read two `ClassValue`s over `HashSet`s. A hot JDK class carries many `ClassValue`s once json-io and java-util have seen it: `String` 15, `HashMap` 26 after one read and one write. The JDK keeps each class's `ClassValue`s in a small cache, and lookups that miss it take a slower path, which JFR showed on these calls. Each set is eight classes, now compared by identity in rough order of frequency. The native JSON types are `String`, `Long`, `Double`, `Boolean`, `Object[]` and `long`, `double`, `boolean`; the wrappers are the eight primitive wrappers. Two `ClassValue`s, two `HashSet`s and a static initializer went with them.
  * **Speed**, `JsonPerformanceTest`'s workload, interleaved fresh JVMs: writing with `cycleSupport=true` went from 34.0–34.4 µs to 33.0–33.4 µs over four rounds, and with `cycleSupport=false` from 31.6–32.4 µs to 30.9–31.7 µs over ten, faster in every round. Reading is unchanged.
  * **Not done:** folding the writer's other per-class lookups into one plan per class also measured within noise (1% at best) and was dropped.
  * **Tests.** `PrimitivesTest.theWrapperAndNativeJsonTypeSetsAreExactlyTheseClasses` names every member of both sets and their near misses: `Integer` is a wrapper but not a native JSON type, and `String[]` is not one either. It passes on the previous code too, since the sets are the same. Planted mutants that drop `Object[]` or add `Integer` fail it. Full suite: 4,738 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures on JDK 21; 4,738, 0 failures on a JDK 8 runtime.
* **TESTING**: `MultiKeyMapTest.testMultiKeyMapKeysThatDifferOnlyInNesting`: a `MultiKeyMap` whose keys differ only in nesting (`a` and `[a]`, `{{a},{b}}` and `{a,b}`) is read back with all its entries. Before java-util 4.113.0's fix to the keys `MultiKeyMap` reports through `entrySet()` (which this library's writer uses), the read map had 3 of the 5.
* **BUILD**: `java-util` 4.112.0 → **4.113.0**, which adds the `Charset`, `java.sql.Time`, `InetAddress`, `LongAdder` and `DoubleAdder` conversions above.
* **BUG FIX**: **A builder `CompactMap` in the default configuration reads back.** `CompactMap.builder().build()` was written as an ordinary object of its generated class, `{"@type":"com.cedarsoftware.util.CompactMap$d1b04c6f91811170",...}`, because it answered `true` to `isDefaultCompactMap()`. That class lives in a class loader of its own, so reading the document failed with `Unknown type (class) ... not defined`, at the top level, in a field and in a collection alike. 4.107.0 and 4.111.0 fail the same way. java-util 4.113.0's `isDefaultCompactMap()` is now `false` for any builder map, so json-io writes it with its configuration (`"config":"java.util.HashMap/CS/S50/id/Unord"`), as it already wrote every other builder map, and reads it back as a builder map of that configuration. `CompactSet`s are unchanged. `CompactMapDefaultBuilderRoundTripTest` (2): a default builder map of 0, 1, 3 and 60 entries at the top level, and one in a `Map` field, an `Object` field and a `List`, each read back equal with the same configuration. Both fail when run against the previous java-util classes, with the reader's `Unknown type` error.
* **BUG FIX**: **A JSON string read into a `byte[]` is Base64, as Jackson writes and reads it: json-io decodes it itself, wherever the `byte[]` sits.** json-io's Jackson-compatible mode (`showTypeInfoNever()`) writes a `byte[]` as a bare Base64 string, and reading it back relied on java-util guessing the string's format. The Base64 of 3 bytes is 4 letters with no padding, which the guess took for text, and Base64 made only of hex digits it took for hex: 61 of 1,025 random arrays of 0–40 bytes came back different, and `"AQID"` in a `byte[]` field gave `[65, 81, 73, 68]` instead of `[1, 2, 3]`. Now a JSON string bound for a `byte[]` — at the root, in a field, a collection, an array of arrays or a `Map` — is read as standard or URL-safe Base64 when it is padded correctly -- exactly as an encoder writes it (java-util's `ByteUtilities.decodeBase64`); a string that is not Base64 gives its own bytes. So `"AF"` and `"OK"`, which have no padding (Jackson rejects them too; the JDK's decoder read them as `{0}` and `{56}`), and `"AF=="`, whose left-over bits no encoder writes, give their text bytes. java-util 4.113.0 no longer guesses (`String` → `byte[]` gives the text's bytes). **Behavior change:** a hex-looking string such as `"CAFEBABE"`, or a short word such as `"DATA"`, in a `byte[]` field is now read as the Base64 it also is, as Jackson reads it, instead of as hex or text; `"00 01 02 FF"` gives its text bytes. Cost: none measurable; the type lookup already ran at these sites.
  * **Tests.** `ByteArrayBase64ReadTest` (4): every length from 0 to 40 bytes must round-trip through `writeBinary` under `showTypeInfoNever()`; `"AQID"` must read as `[1, 2, 3]` in all five positions; URL-safe `"-_8="` must read; four non-Base64 strings must give their bytes; seven strings that are not padded correctly (`"AF"`, `"OK"`, `"AF=="`, ...) must give their bytes at the root, in a field, a `List` and the wrapped form, which fails before java-util's padding rule. Three fail on the previous code. `ByteArrayRoundTripTest`'s foreign-input tests now expect the Base64 reading. Full suite: 4,744 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **BUG FIX**: **json-io's wrapped form of a `byte[]`, `{"@type":"byte[]","value":"<base64>"}`, reads back at the root and in an `Object` field.** `JsonGenerator.writeBinary` writes it, and its Javadoc says it round-trips through `JsonIo.toJava`, but only a `byte[]` field read it: at the root and in an `Object` field it came back `null`. The class factory for primitive arrays, `ArrayFactory`, took a JsonObject without `@items` for an empty array. It now reads a `value` (or `_v`) key through the Converter, as a typed field already did.
  * **Tests.** `ByteArrayWrappedFormReadTest` (2): the wrapped `{1, 2, 3}` must read at the root, through `asClass(null)`, and in an `Object` field; every length from 0 to 20 bytes must read in a `byte[]` field. The first fails on the previous code. Full suite: 4,746 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `BitSet` is read from its binary text, 0s and 1s only.** Any character but `1` counted as 0, so `"10x1"` — typed `{"@type":"BitSet","value":"10x1"}` or a bare string in a `BitSet` field — read as {0, 3} without a word. json-io reads a `BitSet` through java-util's `Converter`, which as of 4.113.0 rejects any other character with an error naming it and its index; json-io reports it as a `JsonIoException`, wherever the `BitSet` sits. (json-io's `BitSetFactory` is registered but never reached: the `Converter` reads the typed form first.)
  * **Tests.** `BitSetReadTest` (3): `"10x1"` must fail, typed and bare, naming index 2; the typed form, `""` and a bare string must read; 200 random `BitSet`s must round-trip in a field and at the root. The first fails against the previous java-util. Full suite: 4,749 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **BUG FIX**: **The older index form of a `BitSet`, `{"@type":"BitSet","value":[1,3,5]}`, reads again; `BitSetFactory` is removed.** Only `BitSetFactory` read that form, and it was never reached — java-util's `Converter` reads the typed form first — so such JSON failed with `Unsupported conversion, source type [Object[]]`. java-util 4.113.0's `Map` → `BitSet` now reads a list of set-bit indices (each a whole number below 2^24), at the root and in a field. **Removed:** `com.cedarsoftware.io.factory.BitSetFactory` and its `classFactory.txt` entry; it was public, but json-io never called it.
  * **Tests.** `BitSetReadTest.theOlderIndexFormReads`: `[1,3,5]` must read at the root and in a field; `[2000000000]` must be refused. Fails against the previous java-util. Full suite: 4,750 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **IMPROVED**: **A field whose value cannot be read names the class and the field.** The `JsonIoException` carried only the conversion's message — `Value 'abc' not parseable as an int value or outside -2147483648 to 2147483647` — so nothing said which of a document's values was wrong. It now reads `com.acme.Holder.count: Value 'abc' not parseable as an int value ...`, and the conversion's exception is its cause. A nested object's error names that object's own class and field. Read speed is unchanged (a 2,000-object read: 2.30 vs 2.28 ms on JDK 21; on JDK 8 within 2%, inside the run-to-run spread).
  * **Tests.** `FieldErrorContextTest` (3): an `int`, a `BitSet` (bare and typed) and a `UUID` field must name `Holder.<field>`, with the `IllegalArgumentException` as the cause; a nested object's error must name its own class once; good values must read. The first two fail on the previous code. Full suite: 4,753 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **BEHAVIOR CHANGE**: **A JSON number too large for the `long` (or `int`, `short`, `byte`) it is read into is an error, not a wrapped value.** With `integerTypeLong()`, `12345678901235678901234567890` read as a `Long` gave 5098844603236747986. java-util 4.113.0 narrows a number only when it fits, from every source, so json-io now throws a `JsonIoException`: `Unable to convert 12345678901235678901234567890 to long: it is outside -9223372036854775808 to 9223372036854775807`. The same holds for a JSON number in an `int`, `short`, `byte` or `char` field, `NaN`/infinity into an integer or date field, and an enum ordinal out of range.
  * **Tests.** `ReturnValueTest.testReturnAsJsonObjectWithBigIntegerJsonPrimitiveForceToLong` now expects the refusal in Maps and Java modes (it pinned the wrap); `JsonParserErrorHandlingTest`'s stale "wraps around" comment is corrected. Full suite: 4,753 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **BUG FIX**: **A Map read into a non-Map type converts its values by their own types.** In `{"duration":{"@type":"BigDecimal","value":"123"}}` read as a `Duration`, the value json-io wrote with its type reached java-util's `Converter` as a Map holding the text `"123"`; the `Converter` reads Maps, not json-io's `@type`. So the `BigDecimal` of seconds became text: 123 s read as 123 ms (java-util 4.113.0 reads integer text as milliseconds), and `{"instant": BigDecimal 123}` read as 123 ms after 1970 instead of 123 s. A Java `Map` holding a `BigDecimal`, written by json-io and read back as a `Duration`, was off by the same factor. json-io now resolves each typed value (`BigDecimal`, `BigInteger`, `UUID` and the other simple types), nested ones first, before converting the Map — at the root, in a field, in Maps mode and in the wrapped form. Read speed is on par (3,000 objects with four typed values each: 4.06 vs 4.05 ms on JDK 21, 4.23 vs 4.20 ms on JDK 8).
  * **Tests.** `NestedTypedValueTest` (4): a typed `BigDecimal` and `BigInteger` must convert by their own types into a `Duration` and an `Instant`, at the root and in a field; a Java `Map` holding a `BigDecimal` or `BigInteger` must round-trip into a `Duration`; untyped numbers, ISO text and decimal text must read as before. Three fail on the previous code. Full suite: 4,757 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **SECURITY**: **A `BigDecimal` with a huge exponent is written with its exponent, not a billion digits.** json-io wrote every `BigDecimal` with `toPlainString()`. `1E+999999999` is 12 characters in, but written out it is a billion digits, so re-writing a `BigDecimal` read from untrusted input exhausted the heap. This applied to the object writer, `JsonGenerator.writeNumber(BigDecimal)` and the TOON writer. They now follow java-util 4.113.0's `ConverterOptions.getMaxPlainDigits()` (default 1,000, through `WriteOptions.getConverterOptions()`):
  * **Unchanged:** a value whose plain form fits — every amount of money, `9.99E14` (`999000000000000`), `1E18` — is written as before.
  * **JSON:** past the limit the exponent form `1E+999999999` is written, a valid JSON number and inside the quoted string json-io uses for `BigDecimal`. It reads back as the same value.
  * **TOON:** its numbers have no exponent, so such a value is written as a quoted string, which reads back into a `BigDecimal`.
  * **Cost:** writing is on par (15,000 `BigDecimal`s: 1.37 vs 1.36 ms on JDK 21, 1.25 vs 1.25 ms on JDK 8).
  * **Tests.** `HugeExponentWriteTest` (2): `1E+999999999` and `-1E-999999999` must be written in under 200 characters by `toJson`, `JsonGenerator` and `toToon`, and read back equal; money, `9.99E14` and `1E18` must stay plain. On the previous code the test JVM ran out of memory. Full suite: 4,759 + 165 + 28 tests, 0 failures, against java-util 4.113.0.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `Pattern` is written with its flags inline and reads back matching what it matched.** `Pattern.compile("abc", CASE_INSENSITIVE)` was written `"abc"` and read back case-sensitive; it is now written `"(?i)abc"`, in Java's own inline syntax, through java-util's `Pattern` → `String` (`LITERAL` as `\Q...\E`; `CANON_EQ` has no inline form). A `Pattern` without flags is written as before, and JSON written before reads as before. Map keys and TOON already went through the `Converter` and follow it.
  * **Whitespace is kept.** With java-util 4.113.0 a JSON string read into a `Pattern` is not trimmed: `" x "` was read back as `"x"`.
  * **Tests.** `PatternFlagsTest` (5): a `CASE_INSENSITIVE` field, a `MULTILINE` array element, a `DOTALL` map value and a `CASE_INSENSITIVE` map key must read back matching what they matched, with type info and under `showTypeInfoNever()`; a root `Pattern` keeps its flags, and `LITERAL` keeps the dot literal; TOON keeps the flags; a `Pattern` without flags is written as before and old JSON reads with flags 0; whitespace is kept. The two `PatternWriter` tests fail on the old writer.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `CompactSet` keeps its backing map through JSON.** Its config string had no place for it, so a set built on `ConcurrentHashMap` was read back on `HashMap`, and one on `ConcurrentSkipListMap` on `TreeMap`: no longer thread-safe, without a word. The map type is now written as a fourth part of the config, `"CS/S2/Unord/java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap"`, when it is not the ordering's default (`HashMap`, `TreeMap` for sorted and reverse, `LinkedHashMap` for insertion order). Uses java-util 4.113.0's `CompactSet.getConfig()`, which now reports it.
  * **Unchanged for ordinary sets:** a set on its default map is written exactly as before, three parts, so older json-io reads it. JSON with the fourth part is refused by older json-io ("Invalid CompactSet config format") rather than read on the wrong map.
  * **Vetted as CompactMap's is:** the class name comes from the JSON, so it is looked up through `ClassUtilities` (the security block list) and must be a `Map` from a package `CompactSet` allows; anything else is a `JsonIoException`, not a silent fall-back to the default.
  * **Tests.** `CompactSetMapTypeTest` (5): `ConcurrentHashMap` (case-sensitive and not) and `ConcurrentSkipListMap` (sorted) are kept, as is the order; each ordering's default map is written as before and reads back the same; the old three-part form reads; `String`, `ProcessBuilder`, an unknown class, a non-`Map` and a fifth part are refused; a set inside a map keeps its backing map. The round trips fail on the old writer.
* **BUG FIX**: **json-io works as a Java module.** On the module path it could not be used at all, including the released 4.112.0. Its module descriptor exported two packages that no longer exist (`com.cedarsoftware.io.prettyprint`, removed in May 2026, and `com.cedarsoftware.io.reflect.injectors`), so the JVM refused the module ("Package ... not found in module"); with those removed, it then failed on first use ("Resource not found: config/classFactory.txt"), because its `config/` folder is a package of the module that no class loader can read from outside it.
  * **Exports:** both packages are removed from the module descriptor and from the OSGi `Export-Package` (they were empty there).
  * **Config:** `MetaUtils` reads a config file as before (the context class loader first, so a copy there still wins) and, only if that finds nothing, from json-io's own module. Classpath use is unchanged.
* **BUILD**: **`module-info.class` moves to `META-INF/versions/9` of a multi-release jar**, as in java-util 4.113.0: it was a Java 9 class file at the root of a JDK 8 jar, which JDK 8-era tools reading every class can fail on. JDK 8 ignores `META-INF/versions`; JDK 9+ reads the same module. The OSGi manifest is unchanged apart from `Multi-Release: true` and the two exports above.
  * **Verified on the built jars:** a module requiring `com.cedarsoftware.io` writes and reads JSON on JDK 11, 17, 21 and 25; classpath use on JDK 8 and 21.
  * **Tests.** New integration tests (`maven-failsafe-plugin`, after `package`, against the packaged jar, on the classpath): `JarLayoutIT` (5): every class outside `META-INF/versions` is a JDK 8 class file; `module-info` is under `versions/9` of a `Multi-Release` jar; on JDK 9+ the module descriptor can be read, and json-io runs as a named module (loaded with java-util into a module layer of their own, with the context class loader kept off the class path); the OSGi headers are kept. On the old jar four of the five fail.
* **BUG FIX**: **A double or float is written as the same text on every JDK: the decimal `Double.toString` / `Float.toString` print from JDK 19 on.** JDK 8 through 18 print many doubles differently -- `1.0E23` as `9.999999999999999E22`, `2.0E23` as `1.9999999999999998E23`, `8.41E21` as `8.409999999999999E21`, most whole doubles beyond 2^53 with a longer tail (`4.8796343143351112E18` for `4.879634314335111E18`) -- and 19 floats longer (`9.9E-44` as `1.0E-43`). Both read back as the same number, but the same object wrote different JSON on JDK 8 and JDK 21, and TOON, which spells numbers out in full, wrote `99999999999999990000000` for `1e23` on JDK 8.
  * **Every path:** primitive, boxed and `Object` fields, `List`/`Map` elements, `double[]` and `float[]`, `JsonGenerator.writeNumber(double/float)`, TOON, and `JsonTokenizer.getText()` for a number it read. They use java-util 4.113.0's `DoubleDecimal` / `FloatDecimal` (internal): the JDK 19 algorithm on every JDK, checked against JDK 21 on about 7 billion doubles and every float. The generator lays the digits out in a reused buffer, with no `String` made.
  * **Faster:** JDK 8 writes doubles 34-40% faster (200 objects with 8 double fields 163 → 98 us, 2,000 boxed doubles 117 → 72 us, `double[2000]` 139 → 92 us); JDK 21 2,000 boxed doubles 87 → 75 us (-13%), 200 objects -3%, `double[]` and TOON on par.
  * **Tests.** `FloatingPointTextTest` (5): every path above writes the JDK 19 text for doubles and floats JDK 8 prints differently. On the old code all five fail on JDK 8; on JDK 19+ the old code passes, since the JDK printed the same text there.

* **BUG FIX**: **TOON writes a large whole double or float as the decimal it prints.** At 2^63 it wrote `9223372036854775807` -- `Long.MAX_VALUE`, a different number: the long cast it went through clamps there. Above 2^53 (2^24 for a float) it wrote the binary value (2^60 as `1152921504606846976`, `1.5e10f` as `15000000512`), which is neither what the TOON reference encoder writes (JavaScript's `String(2**60)` is `1152921504606847000`) nor what json-io's JSON writes (`1.152921504606847E18`). It now writes the decimal the number prints, in full: `9223372036854776000`, `1152921504606847000`, `15000000000`. Below 2^53 (2^24) every whole number is exact and is written as before.
  * **Tests.** `ToonWholeNumberTest` (3): large whole doubles and floats, alone, in fields and in primitive arrays, and their round trip; exact ones unchanged. Two fail on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **json-io runs in an OSGi framework.** It used `com.cedarsoftware.util.internal` (`VectorizedArrays` since 4.103.0, now also `DoubleDecimal` and `FloatDecimal`) without importing it, so in a framework it failed with `NoClassDefFoundError`. It imports that package now, with the attribute `friend=json-io` that java-util 4.113.0 exports it under for json-io alone. With java-util 4.113.0's OSGi fixes (the bundle resolves; class lookup sees json-io's own classes and the JDK's), json-io writes and reads JSON and TOON in Apache Felix 7.0.5, on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
  * **Tests.** `JarLayoutIT.everyJavaUtilPackageItUsesIsImported` (the packaged jar): every java-util package referenced by json-io's classes is imported, the internal one with the attribute. It fails on the old manifest.
* **BUG FIX**: **A field declared with its class's type variables reads as the type its object binds them to.** For `Box<T extends Number> { T value; List<T> list; }` held in a field `Box<Integer> box`, `value` is an `Integer` and `list` a `List<Integer>`; for `Gen<T> { List<T> items; Map<String, T> byName; }` held as `Gen<Item>`, the items are `Item`s; `OC<String>.Inner`'s `T value` is a `String`; `G<Integer>`'s `T[] arr` an `Integer[]`; a subclass that binds `T` (`IntBox extends Box<Integer>`) or passes it on (`Mid<U> extends Base<List<U>>`) works too. The object's parameterized type was replaced by its raw class while it was instantiated (`Resolver.resolveTargetType`, `JsonValue.setTarget`), so `T` was left unresolved -- `Unresolved type: java.util.List<T>`, also for json-io's own output, which then could not be read back -- or became its bound (a JSON `"5"` refused for `Number`), or stayed `Object` (a `Long` stored where a `String` was declared, a `ClassCastException` later; `java.lang.Long` as the whole message for a `T[]`). A generic class read raw, with no argument anywhere, reads its containers' elements as they come instead of failing.
  * **Speed:** reading 200 ordinary objects is unchanged (JDK 21 and JDK 8); only fields declared with type variables resolve them, once per object.
  * **Tests.** `GenericFieldTypeTest` (8): each shape above, a raw generic class, and the round trip of json-io's own output with and without type info. All fail on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **A Map or Collection subclass whose type arguments are not Map's or Collection's, in order, reads with the right key, value and element types.** json-io took a parameterized container's first argument as the key and the second as the value (and the first as a collection's element): for `StringMap<V> extends HashMap<String, V>` held in a field `StringMap<Integer>`, the key `"k"` was parsed as an int and failed; `Reversed<V, K> extends LinkedHashMap<K, V>` swapped them; `MultiMap<K, V> extends HashMap<K, List<V>>` lost its `List`; `Tagged<X> extends ArrayList<String>` converted its Strings to `X`. Keys, values and elements are now matched to Map's and Collection's own, through the hierarchy (java-util's `TypeUtilities.inferKeyType` and `inferElementType`), when reading fields, nested containers, TOON key/value lists and map values, and for the key type the writer records. The JDK's own containers take their arguments in that order already and are read as before, so reads cost the same.
  * **Tests.** `ContainerSubclassTypeTest` (6): each shape above, object values of a map subclass, and the round trip with and without type info. All fail on the old code.
* **CHANGE**: **A blank string read into an enum or a `UUID` is null**, following java-util 4.113.0's Converter, which converts blank text to null for every type without a zero, as for the dates (it threw). `EnumTests` and `UUIDTest` pinned the exceptions and now expect null.
* **BUG FIX**: **A field declared `Number`, or a type variable bounded by `Number`, takes number text.** `{"value":"5"}` read into a raw `Box<T extends Number>` (or a `Number` field) failed with `Unsupported conversion, source type [String (5)] target type 'Number'`. java-util 4.113.0's Converter now reads number text as a `Number`: whole text a `Long` (a `BigInteger` beyond a long), other number text a `BigDecimal`.
  * **Tests.** `NumberFieldTest` (2): `Number` fields from whole, large and decimal text and a JSON number; a raw generic class whose `T extends Number` takes text, alone and in a list.
* **TESTING**: **The packaged jar is now run as a bundle inside an OSGi framework, beside java-util's.** `OsgiBundleIT` (7, failsafe, after `package`) starts Apache Felix 7.0.5, installs and starts java-util's jar and json-io's, and uses json-io from inside: both bundles resolve and start; doubles are written and read back (`[1.0E23,0.1,-2.5]`); TOON writes 2^60 as `1152921504606847000`; the tokenizer gives `9.999999999999999E22` the text `1.0E23`; a `TimeZone` reads back as `sun.util.calendar.ZoneInfo`, a JDK internal json-io's bundle does not import, by its alias and by its full name; a `CaseInsensitiveMap` named in the JSON reads back as the bundle's class, not the copy on the launcher's class path that the context class loader sees; and a `CompactMap` from the builder round-trips as its generated class.
  * **The framework is test scope and kept off the unit tests' class path** (`classpathDependencyExcludes`), so the unit tests run as before.
  * **Planted defects caught:** json-io's manifest without the `com.cedarsoftware.util.internal` import (5 of 7 fail, `NoClassDefFoundError`), java-util without its fallback to the JDK's loaders (the `TimeZone` test), and java-util using the context class loader whatever it can see (4 fail). It passes on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
* **BUG FIX**: **`AtomicReferenceArray`, `AtomicMarkableReference` and `AtomicStampedReference` read back on every JDK, in JSON and TOON.** Their state is in fields java.util.concurrent closes from JDK 17 on: json-io wrote them there with that state as null (`{"array":null}`, `{"pair":null}`) and could not read them back. Each now has a `JsonClassWriter` and a `ClassFactory`. They are written through their public methods, in the JSON json-io (and Gson) wrote by reflection on JDK 8 and 11 — `{"array":[...]}`, `{"pair":{"reference":...,"mark":...}}` or `"stamp"` — the same in every type-info setting, so JSON written there reads back. (With `showTypeInfoAlways()` the pair no longer names java.util.concurrent's internal `Pair` class; no reader needs it.) Measured: json-io now round-trips them on JDK 8, 11, 17, 21 and 25; Jackson 2.22 cannot write `AtomicReferenceArray` and neither Jackson version reads the other two back; Gson cannot write them on JDK 17+.
  * **Object graphs:** the reference trace (JSON and TOON) visits their contents, so an object shared through them stays shared and a cycle through them reads back. A reference to an object not yet built when the holder is read is set into it once that object is built.
  * **Types:** elements and references are read as the field's type argument (`AtomicReferenceArray<Item>`) where the JSON does not name their type — on JDK 8 too, where they came back as maps.
  * **API:** `WriterContext.writeObjectField(name, value, declaredType)` writes a field as json-io writes its own: `@type` only where that declared type needs one. `Resolver.resolveHeldValue(holder, value, type, later)` lets a `ClassFactory` that fills its object itself read a value, a `@ref` to an object not yet built included.
  * **Speed:** writing a 500-object graph with cycle support (fresh JVMs, median of 6): JDK 21 336.4 → 338.5 µs, JDK 8 345.0 → 345.0 µs; without cycle support, and TOON, unchanged within noise. The trace looks at these types only for an object with one field, as each of them has.
  * **Tests.** `AtomicHolderRoundTripTest` (10): the JSON json-io wrote on JDK 8, in every type-info setting; round trips at the root, in a list and as typed fields with and without `@type`; a forward reference; shared objects and cycles; TOON, with and without cycle support; JSON written before (json-io's on JDK 8 with every type shown, Gson's). All 10 fail on JDK 21 on the old code; 5 on JDK 8, where elements typed by generics came back as maps and `showTypeInfoAlways()` named the internal `Pair` class. Six planted defects each fail it: no trace of the contents (JSON, TOON), no setting of a forward reference, untyped values not given the field's type, the declared-type write always typing, TOON not recognizing them.
* **DOCS**: **The JDK-types comparison is updated.** In the user guide's *JDK Types Compared with Jackson* json-io keeps 100 of the 104 values on JDK 21 (was 97) as on JDK 8; the three atomic holders and `Pattern` move from "what neither keeps" to what json-io keeps and Jackson loses (a `Pattern`'s flags are written inline since this release; Jackson writes `"a+b"` and loses them); the TOON types table lists the holders. The README's json-io / Jackson / Gson chart gains a **JDK types** section, its type list the atomic holders; `JsonIo`'s class Javadoc gains a short *JDK Types Compared with Jackson* section.
* **FEATURE**: **`JsonClassWriter.toData(T)`: a custom writer gives its object as plain data, and json-io uses that data wherever it works on contents rather than JSON text.** TOON writes it, and the reference pass (JSON and TOON), which finds the objects written more than once and the cycles, visits it. A `Map` of name to value is written as the object's fields, with the object's own type and id; any other value is written in the object's place, as an `Optional` is written as its content; the default, `JsonClassWriter.NO_DATA`, keeps the field-by-field handling. JSON output does not change: it is written by `write`. Whether a class's writer gives data is decided once per class and carried with the class's cached write plans, so other objects pay nothing.
  * **TOON, fixed:** `AtomicIntegerArray` and `AtomicLongArray` were written `array: null` on JDK 17+ and read back empty on every JDK; a `Semaphore(3)` came back with 0 permits, a `CountDownLatch(2)` with a count of 0, a fair `ReentrantLock` or `ReentrantReadWriteLock` unfair; an `Optional` in a list or an array lost its value on JDK 17+ (`- value: null`) and came back as a map elsewhere. Each type's writer now gives the values its factory reads, and all round-trip.
  * **Custom writers, fixed:** an object a writer writes but its class's fields do not reach (computed, transient) was invisible to the reference pass: if it was also elsewhere in the graph it was written twice and read back as two objects, and a cycle through it recursed until the stack overflowed. A writer that gives its data keeps both, in JSON and in TOON.
  * **Simpler core:** JsonWriter and ToonWriter no longer know specific JDK types. TOON's special cases for `Optional`, `OptionalInt`, `OptionalLong` and `OptionalDouble` (four write paths and the reference pass) and for the atomic holders, and JsonWriter's for both in its reference pass, are gone; those types' writers give their data. TOON's per-class category cache moves to the `WriteOptions`, where the writers are.
  * **Speed** (a 500-object graph, fresh JVMs, medians of 5-7): on JDK 17 and 21, JSON writing with cycle support is 2-3% faster (367.8 → 361.1 µs and 351.2 → 339.6 µs), JSON writing without it and reading are on par or 1-3% faster, and TOON writing is on par on JDK 17 (254.3 → 253.5 µs) and faster on JDK 21 (286.4 → 267.3 µs). On JDK 8 JSON is on par; TOON writing is about 10% slower (259 → 286 µs).
  * **Tests.** `CustomWriterDataTest` (6): the JDK types above through TOON, at the root and as fields; `Optional`s in lists through TOON; a user's writer whose object is shared and whose objects form a cycle, in JSON, and its data through TOON. All 6 fail before json-io consults `toData`. Twelve planted defects (no trace of the data in JSON or TOON, TOON never using it, data in a list not written inline, data not written in place at the root or for a field or a list element, `toData` never seen as overridden, and the reader changes below) each fail the related tests.
* **BUG FIX**: **An `Optional` inside a collection, an array or a map's values reads back as an `Optional` of the declared type**, as in a field. Written as its content — Jackson's form, json-io's TOON, and its JSON without type info (`standardJson()`) — it was put in the list, array or map bare: a `List<Optional<String>>` or a `Map<String, Optional<String>>` held Strings (a `ClassCastException` at the first `get()`), a `Map<String, OptionalInt>` held numbers, and an `Optional<Item>[]` threw `ArrayStoreException`. In json-io's object form (with type info) an object shared through one lost its identity. Null reads as `Optional.empty()`; a `@ref` to an object not built yet is wrapped once it is; `{value: ...}`, the form TOON wrote by reflection on JDK 8 and 11, still reads.
  * **Tests.** `OptionalElementsTest` (5): lists, sets, arrays and map values of `Optional` and `OptionalInt`, holding a string, an object, a list or nothing, from JSON with and without type info and from TOON; forward references, through a map value too; TOON written by an earlier version. 4 fail on the old code, on JDK 21 and JDK 8.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON with type info (`showTypeInfoMinimal()`, `showTypeInfoMinimalPlus()`, `showTypeInfoAlways()`) writes `$type` where the reader needs it, and what it writes reads back.** TOON writes no type info unless asked. Asked, it wrote `$type` on every object, list and map whatever its declaration — a list of 50 DTOs in a field went from 2,107 tokens to 3,876 (+84%, cl100k) — and several types still did not read back: a `CompactMap` was named by its generated class (`CompactMap$8d10f…`, which no reader knows), an `EnumSet` as `RegularEnumSet` with no enum class (unreadable at the root), a `MultiKeyMap` without its configuration ("requires a config string"); a `BigDecimal`, `Integer`, enum, `char[]` or `Optional` in an `Object` field came back as a `Double`, a `Long`, a `String`, null, or its content; a `Map<Integer, …>` at the root came back with String keys.
  * **Minimal is minimal.** `$type` only where the declared type does not determine the class the reader builds: a subclass in a field, list or map; anything in an `Object` slot or in a list nothing declares; an exception's cause; and the root (unless `omitRootTypeInfo()`). A field, list, set or map holding its declared class, or the class the reader builds for the declared interface (`ArrayList` for `List`, `LinkedHashMap` for each map of a `List<Map<String, Object>>`), carries none; nor does the content of an `Optional<BigDecimal>` or an `OptionalInt`. The 50 DTOs in a field now cost 2,116 tokens (the root's type line); as a root list, whose elements nothing declares, 2,608 (+24%); a polymorphic graph 532 (+80%, as before, and it now reads back whole). Minimal-plus also leaves out what JSON's minimal-plus does (an int in an `Object` slot reads back as a long); always types every value but a String, Long, Double or Boolean.
  * **Written as JSON writes them.** A scalar that needs its type is `{$type, value}`, a `char[]` `{$type, $items}`; an `EnumSet` names its enum; a map whose keys the reader must be told is written as `$keys` and `$items`; a `CompactMap`, `CompactSet` or `MultiKeyMap` names its base class and writes its configuration through its writer's new `toData`, so a case-insensitive map reads back case-insensitive, and one that is shared stays shared. TOON consults a map's or list's `toData` only where it writes the map's or list's type.
  * **Reader.** A list at the TOON root read as a raw `List` resolves its elements that name their type (they stayed `JsonObject`s); an object typed `{$type: BigDecimal, value: 1.5}` in an `Optional`'s place is its content, not `Optional`'s `{present, value}` form; `CompactSet`'s and `MultiKeyMap`'s factories accept the List TOON reads where JSON has an array.
  * **Unchanged:** TOON without type info and JSON write byte for byte what they wrote before (20 graphs and 7 option sets, 140 cases each). Speed (a 500-object graph, fresh JVMs, medians of 7): TOON writing 269.4 → 258.3 µs on JDK 17 and 276.5 → 252.3 µs on JDK 21, JSON writing 236.9 → 239.0 µs and 241.1 → 241.7 µs — on par (on JDK 8, TOON 291.4 → 289.4 µs).
  * **Tests.** `ToonMinimalTypeTest` (18): DTO graphs written as without types plus the root's line; polymorphic fields, lists, arrays, maps and exception causes; scalars, enums, `char[]` and `Optional`s in `Object` slots; `CompactMap`, `CompactSet`, `MultiKeyMap` and `EnumSet` at the root and in fields; keys nothing declares; roots of every kind, with and without their type; tables and inline lists that a typed value must not enter; empty containers; shared objects and cycles; `Optional`, `OptionalInt` and the concurrency types' data — in minimal, minimal-plus and always. 15 fail on the old code (the other 3 guard the default and the reference and element-type bookkeeping). 28 planted defects each fail the related tests. `ToonTypeMetadataTest`'s `EnumSet` and `Integer`-key cases, which documented the loss, now assert the round trip.
* **BUG FIX**: **A `MultiKeyMap` written in TOON without type info reads back.** TOON then writes its entries with no configuration: a plain map when its keys are Strings, a list of key/value entries when a key has several parts. The plain map could not be read at all ("MultiKeyMap requires a config string"), at the root or in a field, and the entry list read as a `MultiKeyMap` at the root came back as a `LinkedHashMap`. `MultiKeyMapFactory` now makes a default `MultiKeyMap` of a plain map, which json-io fills as it fills any map. More widely, a TOON map read as a map class the caller names (`ConcurrentHashMap`, `IdentityHashMap`, `MultiKeyMap`, ...) is that class: where its keys are objects, or its values had to be converted, it was a `LinkedHashMap` in its place, for any concrete class but `HashMap` and `TreeMap`. With type info on (`showTypeInfoMinimal()`), a `MultiKeyMap`'s entries are plain maps, as in JSON, not each typed `LinkedHashMap`. `MultiKeyMapTest` and `ToonReaderTest`: fail on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **An empty `Optional` at the root reads back empty.** TOON, and JSON without type info, write it as null, as they write an empty `Optional` field; read as an `Optional` (or `OptionalInt`, `OptionalLong`, `OptionalDouble`, or an `Optional<T>` through a `TypeHolder`) it came back null. Read as anything else, null is still null. `OptionalToonTest`: fails on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **A stack trace in TOON reads back.** TOON reads a list inside an object as a `List` where JSON's reader gives an array, and `ThrowableFactory` cast it to `Object[]` (`ClassCastException`). json-io does not write stack traces, but TOON from elsewhere can carry one. `ExceptionSerializeTest`: fails on the old code. json-io's other factories already take either. The `ClassFactory` Javadoc and the user guide now say how a factory reads a list from JSON and from TOON: through the `Resolver`'s read methods, which take both (`CustomWriterDataTest` pins it), or by accepting a `Collection` as well as an array.
* **BUG FIX**: **A map or collection type json-io cannot instantiate -- an interface, an abstract class -- is built as the Converter builds it, wherever it is asked for.** `ConcurrentMap`, `ConcurrentNavigableMap` and `AbstractMap`, and `BlockingQueue`, `BlockingDeque`, `TransferQueue`, `AbstractCollection`, `AbstractList`, `AbstractSet`, `AbstractQueue` and `AbstractSequentialList`, failed as a field's type and at the root ("Cannot instantiate interface"). A `ConcurrentMap` is now a `ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe` (JSON carries null values, which a `ConcurrentHashMap` refuses with a bare `NullPointerException`), a `ConcurrentNavigableMap` a `ConcurrentNavigableMapNullSafe`, a `BlockingQueue` an unbounded `LinkedBlockingQueue` (java-util 4.113.0), an `AbstractList` an `ArrayList`. An interface of the caller's own that none of them is fails saying so, and what to do. And where TOON's reader builds a container itself it builds the class asked for: a TOON list at the root read as a `LinkedList`, `Vector`, `Stack` or `CopyOnWriteArrayList` was an `ArrayList`, and a map with object keys read as an interface a `LinkedHashMap`. `ContainerTypeAskedForTest` (5): fails on the old code.
* **BUG FIX**: **The type asked for at the root reaches what is read: `asType(new TypeHolder<List<Employee>>(){})`, the README's example, gives `Employee`s from plain JSON.** A root list's, set's or map's type arguments were applied only where the JSON named its elements' types (json-io's own output). From plain JSON -- Jackson's, a REST service's, json-io's without type info or with minimal-plus -- a `List<Employee>`'s elements came back as maps (a `ClassCastException` at the first `get(0).getName()`), a `List<Integer>`'s or a `Map<String, Integer>`'s numbers as Longs, a `Map<Integer, Employee>`'s keys as Longs, and the user guide's `Map<String, List<Department<Employee>>>` with `Object[]` values. So since at least 4.70.0. They are now read as a field declared with that type is: elements built as the type argument, numbers converted, nested arguments applied, a map's list values lists; from TOON too (lists of numbers, maps of numbers or of lists). The JSON's own type (an `@type` ArrayList) still decides the class, with the arguments asked for. Reads with `asClass`, with no class, and in Maps mode are unchanged: a differential over 308 reads differs only in `asType` reads that now keep their types. `RootTypeArgumentsTest` (4): fails on the old code; 5 planted defects each fail it.
* **BUG FIX**: **A map's entries are its contents, never its constructor's arguments.** A map class without a custom factory was built by matching its JSON entries to constructor parameters -- by name, or, for a class compiled without parameter names, by position -- so `{"capacity":7,"b":2}` read as a class with a `(capacity)` constructor was built with capacity 7. A map class is now built with no arguments, as one read for an unknown type always was, and filled with its entries; only one that cannot be built so (no constructor takes json-io's defaults) is built from them, as before (`LRUCache`, which needs a capacity, gets its own factory: see below). `@IoCreator` is unchanged. Tests: `MapEntriesAreNotConstructorArgumentsTest` (2); against the previous code the first fails (`(7)`), and without the fallback the second.
* **BUG FIX**: **TOON with type info writes a list's or map's configuration where its class goes without saying.** A container whose custom writer gives it as data (`MultiKeyMap`, `CompactMap`, `CompactSet`) was written as that data only when it also needed `$type`; in a field of its own class TOON wrote its entries alone, so a case-insensitive `MultiKeyMap` read back case-sensitive. It is now written as its data there too, without `$type`, as JSON writes it. TOON without type info is unchanged (a map is its entries). Cost: writing a 50-DTO graph with `showTypeInfoMinimal()` takes 25.0 us instead of 24.5 (JDK 21, fresh JVMs), a cached per-class lookup for each container. Test: `ToonMinimalTypeTest.aContainersConfigurationIsWrittenWhereItsClassGoesWithoutSaying`; against the previous code it fails.
* **BUG FIX**: **java-util's `LRUCache` and `TTLCache` read back, and still work as caches.** No release could read either one back: every one since 4.56.0 threw ("Capacity must be at least 1", "TTL must be at least 1 millisecond"); 4.28.0 - 4.52.0 read an `LRUCache` back empty, without an error; and plain JSON read as one took its first value for the capacity or the TTL.
  * **`LRUCache`** is written as before, as the map of its entries, and `LRUCacheFactory` reads it back with them, in a cache with room for each (its capacity is not written), at least 16.
  * **`TTLCache`** is written as its settings and no entries -- `"config":"ttlMillis/maxSize/cleanupIntervalMillis"` (`TTLCacheWriter`) -- as Guava and Caffeine serialize their caches: a reader cannot know how old an entry is, so one read back could outlive its TTL. `TTLCacheFactory` builds it back empty, configured as it was, and a cache that lands in JSON by accident costs a few bytes, not its contents. One written as its entries (by an earlier release, by TOON without type info, or plain JSON) keeps them, without expiry, holding at most as many (at least 16). Needs java-util 4.113.0's `TTLCache` getters.
  * **Tests.** `CacheRoundTripTest` (5): JSON and TOON, typed and not, at the root, in fields, in an undeclared slot, the old entries-only form and plain JSON, each cache used after it is read. Without the factories and writer all 5 fail; without the writer 2; without the capacity floor 2.
* **BUG FIX**: **An application's own `config/aliases.txt` decides the names json-io writes; json-io still reads the names every copy defines.** An application can put its own copy ahead of json-io's on the class path -- n-cube does, to keep writing the names its older clients read (`java.util.ArrayList`, `int`, `string`). That copy took json-io's place for reading too, so the names json-io writes were unknown (`Unknown type (class) 'ArrayList' not defined`): n-cube could not read the pull requests it stored in the months it wrote json-io's names. `ReadOptionsBuilder` now also reads the aliases of the other copies on the class path, json-io's own among them, under the first copy's, which win where they disagree; writing still uses the first copy alone. Reading more names opens nothing new: a full class name is always readable, and the security checks are unchanged. `removePermanentAliasTypeNamesMatching` removes a name whichever copy defines it. With only json-io's copy nothing changes, and `ReadOptionsBuilder`'s first use takes as long as before (87 ms, JDK 21, fresh JVMs). Test: `AliasesOfEveryCopyAreReadTest`, json-io loaded in its own class loader behind an application's copy, as on such an application's class path; against the previous code it fails with that error.
* **DOCS**: **`ReadOptionsBuilder.removePermanentAliasTypeNamesMatching` is documented as what it does: it matches alias names** (`"ArrayList"`, `"*Map"`), not class names, and stops them being read -- its Javadoc said class names and "during output". The per-builder `removeAliasTypeNameMatching` and the write-side methods match class names, which their docs say. The read-options user guide: the same correction, the right method names (the per-builder read method is `removeAliasTypeNameMatching`; the permanent one's signature line named `WriteOptionsBuilder`), and what a custom `config/aliases.txt` does since 4.113.0 (it decides the names written; every copy's names are read). `CompactFormatTest` asserts that the legacy wrapper `{"@type":"ArrayList",...}` keeps a `List<ZonedDateTime>` field's element type, where a stale comment said it did not (it has since at least 4.111.0).
* **DOCS**: **User guide: "Which Class You Get for an Interface"** -- the class json-io builds for each interface or abstract container type asked for (`Map` a `LinkedHashMap`, `ConcurrentMap` java-util's `ConcurrentHashMapNullSafe`, `Queue` a `LinkedList`, `BlockingQueue` an unbounded `LinkedBlockingQueue`, ...), and that a root `TypeHolder`'s type arguments now apply as a field's do. `ContainerTypeAskedForTest.theClassBuiltForEachInterfaceIsTheDocumentedOne` pins the table (21 types, root and field, JSON and TOON).
